Introduction Lovely Ladies! What is the art of being a woman? Perhaps it has never before occurred to you that there is such an art. Ah, but there is, decidedly! To be beautiful, to be intelligent, to be gracious, kindly, tolerant, interesting, witty-in short, to be any and all of the things that it is required a woman should be is an art. And, like all arts, it must be consciously studied, practised, perfected. 

This book has come to life, illuminating the path, both theoretically and practically, pointing the intelligent way for all women to study and practise the art of being a woman. 

Who am I, to paint a picture of woman and to presume to hold the portrait up to the careful scrutiny of my kind? 

But, you see, it is not only I who do this. Many things and many peoples, many ages and many countries have given their brush strokes to this canvas. I merely presented the canvas, like a mendicant, from door to door, from book to book, from country to country, with a plea for expert brush strokes in the painting of my portrait of woman as 

XV

Introduction she should be. Nor was I denied, for all Nature worships loveliness in womankind. And from the tiniest butterfly who gladly gives its iridescent wings to be fashioned into a brooch for Miladi, contributing just one iota of momentary and outward beauty, to the tall, symmetrical buildings that rear their terraced stories of stone and plaster to house her loveliness, all have willingly contributed their brush strokes to the canvas that portrays woman. 

I want you to feel that this book is a living thing, that it pictures life, not just one idealistic person's idea of the things to do and not to do, to know and not to know, to be and not to be. Life and its antithesis, Death, have both stalked across these pages. Years have trod on years since 

Lovely Ladies began to unfold. Sorrows, heartaches, romances, thrills, tears, hopes, fears! Each page of this book has its quota. Could you only read between the lines!

Could you, as I do in turning the pages, see this or that picture, this or that famous person, this or that beautiful woman tread in memory the printed form. . . . Here are some leaves from the sketchbook of Lovely Ladies. 

“Fee-e-e-e-sh”—but instead of fish 'twas white lilacs that he brought, this Marquis de G, exiled from Russia and trying to piece together the scattered parts of his existence in China. On a leave of absence in Paris, marching into the sanctum sanctorum of “Lovely 

Ladies" with a sheaf of French, Spanish, and Chinese quotations and allegories to be put in the manuscript. The next day a note: “Here, dear one, the lilacs-but myself you must excuse. I am exhausted from my literary efforts." And he was. xvi

Introduction 

"Teaching women the art of being a woman-they certainly need it!" barked the not-at-all-barking professor of psychology of Cornell 

University. 

"You will show women how to be lovely-what a wonderful mission!" exclaimed Isadora Duncan at Nice the day before she said, "Je vais á la gloire!" and died. "Dah-ray-p-r-u-u-n-e-e-s !” 

No need to call, we could smell 'em! (You see, it was the sixth kettle of prunes we had put on the stove, determined not to burn these, having burned the other five, and we did want prunes because they are so-0-0-0 good for one.) 

And then page after page of "Lovely Ladies" spun on, and the prunes went the way of all things that come into too close and prolonged contact with fire. This time, in sheer ennui, the bottom fell out of the pan when Annette tried to restore it to an unburned state of usefulness. 

Prince Y., bending over to kiss my hand and saying, with all of the grace that only a Russian prince can bend to flattery because he construes flattery as truth: "The art of being a woman-you-can well write it." White and blue and gold. . . . . I alone seemed to know that her exquisite loveliness was not long for this world. Yet that inexorable need to continue the fitting of words on words in another part of the house rather than sit by her and lighten the brooding air with the jeweled butterflies of laughter and conversation. xvii

Introduction 

And then, weeks later, the telegram telling me that at last her suffering eyes were closed. And "Lovely Ladies" marched on page by page, perhaps strengthened by grief and more alive in the memory that one lovely lady had left the knowing of her loveliness to the world and gone "where all things lovely go, and there remain."

And Lars, self-exiled from America because of a dramatic intrigue quixotic in its implications. Faced with the immediate need of writing this book, for which I had been gathering material all of my life, and very slowly recovering from "meningite cérébrale," what could I have done without his flying fingers tirelessly pounding at the typewriter keys until he swore that “it is not always tight shoes that produce corns !” 

Ah, the much more to writing than writing! Had Lars not typed away from eight o'clock in the morning till midnight, and often after, and starting again the next morning at eight, "Lovely Ladies” might still be wandering in the borderland of unwritten books. 

Ah, Annette, Annette, femme de ménage extraordinaire, could your failing eyes and rigid mind but have been able to understand what it was that wore out your "petite Madame" for those long, long hoursthose many weeks! You would have approved, serving as you have for over fifty years, the lovely ladies of the French aristocracy. But I forgave you as it enraged me—your tender solicitude, your worried expression, your head shakings and grumblings, your cajoling, and the tempting trays you forced me to eat at the time when, spurred by the whip to expression, the frail human body is unable to desire or digest food. 

May you never know with what solemnity Lars and I wrapped that fatal bread pudding, with what serious faces we begged you for more and added it to the parcel, with what stealth Lars carried the parcel toward home, only to drop it at a quiet corner! xviii

Introduction 

An accusingly white canvas, an untouched paint box, and a sudden restless, imperative need to work with hands, while plumbing the depths of knowledge and understanding to bring forth elusive material that could depend upon no research work, no study, no notes. Painting with one hand, leaning on a cane with the other and dictating "Lovely 

Ladies !" 

The badness of the first portrait and the furious swirl of paints on the pallette followed by a vicious sweep of the paint brush that left a wide swab of color across the face of the portrait-and resulted in an almost unbelievably beautiful and mysterious bringing to life of the eyes, the only part that remained untouched. 

And the second portrait that helped, oh! how much, by the mere effort of mental concentration, to make the mind produce more lucidly. The second portrait that disappointed many, thrilled many, and finally hung in the Knoedler Galleries and was invited to hang in the Salon d'Autumne, an almost unheard-of honor for any but an already acknowledged artist.

Sava, son of a country that admits of no divorce, Sava of the beautiful and haunted eyes, Sava who has lived with a love that amounts to desperation and madness, for a woman married in his country to another man-loved her for fifteen years, which is a sum of years for one who is not an old man. Sava, who welcomed the news that he was to join the airplane testing force at the flying field. In my little circle of friends there is not one of us who does not breathe a prayer for Sava's safety each night-as he tests the planes and sees in the danger a surcease! 

And then the day the news comes that Sava has crashed. Call it what you will, for though Lars was so broken up he had to go home, I stayed and found my surcease in doing pages on pages of "Lovely Ladies." 

Painting, music, sculpture will not do it-for they permit the heart and mind to wander afar, seeking their own bruising and relief. But writing can ease pain, casting it behind to furnish a somber background xix

Introduction that perhaps makes the words seem more brilliant as they drop slowly from the pen.

Turning the pages of the section on Beauty, Benco of the solemn mien smiles up at me, with one of the stray smiles we had to control that winter afternoon before his so-serious “professeurs."

Money couldn't do it, because of that he had plenty. Science couldn't do it, evidently, because he himself was a student at a Medical College, and not one of the eminent professors of this, that, or the other thing had been able to give him a successful remedy for the very annoying and humiliating skin eruptions that persisted in appearing on his face. 

But we did it! and the procedure and results are described here in the 

Beauty section. What I was not able to picture was the formal letter received from the dean of professors, asking if he and some of his colleagues might call on me—to discuss the methods I had used for curing this very bad complexion!

And then when they did come they all but insisted that I was holding out some mysterious formula on them, being unable to believe that the absurdly simple thing that we did so was all that was necessary, and all that we really did.

The pictures are endless, each with its laugh, its tear, its smothered pain, or its brilliant flash of insight. Genia, the lovely Russian artist, tracking to earth the quotation attributed to Nichiren and finally finding it embroidered on an old tapestry in the Louvre, hung in such a position that she had to sit on the stone floor and then all but stand on her head to copy it, to the audible delight of the fortunate artists who had enough sous to buy the privilege of painting for a day among the deathless masters. XX

Introduction 

And Paulette's baby! Three of us adopting it simultaneously, and all but coming to blows over how many names fifteen pounds of large-eyed and shrieking humanity could conveniently carry through life. 

And so many others. That is why this book lives. Nothing in it is far-fetched, nothing untrue, nothing that other women have not done, from the principles to the actual things. 

And before I close I must, even though I may seem to wear my heart on my sleeve, tell you something you should know, because it will help you to believe and to understand that this book is not just one of theoretical ideas falling short of the actualities of life. 

Once, in writing a newspaper article about Motherhood I mentioned, as I do in the chapter on Motherhood in this book, that science had made pregnancy safe and practically painless, and that there are other pains yearly and hourly endured by the human body that are far, far worse than childbirth.

Just one woman was stupid enough to write an indignant letter to the editor in which she said, among many other things, that it was quite evident that I was a silly, flighty, and sterile woman. 

That doesn't happen to be true. I have had a child, and know all of the joys and discomforts of its prenatal months, as well as the suffering that a woman must endure in giving birth. But, I have also known much worse physical pain, that lasted much longer and had no such compensation at the end. xxi

Introduction 

And my dear, outraged madame, I have known something I hope you may never know, something which, once known, gives a woman a tolerance and wisdom never reached through any other sorrow. 

I have known the still, white face of my baby, piercingly beautiful in its serenity, terrifying in its rigidity, mutely imploring understanding and forgiveness for having taken with it to the other world my hopes, my dreams. 

And that who knows?-may have been one of the reasons why this book took life. xxii

BOOK I 

BEING discussions of all of the abstract qualities that a truly lovely and intelligent lady must have; of the social arts a lovely lady must know; and of that which a lovely lady must be.

Chapter I MODERN AND LOVELY WOMAN 

Jare 

MODERN and lovely woman-she who has developed her intellect in the face of tremendous odds; who has intelligently and consciously developed beauty, both character beauty and physical beauty; who has learned to fraternize with man as a woman, without in any way feeling inferior to him, or antagonizing him; who will rear her children in the paths of intelligence, loveliness, and beauty; who has demanded and been given the right to be an individual!

Lovely Ladies 

Now what is "modern"? Surely Hypatia, the Greek mathematician and philosopher, was a modern woman of her day. Surely your great-grandmother was a modern woman of her day. "Modern" means "of the present or recent times."

Therefore the pictures of "modern women" that succeeding generations hold up must of necessity be limited by the ideas of each age.

LET us turn back a few pages of history 

Looking Back and see woman as she has been, woman treated by man for centuries as a child, a plaything, a comfort-giving animal. Only in occasional instances of history has she been permitted importance in the social scheme, and then only as an individual, never as a class. 

This was not because women were not capable of taking part in important affairs, but because the sociological conditions of the times prevented them from being mentally equipped to take such a part. 

Man, knowing that a woman could not be his physical equal because of the years she would have to spend in the bearing and rearing of her children and in the fulfilling of the biological functions of her sex, fashioned a place for her-one permitting her solely to minister to his comfort, in the pretense that he was protecting and sheltering her with his superior physical strength.

And for centuries he tried to keep her in this place. Nor was it a difficult or unhappy one. That was why woman did not sooner realize that she could be a woman in all

Modern and Lovely Woman of its physical implications, and an intellect as well, at no sacrifice whatsoever of her biological duties to the race, and of her feminine prerogatives of grace, charm, and loveliness.

But it took man longer to recognize that this was true. 

The birth struggles of feminism were dreadful, and they were made so largely by man's lack of understanding. Not realizing that woman could continue to contribute to his physical comfort, fulfill her duty of reproduction, and at the same time develop her intellect to a degree that would make her a far more interesting companion to him as well as a more efficient mother to his children, he tried to keep from her the possibility of intellectual development, putting in her path obstacles which were almost insurmountable. 

First Efforts 

IN RETALIATION a few women whom circumstances did not force to depend upon men or a man, in an effort to convince him (as well as herself) that she could develop an intellect and that she did have a place in the world other than the home, unfortunately overdid the gesture. This resulted in the so-called "modern woman" of recent years, who, though of unquestionable ability, succeeded only in making men detest her and all that she implied. Yet this type of woman was not a picture of what intelligent and lovely women are striving to be.

ONE of the most important and necessary feminine qualities or attributes has always been and will always be

Lovely Ladies beauty, giving rise as it does to many cultural qualities. In her first efforts toward freedom woman was too much inclined to forget or to minimize the impor- 

Beauty tance of these things, feeling that all of her feminine prerogatives and duties were petty. 

But to-day's modern woman no longer makes this mistake. The modern woman of the early part of this century and the latter quarter of the last century was a joke, and no one could treat her as anything else. But men respect and appreciate, and other women admire and envy, the present lovely modern woman, who recognizes and develops the worthwhile feminine arts and qualities as well as those character qualities that only men have hitherto developed. 

Intelligent women realize that beauty is not a gift of the gods, to be hoped for in one's self and one's children, but the result of a conscious determination. To have and to hold beauty, to create it and inspire its creation in others, to make it an accepted part of life for all classes and not just a luxury for some, is woman's duty. Nor is that platitudinous, even though it may sound so upon first reading. The consciousness of beauty and the appreciation of its actual need is more highly developed in women than in men, and always has been. Intelligent women cannot but realize that they owe to life and civilization every possible furthering of beauty, every possible expression of it in themselves and in their lives, and that upon them and their teaching depend their children's appreciation and expression of it.

Modern and Lovely Woman 

Secrets PERSONAL beauty has never been an accident of nature. The first woman who outshone her sisters in beauty was the woman who discovered that by doing certain things and not doing others she could acquire beauty of hair, freshness of eyes, pleasing contours of the body, and keep them long after they were lost to other women of her age. Her secrets, jealously guarded, were passed on to her daughters, who in turn told them to theirs. And then heredity had its say, automatically passing on to the children physical symmetry and specific figural refinements that were perhaps painfully acquired and held by previous generations. 

The conscious cultivation of beauty gives rise to an assurance of this beauty, and this assurance expresses itself in that effect which we call loveliness. Loveliness is one of the feminine attributes based more upon spiritual beauty than actual physical beauty. Loveliness is feminine strength, and it is woman's compensation for her physical inferiority to man.

To be beautiful and lovely is unquestionably a woman's spiritual duty to life. And every woman can be lovely!

First Feminine 

Instincts 

WOMAN, sitting alone possibly in her smokefilled cave (and by the way, who but a woman hewed out the first window and hung it with a hide!), perhaps with a couple of youngsters at her knee, while her man was out roaming the hills and valleys (exercising the delicious masculine prerogative of freedom ostentatiously in quest of food, but in reality wrong

Lovely Ladies in quest of adventure,) realized that man was and would forever be her physical superior.

In rebellion against the unfairness of this was born the first instinct of loveliness. If nature prevented her from holding her place with man in every physical activity, there were still ways to divert this masculine strength and use it for herself.

Qualities of 

Loveliness 

LITTLE by little, woman disciplined her tastes, schooled and developed spiritual qualities and the outward manifestations of them and taught them to her daughters.

The way was long and discouraging. Often from one generation to another the carefully disciplined and developed tendencies would be lost. Yet unbounding patience prevailed, and lovely ladies began to gleam intermittently through the pages of history, like beacon lights of inspiration for man and woman alike.

Modern 

Loveliness

FOR Woman to overlook the inestimable value of loveliness is for her tamely to admit her inferiority. And the perfection of this purely feminine quality is the only avenue to equality with men.

It is of the utmost importance for modern women to realize this! Our intelligence alone is going to win us our rightful place in the scheme of things, and it does not take a great deal of intelligence to understand why it is impossible for us to be physically equal to men. Instead of

Modern and Lovely Woman striving for the impossible and thereby making ourselves ridiculous-in our own eyes as well as in the eyes of menby improving what it is possible to improve we can achieve a much greater and more real and permanent victory. 

LOVELINESS is an acquired virtue. No 

Not a Gift woman is born with it, although some may inherit tendencies which make it easier for them than for others to develop the qualities that produce loveliness. 

Also, some of us are more fortunate than others in that we have been raised in an environment conducive to the development of these qualities. 

But environment and heredity notwithstanding, loveliness is an effect that can be developed by any woman. 

It must be done consciously and with a certain amount of effort, for it takes real effort to discipline one's temper, appetites, moods, and instinctive selfishness; and real effort to learn how to keep the body groomed to the degree society recognizes as evidence of our cultured civilization. But the effort is only in acquiring the habits of these things. Once learned, they become almost instinctive, certainly subconscious and effortless. 

Loveliness is purely feminine, for under no circumstances can a man be lovely, no matter how admirable or handsome he might be. And for a woman either to overlook the need for loveliness or to depreciate it is the grossest stupidity. 

Her loveliness is her strength!

Chapter II CHARM AND GRACIOUSNESS 

Not Necessary to Charm

CHARM is an aura which emanates from a gracious personality. It is an effect based upon the spiritual, or character, qualities which we will later discuss. 

And as an effect, and not a definable quality, it is difficult indeed to explain. 

One could so simply say that, were one to acquire and develop certain abstract and actual qualities, one would be charming. But that would not be very much help! 

FIRST of all, let us eliminate some things that a woman has not-beauty, perhaps. 

Decidedly one does not have to be beautiful to be charming. And then, too, she may not be especially brilliant—although all true loveliness and charm must have intelligence for its background. Perhaps also she has not an outstanding ΙΟ

Charm and Graciousness personality-she could move quietly through her little social circle and never be especially noticed unless one came into direct contact with her, within the radius of her charm. But then one never forgets her.

A Necessity 

VERY definitely she is well bred. Not superficially encased in a knowledge of the correct thing to do and say, but truly well bred in her instinctive kindness and courtesy toward the people with whom she comes in contact.

Understand, though, that one does not have to have been raised in an atmosphere of luxury and to have known the privileges accorded to well-to-do people in order to be well bred. Good breeding consists merely in a kindly consideration for others and its expression, which is good manners. There is proof enough of this about you! 

Strangely enough, although in order to be charming one must be well bred, many well-bred persons are not especially charming, and many interesting personalities do not have charm. The answer is, then, that merely being well bred is not sufficient.

Wedding courteous manners with kindness and consideration that is not ostentatious will bring you nearer to charm.

And then we must add to courteous manners and kindness a sense of humor. But as you will be warned in the chapter on Humor, this must be a kindly humor, ever on the alert not to hurt.

II

Lovely Ladies 

AND there must be sincerity. Charm is

No Affectation something that cannot be affected. It must be an effect of an inner prompting, or it is odious. 

Is there anything quite so infuriating as the, what I like to call, "professionally charming manner"? 

No shallow person can be truly charming. The Goddess of Sophistication may paint you with layers and layers of good breeding, good manners, courtesy, and poise, and still you will not be charming if kindness and understanding are not in your heart.

Poise 

WITH the assurance of your charm will come poise. A charming person cannot be other than poised, because the exercising of kindness and consideration toward others develops assurance, which is poise. 

Do you realize that not only charm but civilization rests upon kindness and consideration toward the other person? 

As your intelligence makes you appreciate the fact that life at its best is none too easy for anyone, you will realize that it is not worth while to be anything but considerate to your fellow dreamers. All of our so-called social qualities arise from the knowledge that life is as difficult for others as it is for us, and a desire to make the little part of the other lives we affect as pleasant as possible. And Graciousness

CONSIDERATION for others is expressed in graciousness. It may be said of charm in relation to graciousness that charm is the aura that emanates from a gracious personality, and 12

Charm and Graciousness graciousness is the shedding of the light of that aura upon others.

Charm, then, is not a quality, but the effect produced by a number of qualities, and graciousness is the application of charm. You might be fairly charming, but making no particular effort to employ this charm, you would not be gracious.

Defensive Shellac 

BEFORE we go any further, I must stop and point out to you an antithesis of charm that so many modern women unconsciously acquire. For lack of a better word we had best call it "hardness," this defensive shellac with which otherwise intelligent women seek to protect their finer sensibilities from hurt!

Hardness in a woman refutes the very end to which she thinks this hardness is leading her and repels the things she wishes to attract. It is all very well to say that you do not want any help from anyone in anything, that you pay as you go, that you take what you get and give what you get, and all of the other unthinking things women are wont to say. But this is not strictly true. No one can go through life, as closely packed as it is with people, without realizing the need of assisting and being assisted by others. Appearing Selfish

HARDNESS SO often appears to be selfishness, even though many women who are totally unselfish, and in every way worthy, labor under the delusion that to show an opposite face to the 13

Lovely Ladies world is to enhance their admirable qualities. Intelligence shows this to be a false idea. What can one hope to gain by hiding the richness of one's soul beneath a cloak of unpleasantness?

Tells a Story 

OBVIOUS hardness in a woman is an indication of age and defeat. Only unsuccessful women are hard-women who have had to wrest every luxury, every privilege from life, and who have not done it very well. Any woman who has had to struggle for the privilege of living should recognize the need of inspiring all possible assistance in others.

Adolescent Ignorance 

AND now for the modern and muchdiscussed younger generation, accused of being "hard-boiled" and selfish. Are they?

And the few who really are-how have they become that way without the assistance and example of their parents?

The fact is that the younger generation is neither hard, cruel, nor selfish. It is simply shellacked, with a shellac that is obviously removable. It is a combination of ignorance and thoughtlessness resulting from an admirable if misdirected desire on the part of the parents to save the child all of the character-forming "hard knocks" that they, the parents, have had, without offering any balancing substitute for them. And the child, who has never had any spiritual trials—or, more important, any desires-toward the fulfillment of which he had to expend energy or make 14

Charm and Graciousness sacrifices, cannot possibly appreciate the unhappiness or sufferings of others. The result seems to be hardness. 

Yet hardness and self-interest in the younger generation is superficial and, while regrettable, of no especial importance. It is but an indication of the attitude of the parents. 

Therefore, hardness in a woman who is old enough to think and feel for herself is even less excusable, since this attitude is bound to be reflected in her children. Guard

Against Hardness

NATURALLY Women, in their struggle for recognition as individuals, have had to indulge in spiritual and even physical labors that hardened them. But spiritual battles are not necessarily the same as physical ones. 

Life is not a prize fight, and character strength is not muscular.

Disappointments, hurts suffered at the hands of less enlightened people, do not prove that one should retaliate. 

If you adopt a protective hardness against the petty hurts and disadvantages occasionally suffered at the hands of the ignorant, this same protection will shut out from your life all the beauty and consideration and companionship it could be receiving from those who have found that they cannot afford to be hard.

Charm is most certainly something that can be developed. Guard against its antithesis, hardness, and do not try to develop charm without first understanding and developing the qualities from which it springs, which are discussed separately in other chapters. 15

Lovely Ladies 

Charm is based upon a sincere appreciation and understanding of your place in life relative to your fellow men. 

And from this sincere appreciation spring courtesy and graciousness, kindliness and understanding, and all of the other definite qualities of civilization which are massed to produce an effect-charm! 16

Chapter III SPORTSMANSHIP 

LET us see what this quality of sportsmanship amounts to, and how important it is.

I will begin with the admission that it is instinctive for women to be poor sports, and mitigate this statement by another: It is not instinctive for men to be good sports either!

Yet the majority of Anglo-Saxon men are good sports. 

Sportsmanship is a virtue of civilization. An infant has not the faintest conception of sportsmanship. In fact, it is not at all easy for a child to understand fairness. Its every instinct is to fend for itself, keep its own toys and books, shriek when it is hungry, and shriek when it is sleepy.

Sportsmanship, an altruistic quality, is certainly as yet not instinctive.

Primitive Infants AN INFANT but retreads man's biological path, covering in the short space of a few years the road which it has taken man so many centuries to cover. Thus are we able to understand our primitive forefathers. And it is not difficult to see that primitive man or woman could not possibly have indulged in the refinements of good sportsmanship and have sur、 17

Lovely Ladies vived. Sportsmanship was a later development, when there came a lessening of the struggle for existence. 

WOMAN admittedly was not a good sport. 

Women and 

Furthermore, she was neither expected to

Sportsmanship be one nor condemned for not being one.

Sportsmanship simply could not exist along with the deceits and trickeries in which she had to indulge for her very existence; trickeries condoned and excused because they were practised by women, when the same things if done by a man would have merited the severest punishment. One must admit, in all fairness, that, conditions being what they were, women in the past were really not lacking in the quality known as sportsmanship. One cannot lack what one has no reason or need to have. 

But here's the important thing. These conditions do not prevail to-day. Women now know that they are capable of living without the assistance of any man, and they should realize that, since the sociological necessity for feminine trickeries has passed, there is no longer any excuse for them not to be good sports.

Sportsmanship 

Defined

SPORTSMANSHIP has neither sex nor social distinction. To indulge in it one does not have to be wealthy, neither does one have to have any extraordinary amount of intelligence. And in particular one does not have to have physical strength. 

In fact, the most outstanding examples of sportsmanship have been given by the physically incapacitated. 18

Sportsmanship 

Most Anglo-Saxons know what it means to "be a good sport." We are more or less born and raised that way, and we have it pummeled into us by our schoolfellows. We have it daily brought to our attention in our national games and sports, in business, in marriage. 

Probably we all have different ideas of sportsmanship, based upon our intelligence and appreciation. Yet, sportsmanship must have certain fundamental characteristics. A complete egotist cannot be a good sport. Selfishness and good sportsmanship cannot abide in the same person, for sportsmanship is for, and in relation to, "the other guy." Being a good sport consists of giving the other fellow the opportunity to avail himself of some of the advantages which chance, birth, or education may have accorded you. 

BEING a woman, what kind of a good sport

The Female Good Sport are you? Heaven preserve us from the ultramodern female who is so very fond of telling the world in general and in particular what a good sport she is! The fact is that she is not a good sport, because her very attitude is offensive to everyone. As offensive as the physical culturist who goes through life slapping people on the back and poking them in the stomach to "show them how soft they are." 

Women must learn the meaning of sportsmanship, but they must be good sports with charm.

Be a good sport to yourself. To be a good sport at the cost of your charm is not sporting to yourself. Play the 19

Lovely Ladies game, indeed, always admitting the other person's place in it even at your own expense. But do all of this quietly and unostentatiously, thereby being a charming good sport, and making an art of this quality. Thus you will bring womanliness to sportsmanship.

Intelligent Sportsmanship ANOTHER thing-an intelligent woman knows that sportsmanship does not mean being a fool. Too often the expression, “Oh, come on, be a good sport," is used as an excuse for something that is either foolhardy or downright cheap. You do this thing against your better judgment, merely because you are called upon to be a "good sport." And this is sheer abuse of the quality of sportsmanship. A lovely 20

Sportsmanship lady who is a good sport will never be prevailed upon in the name of sportsmanship to do anything that is loud or vulgar, or anything that will make her look ridiculous.

The Good 

Loser

SUPPOSE, then, that something goes wrong and you lose a cherished possession or you do not achieve something you have been aiming for, and it hurts you like the very mischief. Here's where your perspective which includes the other fellow comes in, or your good sportsmanship. The good sportsman keeps his chagrin to himself and does not impose it on those around him because, regardless of his feelings, it is not sportsmanlike to do so. The "poor loser," or poor sport, does.

Our troubles and our sorrows are very real and important to us, but why is it that we so often lose our perspective to the extent of imposing them on our friends, and more particularly on those who love us? We forget that they too have their sorrows and troubles which are as important to them as ours are to us. 

I have in mind particularly the times when we are ill and away from our loved ones. We cable them that we are ill or about to have an operation, or we have someone cable them that we have been in an accident, or even dying. And they may be seven seas and several continents away from us and cannot do a single thing about it. We know quite well that it is impossible for them to come to us. 

How can we so forget their wordless hours and days and possibly weeks of worry and sorrow which we might have 21

Lovely Ladies spared them! The more sporting thing to do is to cable after it is all over, telling them, if you must, what you have been through, but adding that it is all right now. How few people think of this! 

You Need

Sportsmanship in Business 

AND if you are a business woman, are you being a good sport with yourself and with your boss? I put you first, because if you are not putting all of yourself into your business for the hours that you are in it, you yourself are the loser. 

If one of these days someone should ask you if you considered that you were being a good sport when you were sitting at your desk writing a personal letter knowing that there was other work that should be done, or filing something away carelessly, knowing that someone other than yourself would have to look for it, you would be astonished. Perhaps the idea had never occurred to you before.

Be a good sport with your boss during the hours in which you are in his employ. If you cannot give him efficiency, an interest in his business, and, above all, loyalty, you are being a poor sport to keep that job away from some other woman who might be doing these things for him. If he is not the kind of a man who inspires these things in you, then do them for your own sake. Or else work for someone who does so inspire you. 

ARE you a good sport in your married life? Such an important question is this that I have devoted a whole chapter to it elsewhere. As long as a woman is married, 22

Sportsmanship Sportsmanship and Marriage accepts the name, the support, and the interests of a man, she must be a good sport with him, herself, and the institution of marriage. She must give him a fair exchange for his support and his name and the prestige that the world accords her as his wife. And I mean what you know is a fair exchange, and not what you would like to think is a fair exchange.

It is a good thought to carry, that good sportsmanship after all is a way of making your own passage through life more pleasant.

For Your Own 

Sake THIS fact leads one to the realization that sportsmanship is not the purely altruistic quality that it seems. Probably even the first man to indulge in it shrewdly realized that he did so to his advantage.

For sportsmanship unites and solidifies man against his common foes-even if these foes be simply another group of men. And it produces an ineffable glow of self-satisfaction that warms one's entire personality and cannot be produced by anything else on earth. "Well, things didn't go right that time, but I was a good sport, so it's all right." How often in your life have you said this to yourself and actually meant it? If, on the contrary, in a moment of weakness, you have gained some sort of victory at the expense of your sportsmanship, has the experience not remained one of those barely buried dead spots in your heart, ever ready to crop up in a blue moment and look at you accusingly? 23

Chapter IV 

A Useless

Instinct 

WOMEN FOR WOMEN 

"WHAT an extraordinary title! 

I wonder what she means?" 

I can fancy you saying just that. Yes, it is an extraordinary title, and I wanted it to be. 

Have you ever heard of "cattiness"? It is the most detestable of feminine anachronisms, something which is retarding the development of women and has done so for centuries. 

The trait is a useless, petty, and vicious holdover from times that no longer exist, and it is high time that we got after it in energetic style to relegate it to oblivion.

WHAT is this subconscious, instinctive antagonism with its roots deep in the centuries that every woman has toward another woman? It is nothing in the world but the "urge toward self-preservation." 24

Women for Women 

For hundreds of years women have had to depend upon men for their food and shelter-for their whole existence, in fact. The primitive savage, perched upon his sunny rock, speared two fish for himself and one for his woman. 

While the savage maid found it highly convenient to attach herself early and securely to some promising hunter, the woman of yesteryear found it equally advisable to capture some male who might be designated in the community as a "good provider." 

Nothing so menaced the security of the savage maid, or her presumably civilized sister, as-another woman. 

The "other women" epitomized all of the ghastly nuances of uncertainty, hunger, privation, and loneliness. To all save the woman of exceptional spirit these vague fears, and the actualities they represented, were enough to cause everything else—every other instinct and impulseto be thrown to the winds. 

The Natural Growth of Woman's Antagonism 

HENCE there grew up, among women, not merely an antagonism directed against some other particular woman, but a general antagonism among women for all women. It takes little effort to correlate this thought with the fact that, as a general thing, a woman will be more hostile to a beautiful woman than she will be to a plain woman, for the more beautiful woman is merely more of a menace to her than the plainer one. A woman's cattiness toward attractive women is based upon primitive fear. 25

Lovely Ladies 

Didn't you ever feel a wee and unworthy urge which prompted you to be antagonistic to Mrs. Smithers across the street? If you did you need not hesitate to confess it to yourself, but also you should go further than that and analyze the urge. You will find it rooted deep in primitive fear-fear that she may attract your husband, that she may achieve a more personable appearance than you do and so attract your friends, that her personality may surpass yours, and that thus she may become a more interesting person.

While fear is the fundamental basis of the reaction, and while it gives rise to a general antagonism toward other women, it also gives rise to other emotions—jealousy, for instance. In another chapter in this book I shall discuss these other impulses, and tell how to deal with them and understand them. In this chapter I am interested only in showing that the antagonistic feeling of all women for all other women, although it may have served a useful purpose in the Paleozoic age, no longer is of use, nor is it worthy of our enlightened intelligence. 

We Have Progressed

I Do not wish to impose upon this intelligence by emphasizing that women to-day no longer are dependent upon men.

That fact is self-evident. It is a singular fact, however, that although one woman need no longer fear for her actual safety if another woman "steals" her man, the old primitive antagonism still is with us under the guise of “cattiness." And stranger than that, many so-called modern 26

Women for Women women don't seem to realize that this instinctive and perverse feminine trait is weakening the cause of femininity to a marked degree and definitely impeding the progress of women toward complete emancipation. 

Comparing 

Men and 

Women 

MEN naturally have escaped this blight. 

Unless there is some unusually strong mitigating circumstance, the average man will defend another man whenever it is possible. Even when one man knows in his heart that another is guilty of a wrong action, it is only with extreme reluctance that the innocent male will concur in condemning his brother.

This situation is almost exactly reversed in the dealings between women. Women are notorious for their unsympathetic attitudes toward one another. 

Women Must Learn From 

Men 

WOMEN must learn to fraternize as men do. I don't mean that they must organize and generally participate in the shenanigans which currently pass as "good fellowship," but women must learn to bridle their adverse opinions of other women. 

Above all, women must learn not to criticize other women to men. For the moment you start to assure Mr.

Brown that Miss Green is something less than bright, Mr. 

Brown will jump to the conclusion that you have an "ax to grind." Not only is this course poor sportsmanship; it is misguided psychology. Man naturally tends to protect 27

Lovely Ladies the weak, and when you picture your sister as "weak" the male immediately experiences a subconscious urge to give aid, and hence is more inclined than ever to overlook any possible faults which he may detect in the woman of whom you are speaking.

The Sportsmanship Angle SPORTSMANSHIP, which is the way we sum up mental honesty, decency, and honor in one word, forbids this cattiness. on the part of women. A person of honor will not think of using any special advantage which his or her adversary cannot share in equal measure. Yet you are doing just this when you try to pick a flaw in the character, morals, physical or mental equipment of another woman, and hold these weaknesses up for view by others. Instead you should try to understand her shortcomings. If you succeed you will not criticize her, and instead of trying to weaken her in the eyes of others by exhibiting the shortcomings in an unfavorable light, you will be more inclined to cover them, or better still to explain and perhaps transmute them into virtues. 

Please, right here, let me explain one point which you may just possibly fail to understand. I have spoken of the injustice of picking flaws in "another" woman. When I say "another" woman I do not mean any particular other woman. I mean all women. The days of individual thinking are long since passed. A really modern woman and the truly lovely lady must be internationally minded. 

Don't forget that, and when I give a specific example 28

Women for Women be sure that I mean it to apply broadly and to all people. 

The Strong Chain 

"IN UNION there is strength" is not an aphorism that applies to men alone. Nor does it apply only to governments or to whole peoples. It applies universally; and when all peoples of all races are united in the business of living, then life will hold far fewer terrors and a far greater measure of beauty.

Women have waited a long time to recognize this cardinal point of civilization-this excellent ethic of coöperation, kindness, and honorable dealings. But they must recognize it, and recognizing it they must proceed to weld themselves, the feminine sex, into a strong link in the civilized chain. 

The inspiring structures which women, through their outstanding achievements, are rearing as monuments to their abilities are excellent things. They are solemn and grand things. But these achievements, however startling and beautiful, will crumble at the first searing breath of adversity unless they are builded upon firm foundations, and one of the firmest foundations possible is the determination of all women to be for all other women and against no woman! 29

Chapter V SWEETNESS AND KINDNESS 

EVERY real woman must be sweet, and she must express her sweetness in kindness. 

The real sweetness that is the essence distilled from a lovely soul is not the cloying, sticky, kittenish sweetness that all too inadequately conceals its owner's claws, but the deep, real loveliness that shines from the depths of a lovely soul, and often produces an effect of actual physical beauty. 

True sweetness is "as rare as a day in June"-and I mean "rare" in the sense that it was used by the author of this quotation. Rare meaning "choice, priceless, and seldom found" applies to sweetness on all three counts.

A naturally sweet disposition is often an inherited quality or the product of an environment where one is con- 30

Sweetness and Kindness stantly surrounded by others who are so pricelessly blessed.

Yet, even though it may be latent, the germ of sweetness abides in each of us.

Sweetness is founded upon many things. Often it is based upon a philosophical outlook which is sometimes called understanding—at other times tolerance. For one with a sweet personality may, at times, be rather easily hurt, but realizing and having the intelligence to analyze this hurt, that person never retaliates. And also, upon such a one, the cares and irritations of life seem not to weigh too heavily, despite the fact that usually she is not the type of moron who is always happy because she cannot feel sorrow.

Life being what it is, it appears to me that the sooner one cultivates a philosophical attitude tinct with humor, the sooner will one be equipped to go through it with a minimum of discomfort to one's self and to others. 

Kindness Is the Expression of Sweetness 

SWEETNESS is related to kindness in the same way that graciousness is related to charm. One is the thing itself; i.e., sweetness and charm are the qualities. The other is the expression of the thing; i.e., kindness is the expression of sweetness, and graciousness is the expression of charm.

Naturally we presume that this expression must be directed toward someone or something. One might be alone on a desert island, and although having a very sweet personality, be unable to express this toward anything or anyone in kindness. 31

Lovely Ladies Startling Beauty Is Not Essential to Sweetness 

IT SHOULD be plain to everyone that unusual beauty is not necessary to sweetness.

We all certainly may achieve a meritorious degree of the latter, and there is no more excuse for a person to lack this evidence of worth than there is for a modern woman to deny herself the advantages of beauty, though she may not be a beauty. 

WHY should one strive for sweetness? 

That is a fair question.

Why? 

To begin with, the question is put a little badly. Conscientious striving for sweetness should not be necessary. 

This follows because sweetness really is produced by other virtues which the lovely lady and the modern woman truly must have.

When one understands, one does not feel anger-one feels only sympathy and pity. When one is intelligent, one understands. Ergo, an intelligent and understanding person must of necessity be sweet. Directing

Sweetness NEVERTHELESS, a true and objective sweet ness is not always possible to everyone without conscious effort. As I have said, the germs of sweetness frequently may be dormant in a very fine person. But these excellent impulses also may be obscured by unworthy, momentary, and petty impulses which may seem ever so much more attractive at the moment. Therefore we must apply a little logic to brightening and polishing up our sweetness, even though we know that we have loads and loads of the very best impulses. 32

Sweetness and Kindness 

If you stop to think for a moment you will realize the fairness of suggestibility. When you are willing to listen to the opinions of others, and also to give these opinions due consideration along with your own, you are known as a "suggestible" person. Suggestibility is a mark of intelligence. To be suggestible does not mean that you must accept any and every other opinion that is offered you. 

It simply means that you must listen to these other opinions and give them careful thought. Unless you find some fundamental weakness in them it means also that you must admit their strength, and accept them if they are better than your own.

As suggestibility is a mark of intelligence, it also is an indication of sweetness. The truly sweet personality has no room for brashness and brusqueness, but meets others with due consideration, is amenable to reason, is always ready to arbitrate when there is a difference, and, invoking the quality of sportsmanship, is always ready to concede a bit more to an adversary than is necessary. 

The Inner Sweetness 

EXAMINE your conscience, and while you are doing it, examine your face in the mirror. Do your features tell their tale of sweetness—not vapid, unintelligent, stolid sweetness, but the deep sweetness that comes from the soul? Or do they tell a story of acerbity, even the least bit of which is sufficient to rob you of charm? If they do, then why do they? 

And why should you not make an effort, from this moment, to change matters? 33

Lovely Ladies 

"Now that remark certainly was mean!" Or "That really was a sour thought." Little sentences like these you can say to yourself until it becomes quite subconscious to do so. For our subconscious is the most obedient servant in the world, and the most easily trained. We have but to implant an idea in it and it will work on that idea of its own volition. Coué demonstrated this to the world a few years ago. You simply tell your subconscious that you are going to have sweetness of disposition, and it will be on the lookout to warn you against all of the things which might stand in the way. 

Age and

Sweetness 

THEN another point. Why do so many old people seem to think that, just because they have been living a little longer than others, this passing of time gives them the right to impose their tantrums and tempers and rudeness upon others? 

Look to your liver, my dear-look to your liver and try to revive your sense of humor! Realize that other people also have livers and that you are not being a good sport. 

Being unpleasant is permissible only if you can purchase an island in some far-off corner of the earth and never see another person again as long as you live. 

Don't Be a

Pollyanna 

UNDERSTAND, I am not giving you the idea that you should be the reincarnation of Pollyanna. Heaven forbid! A woman with a sweet disposition is not such a perfect thing that you have an irresistible desire to slap her or stick her with a 34

Sweetness and Kindness pin just to see what she will do. She is a lovable and often whimsical person who can indulge in a perfectly justifiable "peeve" or even a spurt or two of temper now and then, but who never allows either of these to turn into deep, mean "grouch." 

Speaking of grouches reminds me of one prize grouch that, unlike any others, repeats itself and is nothing more or less than a very ugly and selfish habit. Grouch! 

THE morning grouch is one mood that

The Morning actually has its roots in the physical. But it is nurtured and strengthened by indulgence. Yet, if you would be truthful with yourself, the mornings that you overcome this unpleasantness merge into more interesting and amusing days than do the other mornings. Or perhaps you don't know this because you have never tried it?

Conclusion 

SWEETNESS, and its expression, kindness, are very necessary allies in your battle with life. With them to aid you, you can fashion things to suit yourself. And every woman should have these qualities regardless of her station in life, her good breeding or lack of it, her so-called opportunities or lack of them, her beauty or even her degree of intelligence. Sweetness is one quality which any woman may have by merely making up her mind to it. 

Serenity, sweetness, tolerance-they will almost of themselves make you a lovely lady! 35

Chapter VI COURTESY AND CONSIDERATION 

THE art of being a woman nat urally includes learning, if you have not already been taught, the meaning of true courtesy. It also includes the art of expressing courtesy toward others in the form of charming manners.

AN AMERICAN sculptor in Paris once said that courtesy was inaugurated in the time of the 

Louis' merely for self-preservation-the tempers and the nerves of the people being so dreadful that a code of ethics had to be drawn up and conformed to by the whole population to save the race from being The Origin of destroyed by itself! The idea presents an Courtesy amusing cartoon, and certainly not only for the French people. 

Courtesy is absolutely indispensable to civilization.

It is a quality which we employ toward others. Acquire 36

Courtesy and Consideration a point of view that makes you consider the feelings of others with whom you come in contact and you will be a truly courteous and well-bred person, no matter where you go. True courtesy is a mark of nobility of character.

Travel-Proof 

Manners

THE very fact that accepted good manners differ in every country in the world should have made thinking people realize the fallacy of courtesy by rote. Take, for example, the manner in which a courteous Oriental guest conveys to his host his appreciation of the excellence of the food put before him. Failure to indulge in this gesture of appreciation was, until very recently, considered extremely discourteous. 

Yet the same gesture of appreciation is, in most other countries in the world, a mark of extreme vulgarity. 

A courteous and well-bred woman is stamped indelibly wherever she may be. And because of her instinctive courtesy, any errors made against the accepted customs of the locality are condoned by other well-bred people. 

It is only a very stupid person indeed who will take offense at anything said or done by a stranger to the manners of his or her country.

On the other hand, a woman may be letter perfect in so-called good manners, meaning the certain civilities that are indigenous to her locality, and not be truly courteous by nature. Move that woman out of her environment, and lacking the qualities from which true courtesy springs, she will invariably offend and be in- 37

Lovely Ladies dexed for the spiritually poverty-stricken person she really is. Painless

Character Forming 

It is not necessary to study good manners and courtesy after the fashion that one studies grammar and arithmetic. It is only necessary to keep the other person's feelings in mind. 

If children were taught at home that good manners and courtesy are only necessities conceived for the happiness and comfort of those around them, instead of being taught these things as becoming a "little lady" or a "little gentleman," the whole world would shortly be a much more pleasant place to live in. "I don't wan-n-n-n-n-t to be a little gentleman!" shrieks Sonny, throwing himself flat upon the floor. "You're a bad boy, then," says the unthinking mother despairingly, and perhaps, also in desperation, she proceeds to tell all of the neighbors and relatives what a bad boy Sonny is. Whereupon Sonny comes to the conclusion that he might just as well be a bad boy, since nothing he ever does is right anyway! "Why, honey, you don't have to be a little gentleman if you don't want to," says the modern mother. "But do stop that noise, because it gives me a headache." This sort of treatment, plus intelligent and persistent essays on the reasons why people do things that they do in consideration for other people who have to live in the world with them, will make a real little gentleman out of Sonny-painlessly for both of you! 38

Courtesy and Consideration Teaching Children to Feel Good 

Manners

CHILDREN actually may be taught to feel good manners, if their interest in good manners and courtesy is aroused. The home should be the temple of good manners, for this is where manners are taught and practised; and if one does not practise good manners, courtesy, and consideration at home, one will never be truly courteous elsewhere.

Modern 

Courtesy 

THIS Country suffered, and profited, in post-war days, from an epidemic of books purporting to teach courtesy and good manners to all and sundry. For months young, middleaged, and old America agonized over where a gentleman should walk when he was escorting two ladies, no girl ever dared to "ask a young man in" after the theater, and chicken salad almost disappeared from the menus of the earth.

Nothing could have been more asinine nor yet more beneficial; because literally millions purchased books on these subjects, and in trying to assimilate them came to the conclusion that all hard-and-fast rules for this display of consideration toward others, which is called "courtesy,” are ridiculous.

As a result, many realized what had heretofore been realized by only a few-that true courtesy and real "good manners" were just an inner desire for beauty and harmony in one's life, expressed toward others and thereby invited by others. 39

Lovely Ladies 

Good Manners 

Do Not mean Courtesy

Good manners alone do not embrace courtesy. This latter quality includes the doing of those little things that one could leave undone without necessarily displaying a lack of good manners, yet which contribute in such great measure to the comfort of those whose society you are enjoying.

The only etiquette that a young man must observe when he escorts a young lady out for an evening's entertainment is the knowledge of what will contribute to her comfort and enjoyment and what will contribute to her embarrassment and confusion. He does the first things, and he strictly refrains from doing the latter.

One may index a married man by the courtesy he displays toward his wife; because the courtesy with which he treats other women may have some business or social motive, and the courtesy with which he treats his mother may be merely the result of respect and obedience which he has had figuratively and perhaps literally pounded into him. But the manner in which he treats his wife, both privately and in public, is an exact indication of his good breeding or lack of it. This applies to the wife as well. It matters not whether love and respect still reign in their relationship. As long as they are man and wife, should all else fail, there remains that eternal refuge of civilization-courtesy.

AND while we are on the subject, what about disputes and arguments? Between business partners, between broth- 40

Courtesy and Consideration Disputes and Arguments ers, between sisters, and between husbands and wives. 

They are tiresome and a terrible bore in the first place, and quite useless-but if you simply must have them the world should never know about it. Despite the fact that there is no rule in the books of etiquette about this, public wrangling is, nevertheless, one of the most glaring breaches of good form. 

It takes two to make an argument, you know. Keep your mouth shut until you are safely behind the doors of home, and the chances are that by the time you are there both will have forgotten the difference. Think of the time and the energy you have saved. Think of your nerves!

Tolerance is the means to a peaceful existence. 

And Poise 

A WELL-BRED person never is embarrassed.

Even in the most embarrassing situations imaginable, poise and a sense of humor will save the day. 

What if you should happen to use the wrong fork at the dinner table! If you are dining in a public place it is not at all improbable that the fork you are using was in the wrong place to begin with. Nowadays, even in the best hotels, it is not unusual to see a fish fork masquerading as a salad fork, for instance.

Impolite 

Disputation 

ONE of the most detestable things that men do is to argue and fuss with a waiter or taxi driver or doorman when they are escorting women somewhere. If a man must do this he should indulge his justification when he is alone, for it is always 41

Lovely Ladies painful for a woman to have to share or observe any such scene no matter how right or how wrong either party is. 

A woman doesn't have to permit her escort to do thismore than once. Simply tell him quietly and firmly—after you are out of hearing of the person to whom his rudeness was directed that you do not wish such a thing to occur again. That, if he must be rude, he must wait until he is alone or with other women. His surprise will probably cure him of the habit.

Woman Do Not Claim 

Equality with

Men in Everything

THERE are some very sad little ideas flying around which I should like to do my bit to change. These ideas relate to the equality of men and women.

All men, and many women, have the ridiculous idea that we claim equality with men in everything. We do not! Intelligent women claim mental equality with men, and that is all. Also, they claim spiritual superiority. But we do not, and never can, claim physical equality.

Women are not equipped by nature to be as strong an animal as a man. Every man knows that women experience natural bodily functions which are always more or less painful, and which rob women of a certain amount of strength. This, and motherhood, unfit women for physical competition with men.

Therefore, even a weak man usually is stronger than a strong woman. And it is nothing short of poor sportsmanship for men to try to give us what they think we claim in 42

Courtesy and Consideration equality by imposing upon us the difficulties, if not the suffering, of this physical equality. The worst part is, that we, being high-spirited creatures, accept it and bear it. 

But this does not alter the fact that it is unfair to us, and very poor sportsmanship on the part of men when they do not try to save us every physical strain they can. 

Try This on

Your Street 

Car 

WHAT actually keeps a woman from saying to a seated man on a street car: "I beg your pardon, but I am tired and unwell. Would you be so kind as to let me sit where you are sitting?"

Probably, if he is a grouch, he will answer that he also is tired, or something of the sort. But, tired as he may be, he never can be as tired as a woman when she is tired, simply because he is a man and she is a woman. Try this sometime. Remember, courage is not boldness.

The Perils of Physical Exertion 

A WOMAN carrying a heavy suitcase? Take it from her! This little courtesy may save a child's life and insure the health of several other children.

It is quite true that some women antagonize men by assuming an attitude of physical equality. But two wrongs do not make a right, gentlemen!

Lovely ladies have given men the delicious privilege of chivalry since the world began. And even a lovely modern lady subtly exacts and invariably receives chivalry from men-whether this be from her own husband, the men with whom she comes in contact in the business world, 43

Lovely Ladies or her tradespeople and servants. And this she exacts and receives, not by demanding it, but because she inspires it. 

Yet, I might add, do not go through life exaggerating the so-called frailties of "the weaker sex," using this as reason for expecting men to worship you and make your path through life a bed of roses carefully pruned of thorns. 

More About 

Manners

OBVIOUS good manners are bad manners. 

Ostentation is the paste diamond of good breeding. A well-bred woman, for instance, will stand whenever another woman or a man considerably older than herself enters a room. But for her to stand whenever any person of any age came into the room would be a tiresome exaggeration of this idea. A courteous woman will unostentatiously care for the comfort of a much older woman, but if she fusses over her as if she were an invalid it is only annoying.

Good manners come from good breeding and are more or less instinctive. They may be acquired, of course, but courtesy, while it is the expression of good manners in relation to others, goes slightly deeper than that. It has its roots in the subconscious kindness which causes you to make many little gestures toward the comfort of other people. And it hasn't a great deal to do with custom, either. An egotist, by the way, can never be a courteous, well-bred person, since he thinks of himself and his feelings and desires to the utter exclusion of anyone else. 

The other person-that's the thing. "Do unto others..." and oddly enough and averaging it all up, they do! 44

Chapter VII THE ART OF HOSPITALITY YOUR home is the frame of a masterpiece. 

The masterpiece is you. 

Starting when you were very young you have marceled, massaged, punished, coaxed, urged, instructed, and disciplined your inner self, your personality, into the shape that you want it to take, into the masterpiece that you are. 

And then you make a frame for this masterpiece, which is your home. When it is varnished with charm, gilded with happiness, mellowed with heartache, it is the appropriate frame for you. At your invitation the outside world looks in, and the favored ones are allowed to enter. "In Palaces of

Fade- 

WHETHER your home consists of a single room or a spacious mansion, this should not make the slightest difference in your manner of receiving the world. Every lovely lady with any claim to individuality at all expresses her personality in the place in which she lives, even if she dwells there but twenty-four hours. And anyone who steps within the frame of your masterpiece should be received with all of the charm and graciousness and cordiality to which he or she is entitled by the mere fact of being allowed there at all. 45

Lovely Ladies A Fine Art 

THE art of receiving guests into your home with charm and graciousness and cordiality is called hospitality. And hospitality is more than just a matter of good manners. It is a matter of the kindly, intelligent cordiality that comes from the heart-allied 2 Darc with courteous manners that make one's home the peaceful, beautiful thing it is.

Let's review the intelligent principles of hospitality. 

You have invited a guest into your own home. Your very first consideration is to make that guest comfortable and put him at his ease. "Just put your hat and coat anywhere at all" may be permissible for old friends, but it is rather disconcerting and cannot be called the acme of graciousness when it is said to a new guest. 

Despite the fact that in certain countries it is considered 46

The Art of Hospitality good form to give week-end guests the run of the house and grounds and see them only at meal times, a truly gracious hostess draws a very careful line between this type of hospitality and the type of overfussiness and supercordiality that merges into actual annoyance. 

Nor will a kindly hostess cease her attentions by merely introducing a new guest to the other guests and leaving him to shift for himself. The only time that it is permissible to leave a guest is when you are absolutely certain that he or she has found someone interesting to talk to or something pleasant to do (even though the latter means that the guest prefers to be left alone), and then you must always watch for the time when that person or thing ceases to interest. Yet do not impose constant watchfulness upon a guest.

There is the saying that a host never enjoys himself at his own party, but must go elsewhere to be treated as a guest in order to enjoy himself. Well-bred and intelligent hospitality is not an effort—the only real way to entertain a guest is to enjoy yourself at the same time. 

Another very important thing—a charming hostess will not impose the routine of her house upon her guests, nor will they disturb this routine if they can avoid it. There is a certain give-and-take of mutual courtesy between guest and hostess, based upon good sportsmanship and courtesy. 

Any routine can be altered to conform to temporary exigencies without being deranged.

There is no actual reason why you cannot adjust such little things as meal times, particularly in the matter of 47

Lovely Ladies breakfast, to your guest's habits. The charm of hospitality is based upon intelligence, and the clever hostess will ascertain her guest's tastes and do everything that she possibly can to conform to them.

Do Not Make Your Guests 

Uncomfortable 

NEVER impose upon a guest anything that he or she might not really wish to do. "Now I am going to let you go out in the garden and pick your very own vegetables for dinner" may be quite amusing for your city guest, if she isn't afraid of bugs or susceptible to sunburn, but I fear that under such circumstances I should politely remark that I preferred eggs if I didn't have to "go and pick them off the bushes myself"! 

If you are a bridge enthusiast, be sure that your guest is one also before you impose rubber after rubber upon him. And if your idea of a pleasant Sunday afternoon is a picnic in the woods with the children, interspersed with hard-boiled eggs (the picnic, I mean, not the children), mosquitoes, and poison oak or ivy, don't impose it upon your guest, even though he politely accepts the invitation because he doesn't want to hurt your feelings, unless you are quite sure he will enjoy it. 

Let Well 

Enough Alone 

BE A perfect hostess-in making your guests feel at home but not too well cared for. 

Furnish his room, for instance, with all of the little intimate things that your instinctive daintiness tells you will contribute to his bodily or mental comfort- 48

The Art of Hospitality but don't fuss over him. See that he is not bored, and see also that he is not bored with your efforts to see that he is not bored! 

Unless you are prepared to expend this kindly cordiality toward a guest do not have guests. If it should be necessary, for diplomatic or any other purposes, to have a guest toward whom it is difficult for you to show this cordiality, then you must simulate it—if you would be a lovely lady. 

And in the fun of the simulation you may discover in your guest really interesting and pleasing qualities that you did not suspect.

ONE does not learn the fine art of hospi- 

Not by Rule tality by rules. But a very good base upon which to build is to keep your guest's tastes, habits, desires, and inclinations in mind every minute of his or her stay; and the proper kind of a guest will not impose his or her tastes or habits upon you. This mutual consideration cannot but result in a pleasant interlude and a fragrant memory.

Quite naturally, a woman expresses in her hospitality everything that she has in herself. Her daintiness and efficiency are expressed in the way she provides for her guest's comfort; her kindness, sweetness, charm, and graciousness contribute to the atmosphere of the home and consequently to this happiness. Her sense of humor entertains him, and her personality and individuality interest him. Unless a home expresses all of these things, which are but mute tributes to the woman who is the 49

Lovely Ladies ruling quality of them all, then she has no right to have guests. Especially any guest one does not wish to have see too closely into one's personal life. The slightest strain in the atmosphere will ruin the complete program. 

A faux pas must be handled as it would be handled at any other time, guest or no guest. The children should be disciplined as usual, but well-bred discipline is at no time offensive or uncomfortable, for either the disciplinarian or the person disciplined. Remember the sense of humor that saves all perilous situations!

The Art of 

Hospitality an 

Essential of Loveliness 

THE art of hospitality is absolutely necessary to the completion of a lovely lady's personality. All of its roots are within her. 

And in proffering the blossoms to the stranger whom you esteem sufficiently to admit to your home you are but indulging in a pleasant personal expression of your own loveliness.

Invite only such guests within the frame of your masterpiece as you know will fit harmoniously into the scheme of things. Then, well-bred and intelligent hospitality will not be the effort that it might otherwise be. Yourself, your home, your hospitality-three things that follow in a logical sequence. Handle them in their correct relation to each other, and make yourself and others happy by doing so. 50

Chapter VIII "WOMANLY MODESTY" 

As you progress in this book, you cannot but have a feeling that you are embarking on a crusade, something like a knight of old, marching along in shining armor to storm and overturn the smug forces of unreasonable traditions and false ethics, in the name of Beauty and Harmony, both optical and spiritual. 

Now what, I ask you, is "womanly modesty"? How often we've heard that old phrase chanted! The trouble is that when we get out the old microscope called "reason and logic" and put "womanly modesty" on the slide, we find that we can't see anything. There just isn't anything there!

Modesty actually is a civilized instinct of refinement. But womanly modesty, a weapon fashioned and bejeweled by women to intrigue men, is despicable. From mother to daughter, generation after generation, the thing called modesty has been handed down in the guise of a virtue. 

Woman's whole life revolved around men, trickery was legitimate, and any means to attract the necessary man were fair. 

I'm sorry if I'm tearing down some of your pet ideas, but the truth is that womanly modesty is, in the light of 51

Lovely Ladies our intelligence, something less than the flame of a candle which has been extinguished. 

I use the metaphor of the candle advisedly, for it is particularly apt. Once the candle flame was there, and there was, at one time, such a thing as womanly modesty. 

But that thing was not a virtuous thing, nor was it admirable. It was, instead, an execrable cloak for unworthy desires and dishonest trickery in the face of man's consuming curiosity. He wants to see what he cannot see, and he wants to know what he cannot know.

How False 

Modesty Was 

Used as Bait 

WOMAN played upon this curiosity by concealment. That's a brief history of womanly modesty. A false modesty was established, and when anyone says "womanly modesty" what is meant is "false modesty."

Woman found that it quite suited her purpose to exalt modesty and to make it a virtue and its absence a sin. 

Why Womanly 

Modesty Was

Made 

Virtuous "BUT," I hear you say, "I've always thought that womanly modesty was a virtuous thing. Surely you're not going to attack virtue so openhandedly?" 

Don't be alarmed. I'm not going to attack virtue, because in this instance there is no virtue. 

Womanly modesty became a virtuous ethic because it so handily served an interesting purpose. Any act, custom, or privilege which is performed or enjoyed by a majority of the people will become a virtue. Was it not at one time the 52

"Womanly Modesty" 

custom in ancient Russia, for instance, for the husband's father to have access to the young wife's bedroom? And the young woman's acquiescence was considered the highest sign of filial obedience and respect. 

Modesty Has No Sex 

I Do not for one moment wish to be construed as ridiculing modesty. Spontaneous and natural modesty, which is merely an instinct of refinement and a desire to keep one's own self and life a little aloof from the mob, is a natural effect of civilization. Real modesty and womanly modesty have nothing in common.

Modesty has no sex. It is of neuter gender. And true modesty springs from the character of the individual and is not something that has to be taught. That which offends the natural instincts of refinement is immodest. 

Why should it be generally thought that only women must be modest? Modesty is a prerogative of all civilized persons.

As a general thing men are less modest than women primarily because they are also less sensitive. Of course I am speaking of generalities, and of course there are men who are as modest and acutely refined as any woman. I can see no particular reason why there should be any difference between men and women in the matter of true modesty, but the fact remains that men sometimes inflict deep psychological scars upon women through a lack of appreciation of the more sensitive feminine nerve mechanism. 53

Lovely Ladies Social Exigency 

OF COURSE it is difficult to say what the natural civilized instincts of refinement might be, because these cannot but differ with social conditions. As an example, a business woman who necessarily adopts somewhat conservative clothing might easily be pardoned considerable confusion if forced to don the scanty attire of a chorus girl and exhibit herself in public. Her confusion might be put down to modesty, and although the real root of the matter is much deeper than that, the example serves to show how conceptions of modesty may differ. Understand, though, that this difference in conception has nothing whatever to do with the consciously false and calculated modesty that we discussed at the beginning of this chapter. 

The Other 

Extreme 

AS ALWAYS, there is the other extreme.

We have with us to-day many women, especially young women, who, exactly because their elders tried to force false standards of modesty upon them when they were children, have broken away from this tradition to set up a defiant standard of their own, which is just as false. 

This is offensively evident in young girls who have been taught the virtue of modesty in schools in so distorted and exaggerated a way that the inevitable result is one of revulsion and rebellion in those who have character and enough intelligence to refuse to be browbeaten.
