Many selfstyled modern young women thus offend greatly, throwing off too completely and at too great a sacrifice of re- 54

"Womanly Modesty" 

finement the hypocritical ideas of modesty that were forced upon them in their childhood, in their very efforts to insist that they will not be hypocrites. 

Demonstrations and displays are vulgar, and it is the part of true modesty to refrain from any action or speech which, although you yourself may consider it entirely casual, may yet shock or offend those in whose company you find yourself.

False Mod esty in

Children PSYCHOLOGY, Comparatively new as a popu- 

The Danger of lar science, has shown us what tremendous effect the lessons of youth may have on the mature individual. I do not wish to attempt to speak as a psychologist, for that

I am not. But I do want to point out to you the extreme danger of inculcating in children a false idea of modesty. 

The sex-conscious child faces an enormous handicap in life, and there is no reason why it should. Don't, unthinkingly perhaps, pass on to your children the false ideals that you were, perhaps also unthinkingly, taught to consider as a virtue. Teach your children not to regard natural functions and phenomena as immodest. By doing this you will not only secure to your children innumerable benefits, but you will also safeguard them from many real perils. 

A Personal 

Thing It is almost impossible to say where the line should be drawn between what should and what should not offend or outrage the instincts of refinement. Men impose upon their wives and sweethearts their ideas-young women impose upon the 55

Lovely Ladies world their ideas-without the least concession to the other person's sensitiveness, training, and environment. 

No one should do this. True modesty is essentially an innate and personal thing, and while it should not be used to cloak sex allure, individual differences of opinion should be respected.

Modesty is a civilized instinct of refinement. Analyze it, strip it of its fallacies, and accord it its due. 56 59

Chapter IX A SENSE OF HUMOR 

HUMOR is another of the intangibles, an invaluable aid to poise and one of the essentials of charm. Perhaps it is one of the most elusive qualities that our lovely lady, to be lovely, must possess. 

A sense of humor is really a necessity to "life as she is lived" in this turbulent, obstreperous, wicked, roaring, gallumping, interesting, lovable Twentieth 

Century, and a lovely lady should have an appreciation of the comical, and the ability to indulge in the expression of this appreciation, laughter.

Life without a sense of humor is food without salt. 

Humor, like salt used properly, gives to everything a more piquant flavor. "Attic salt" is the term we use for the refined, gentle wit of the ancient Greeks, than whom no race lived more beautifully and fully.

But salt used in large quantities is an actual poison, and a sense of humor is not that neurotic cynicism, the refuge 57

Lovely Ladies of the misfit, which distorts everything and everyone into something funny. That is the abuse of humor, and the gracious personality is ever on the alert to guard against it. 

Humor is really the recognition of the inappropriate, of the incongruous, almost of the discordant, but this recognition must itself be appropriate. Humor may be said to be compensation for the discordant-Nature's safety valve, her way of helping us poor humans out when life becomes too real and too earnest. 

There you have an excellent test for humor-for false humor can be easily recognized because it makes life more discordant than ever, and true humor is a balm and a healing. 

In order to appreciate the comic it is not necessary to hurt others or to compensate one's self, which is too often the cynic's unconscious purpose. No, humor is a universal compensation, a universal harmony, and laughter is the true music of the spheres. Goodness knows there are enough things both actual and metaphysical to tickle the most highly developed sense of humor from the cradle to the grave, without ever once having to indulge your humor at the expense of another person's feelings! 

And right here I want to talk about the modern generation's attitude toward their elders. Their ideas may be rusty, poky, and antiquated, and our highly developed sense of humor may see the ridiculous in many things our ancestors revered. But we can see the ridiculous in these things without in any way scoffing at them and misjudging their value for others. Everyone holds sacred something 58

A Sense of Humor in life has this highly tensed emotional feeling about something which says to other people, "Please do not touch. This is mine and I value it highly." It is fair play and good sportsmanship to respect that attitude. 

Stupidity of 

Sarcasm 

SARCASM-and all cynical humor inspired by and indulged in at the expense of another person-is the easiest form of humor, so simple and so obvious that there is nothing really clever about it. It is always cheap. It is easier for any person with the intelligence of an average ten-year-old child to be sarcastic than it is for him to suppress the desire to make a sarcastic remark. Haven't you ever noticed how much more difficult it is not to answer at all than to answer with all of the clever and witty responses that come at once to your mind? 

Kindness from

Experience 

WHAT is that instinctive kindness that some have to a degree, that is ever on the alert not to hurt another? It is a subconscious remembrance of personal pain, and a desire to shield others from it. Anyone who hurts another shows either that sorrow has never truly touched him, or that he has not the mentality to analyze his own grief. 

And anyone with instinctive kindness will not hurt others with humor. No matter how irresistible an opportunity may be, a kind person cannot be truly amused when his amusement will cause unhappiness.

One must remember that there are very subtle ways of hurting others. Naturally a courteous person will make 59

Lovely Ladies every effort to suppress a desire to laugh when someone takes a comical tumble, but this is a humorous situation that is entirely obvious. Other situations that are just as comical but far less obvious require as great and even greater self-control.

Impersonal 

Humor 

You have not a sense of humor at all unless you can appreciate the comical aspect of impersonal things. Comedy that is built around the lives, the eccentricities, characteristics, griev、 ances, and petty pains of individuals is too obvious for the real humorist. Ask yourself just what inspires the most of your humor. 

Life will have more glamour for you when you can appreciate the comical and ridiculous. Tense moments will lose their tenseness and end in a tinkle of laughterunpleasant situations always have their humorous port of escape. But remember that your sense of humor, like any other precious thing, needs a wise restraint and understanding which can only spring from true nobility of character.

Developing Humor 

How can one develop a sense of humor?

By paralleling the evident aspect of a thing with its humorous aspect. But not in a way that will cause it to lose its true value or become in any way distorted. After the first few determined efforts to see the little grains of humor that are ever there, like the glitter of gold in a pan of sand imparting to the most prosaic things its sheen, your sense of humor, like any 60

A Sense of Humor other carefully tended thing, will begin to grow and develop. 

Just as you play tennis or golf or go swimming when you wish to relax your body, just so your mind finds it necessary to play when the serious work of the day is done. And this play of the mind is humor. Never misuse it. Be intelligent about it. And Restraint 

AND now just one thing more-humor is one of the graces of life, and is there anything graceful or attractive about a woman who laughs continually? Remember that a sense of humor manifests itself very often in restraint rather than overexercise. Laughing can be perilously near to giggling, and having a sense of humor does not mean being silly. 

A completely charming and lovely personality should include a sense of humor. The sweetest and prettiest woman in the world without one is to the mind what a beautiful cake is to the appetite-if it is made without salt. 

Humor is truly the salt of life-for it is as necessary as salt, it can be as poisonous as salt is in too large quantities, and when applied to the open places of spiritual sensitiveness it can hurt and sear wounds so that they will never heal. 

Try to appreciate all of life's comedy and whimsicalities.

A woman without a sense of humor truly misses half of life, since the half of life and even more is comical. And how can you expect to be all that a lovely lady can and should be if you can only appreciate half of life? 61

Chapter X THE ART IN ILLNESS 

You are ill. Perhaps you are not seriously ill, but you feel so badly that that fact doesn't comfort you in the least.

And now the real you is going to burst its silken sheaths of culture, and you are going to show yourself to yourself and to others as you really are.

There is a humorous saying among doctors to the effect that they can always tell whether or not a woman patient is seriously ill by the condition of her coiffure and the daintiness of her lingerie. This attitude is unkind and untrue. A lovely lady is lovely under any and all circumstances! And every nurse can tell you of women they have 62

The Art in Illness seen in the most desperate cases of illness who retained all of their charm and even added to it with their sense of humor and good sportsmanship.

Now the hundred years or so that have just passed when a woman's refinement and good breeding were gauged by her delicate health and numerous illnesses, were perverted years. Even yet we have not quite removed their effect from our subconscious. But we are doing so, and I look for the development during this century of a wholesome contempt for illness based upon an appreciation of beauty as expressed in a healthy body. 

BUT you are ill this moment. Do realize 

You Are Ill you that now more than any other time you must discipline your nerves? Not only does giving vent to them harm you and retard your recovery, but it makes everyone around you so miserable that they are not inclined to do all of the little things for you that they would otherwise be delighted to do, and which would make the dreary days so much more pleasant.

Try to get a perspective on yourself. 

There you are in your little bed and probably in painjust a little speck of quivering nerves, of scarcely any importance whatsoever in the scheme of things; even though your suffering may cause you to lose your sense of humor and perspective to the extent of considering yourself very important indeed, try to realize that you actually are important only to yourself. This is not courage, neither is it fortitude-it is nothing at all but good sense. 63

Lovely Ladies 

Ugliness as a

Diagnosis of 

Illness 

Sympathy and kindness you will get, if you merit them. 

You cannot command them. The more of a good sport you are, the more sympathetic and kind will you find people. 

AND now as to the opinion of the esteemed medical profession that a woman is only as ill as she permits herself to be ugly. 

What lovely lady can be too ill to have her hair neatly combed, to have a speck of powder on her nose, and to wear a dainty nightgown? And what a miraculous effect a bit of rouge has upon your moraleand the attention of those around you! If you are so ill that these things are really an effort for you, there is probably a nurse in attendance pleasantly willing to do them for you if you but ask her. 

And a word in defense of nurses. It is astonishing how a little pain will make anyone lose his or her perspective. 

Remember, if you are in a hospital, that you are not the only patient, but if you are pleasant and courteous to your nurse and your sense of humor does not fail you she will be found to have many little tricks up her sleeve for making you more comfortable than are included in her professional services.

At Home in a Hospital 

WOULD you like to know approximately what a lovely lady should take to the hospital? That is for anything over a week's stay, and hospital trips are seldom less.

First of all, think of your hospital room as a hotel room, not as a hospital. Just calling it a "hospital" seems to do 64

The Art in Illness something to one's morale! (There will be a day when hospital rooms will be equipped like hotel rooms, and when this happens more people will visit them for the rest and repairs which bodies need so in these hectic days.) 

The very first things that you must prepare are your nightgowns. Of course, hospitals furnish nightgowns, but what garments! You must have at least seven or eight nightgowns-the three or four that are enough for home will never do for the hospital. Don't forget that you wear a nightgown twenty-four hours of the day and that you must have a fresh one every morning. But you do not have to spend a great deal of money on nightgowns. 

Borrow them from the rest of the family or make some very simple ones, of georgette. The material you can use afterward for other things. 

Cosmetics! Think of all the time you are going to have to make yourself beautiful! Take not only your make-up and daily beauty aids, but skin foods, face packs, hair tonic, and all of the other things you have ever used or wanted to try-not to mention rubber face contraptions, ankle binders, and things of that sort. By no means forget your perfumes and toilet waters, your hand and face soap, face cloths, bath salts, talcum powder, and simple medicines such as hand lotion, witch hazel, etc.

There is no reason why the hospital dresser cannot be as attractive as your dresser at home. Take all of your toilet articles with you-handkerchief box, jewelry box-and they will do much to slay the hospital atmosphere. 

You will want your books-I mean your favorite books, 65

Lovely Ladies not just the new ones that obliging friends give you to readand magazines. And then, how about having a subscription to your newspaper sent to you for just these few weeks? 

And then include in the list anything else that you want to work upon with your restless fingers! Just reading will not fill the long hours, you know-sewing or embroidery will be amusing. 

Is there any reason why you shouldn't take, or have sent, your favorite vases? Vases are always at a premium in hospitals, and the ones they do have are seldom delightful to the eye.

And though it may sound far-fetched, you might take some dainty chinaware, at least a cup or two, if nothing else, and a pretty water glass and a carafe. 

Also don't you think that friends will be more inclined to come, and go away with a much pleasanter memory, if they have seen your wanness softened by pastel-tinted sheets? 

Your favorite silken spread might be another apt suggestion. 

And there you are! There is no reason why you should be without anything that you wish, and there is no need for you to be asking every kind visitor to bring you this, that, and the other thing. Arrange your hospital trip as you do everything else wisely, firmly. "My

Operation" HELP yourself to recover, and then forget it!

Why is it that so many people, although they know quite well how uncomfortable and miserable 66

The Art in Illness it makes them to hear someone else go into descriptive details about an illness, nevertheless impose in the same way upon their friends after they have been ill?

Again, I say that this is due merely to a loss of perspective and an ailing sense of humor. "My Operation" has been sadly overworked and is no longer either sympathy inspiring or interesting to anyone but yourself. 

Believe me, I know! Now when I had my last operation...! 67

Chapter XI AGING GRACEFULLY 

Jang THIS is a chapter for all women -after thirty.

Any woman who allows herself to fret and fume over advancing age, who permits herself the point of view that sees each approaching year with terror and aversion, who looks at the years that have passed with regret, is stupid.

For the modern woman of thirty, with her modish frocks, her beautiful figure, and her interesting mind, is to-day unquestionably in the very prime of her loveliness. She is beautiful, charming, witty and interesting, interested and intelligent enough to remain so. It is she who reigns supreme, if she has taken intelligent care of her body and face. She is the woman men admire and the woman whose company they seek. 68

Aging Gracefully

There was a day when, approaching thirty and recognizing that she had acquired spiritual qualities and appreciation, tolerance, charm, and other understandings possible only through experience, a woman mourned her departing youth and deeply regretted what she considered the inevitable price paid to the jealous gods of Experience and 

Maturity. No longer! For the only price that maturity to-day demands is appreciation. And if you appreciate anything, you will care for it.

Intelligence will show you the way and ways. Unintelligent women excuse themselves by "lack of time." There is no lack of time to a cultured woman who realizes the need of caring for her person. If necessary she lets something else wait-beauty and loveliness never.

No Old Women 

TO-DAY there is no such a thing among intelligent women as an old woman.

For modern intelligent women know how to retain their interests, as well as their physical contours. 

The one follows the other. Everything to-day is “youthifying." Youthful styles, dieting to regain a youthful figure, rejuvenation and beauty operations of all sorts-'twould seem that Ponce de León actually did find the fountain of youth in America, after all! 

Yet the very term "youthifying" is an erroneous one.

Actually the youthful craze of to-day is simply the product of a realization that there is no need for a woman to acquire either the mental or physical appearance of age. 

For those who have already allowed such things to happen 69

Lovely Ladies to them there are measures, stringent measures, for repairing the mistake. For the modern young woman approaching thirty these are only reminders.

As little as half a century ago average women began to think of themselves as old when they were thirty. Only the exceptional woman did not. And these average women were considered old because they let themselves be considered so. Life therefore relegated them to the certain modes, manners, and places set apart for "middle-aged" and "old" women. But old women, God bless us, where are they to-day? Gone, surely-as surely as the wasp waist that was a perfect symbol of what life was trying to do to them.

Wonderful Women 

BEFORE her death I had the privilege of seeing Isadora Duncan with Elizabeth

Arden. The latter claimed to be nearly twice the age of the former. Yet in appearance she was exactly the reverse. The marvelous Isadora lived strongly and met Time fiercely, yet with humor. The interesting 

Elizabeth refuses to meet Time at all, but keeps her at a distance with a gentle firmness. She has even gone so far as to make a profession of this and to try to teach it to other women. 

Sounds silly? Sounds bromidic? It is neither. You have only to look at certain wonderful women that this age has produced, to see it: Sarah Bernhardt, who kept the fires of youth in her heart and soul even while she kept an artificially colored but none the less effective mop of fiery 70

Aging Gracefully hair burning as a beacon of defiance to the Three Sisters; 

Eleanora Duse, whose romance with the Italian poet will tread the pages of history along with those of Dante and 

Beatrice, Romeo and Juliet, Abélard and Héloise; Paris's beloved Mistinguette, and soul-stirring Cécile Sorel; our own Ann Pennington, amusing Mae Murray, and 

America's sweetheart, Mary Pickford. Not, indeed, that these last three have as many years to their credit as the others, but they help recall to mind the fact that year after year they have been the same Ann and Mae and Mary to us and never will change. Of that I am certain. 

What have these women that you have not? Nothing.

They have not done a single, solitary thing that you cannot do or that you are not going to do. And if you are going to try to get out of this by saying that life was perhaps not as hard for them as it is for you, that is just a lazy excuse which isn't even good enough to believe in yourself. 

There is no harder life than that of a woman whose life is an open book to the temperamental public! Keep

Interesting THE real secret of not growing old is the keeping of your interest in life. Nothing can bring back lost youth, and there is no reason to lose youth. Youth merely means aliveness and interest and bodily beauty. The woman who adopts a modish coiffure, or a certain type of hat, and then forgets to change it is as old as the style of her coiffure or hat. 

Intelligent modern women do not allow clothing, point 71

Lovely Ladies of view, or embonpoint to catalogue them, as their grandmothers once did. 

And when did you and why did you begin wearing those long skirts and those so-called "sensible" shoes? 

They make you old, and yet do not, as you fondly believe, add one whit of "dignity" or "respectability" to your appearance.

Perhaps it is too much to expect of the woman who has allowed herself to become old to change her ideas overnight—to put on a modish gown, chic but still quite sensible clothes, and have her hair bobbed. But there is no reason for her having acquired this elderly point of view in the first place! No excuse for having let age creep upon her with its unmistakable indications. Had she kept her interest in life in this very interesting age she would naturally have moved with it. 

Keep Youth 

LET me repeat again, that modern women do not allow themselves to grow old and then make a frantic inroad on their pocketbooks and dispositions in an attempt to regain what they have lost. 

Each year, perhaps, after thirty, you must increase slightly your care of yourself. You must watch your diet-I refer you to the chapter on the subject in this book—and you must increase the amount of rest you take-not necessarily the amount of sleep, but the amount of relaxation and repose. The clever woman who can do so spends at least one day or two days, if possible, every month in bed, eating only very lightly, seeing no one, and getting caught 72

Aging Gracefully up on all of her reading and possibly letters. I do not mean of course two days in succession, but one day every two weeks. 

And she can give attention to her beauty these daysextra attentions that cannot be given it every night: hair attention, facials, skin foods for the hands, but more than anything else the quiet relaxation, the complete retiring from friends and the world. 

And exercise. You do your morning exercises for eight or ten minutes all your life, of course. I say "of course" because this is one of the principal ways that you have of keeping away the actual brittleness of age. As you grow older you will discard certain of these exercises and add certain others; notably those that stretch your spine and keep all of your muscles flexible. You cannot grow old if you do this! So far as the little things that you must watch in regard to changing hair, skin, and personality, I refer you to the chapters on Types and Colors. 

A New Point of View 

BEFORE thirty Milady may be permitted to take her beauty with only a more or less vague understanding of the mysterious machinations which impel her toward loveliness. But by thirty-if not before-it is time for every woman to give serious consideration to certain deep spiritual and ethical principles which underlie the whole subject. The result of this consideration is the realization that the intelligent woman cannot grow old nor be unlovely. 

Why do you seek beauty and charm? Is it because you 73

Lovely Ladies vaguely feel that it will enhance your chances for enjoyment and bring to you more of the good things of life? 

Is your quest merely an egocentric manifestation dictated by a desire to impress those around you? 

Before thirty you might answer "yes" to any or all of these questions. But by thirty a woman should begin to realize her obligation to be beautiful, and to remain beautiful throughout her life. Modern women do not confess their years by wrinkles, dry skin, sagging chins, aged-looking throats, and unrestrained hips. Any woman who permits these misfortunes does so deliberately. And these signs of encroaching years, flaunted, as they must be, before the world, brand a woman immediately as lacking in appreciation, intelligence, and culture. 

What a tale those years from the baptismal font to the easy maturity of thirty comprise! Joys and sorrows, disappointments and surprises, achievements and failures, passions, loves, hates-the whole gamut of human emotions unrolls. And each new experience, each new sensation, adds a cubit to the spiritual stature of the intelligent woman. Hence at thirty a woman comes into the richest and most important inheritance which life has to offer. 

She is sympathetic, she understands. Her dreams, her hopes, and her loves are tempered with the quality of reality, and her appreciation is mellowed by perspective. 

She is a more interesting companion and a better friend. 

She has survived the "nothing-really-matters" stage and learned to feel the rich fullness of life. 

These are spiritual qualities—the just wages of Task- 74

Aging Gracefully master Life for the sometimes grim business of living, and without them mere beauty is an empty shell, and lasting charm impossible of achievement. But you must not put a priceless pearl in an old shoe for safe keeping, and that is exactly what a mature woman does when she allows herself to become "aged," just when life is handing her the princely perquisites of personality. 

The "sweet young things" still passing through stages of physical development, with their eyes seeking out transient interests rather than immutable and idealistic goals, are not your rivals. For you have arrived. You are of importance in the world. You have something to offer your fellow human beings. It is something which you have lived all your life to acquire. You cannot toss it away through neglect.

Papa Legbo may exert a potent influence in the dim half light of a tropical jungle, and blacks with kinky hair and quivering limbs may bring savory sacrifices to purchase his august protection against the ravages of the years. But, as some keen observer already has remarked, we do those things better here. Modern women need carry no rabbit's foot to thwart Old Father Time! 

Nor will I counsel prayers, incantations, potents, or burnt offerings. Instead a few simple and logical rites conscientiously practised will secure to you the retention of corporal charms at an age when your grandmother would have been scandalized by any program which included aught but a seat by the fireside and a ball of worsted! 75

Lovely Ladies 

"Father Time" 

FIRST let us consider this all-important question of Time. "Father Time," the classics have it. Well-perhaps. An old gentleman with a flowing white beard and an ancient scythe, ensconced in a moss-covered rocky corner, may be an excellent symbol for the passive conception of Time. 

But no woman with her wits about her can be content with a passive picture of Time. To the modern woman Time is an active agent. 

And so I have my own whimsical picture of Time. She is an old lady-a perfect dear, to be sure! She wears a little black bonnet which is held determinedly upon her head by funereal ribbons tied beneath her chin. They cascade over her bosom like strips of sheet onyx, and sometimes float out behind her like battle flags. Beneath her left arm Time carries an umbrella, tightly rolled, and of blackest black. Her thin right hand clutches a reticule, and her abdomen pops out in front in grotesquely humorous fashion, for all the world as if some experimenting taxidermist had stuffed her when he was not quite sure just where to distribute the masses. And so she bobs along, with her sweet and ingratiating smile, laying a trembling hand upon your arm to ask an innocent question and thanking you with such pathetic sincerity that you entirely forget her sinister influence and give her a tolerant smile, feeling secure in your temporary fitness. 

It may be the umbrella, or again it may be the reticule which holds the secret of Time's potency. Whichever it is, she is a designing old woman. She is subtle. She wheedles, 76

Aging Gracefully she amuses, and she coaxes. And for a time you let her get away with it. Then a sudden tremor of alarm.

You gaze in the mirror and see the delicate traceries which she has drawn under your eyes. And you perceive just the faintest suggestion of a double chin. Horrors! You start to fight. You threaten her with violence. You storm against the old woman-try to lay a rough hand on her shoulder and turn her about. And then she's got you! For you can't get rough with Time. She is impervious to violence-in fact, she thrives on it. 

When you realize that the charming old lady whom you are entertaining at tea really is your arch-enemy Time, keep cool! You must repulse her gently but firmly. If you worry, or fret, or fume, you simply are playing into her hands. And if you think for a moment that she is stronger than you are, then you might as well give up right there.

This latter point is most important. You can diet and exercise, and roll and pitch about for the rest of your life without getting any benefit whatever unless you have the proper mental attitude. You must cultivate the "will to beauty." You must realize that you can remain young and beautiful, that you can't grow old and ugly. Proof

EXCEPTIONAL women have left us con clusive proof that age is a state of mind and not body. Zenobia, Queen of Palmyra, walked at the head of her troops when she was seventy years old. Semiramis, through her charm and beauty, caused so much strife in her sixty-second year that she sensibly abdicated 77

Lovely Ladies in favor of her son. Phryne, who is said to have posed for 

Appelas's picture of Venus rising from the sea, and from whom Praxiteles modeled the Cnidian Venus, secured before a group of severe judges, by her beauty alone, acquittal for a crime she did not commit. And she was then over fifty.

Contrast THE Chevalier de Villiers stabbed himself for love of Ninon de Lenclos after she was sixty, and her love affair with the Abbé Gédoyn commenced on her eightieth birthday. She was truly a beauty, but she was much, much more. Indeed, it has often been said that those qualities which made her one of the most fascinating women of all time would have made her so if she had been entirely without beauty.

Nor was her beauty an outstanding one. Rather it was a symphony of perfections. Ninon was an artiste in love, and her talent to make enduring friends of her many lovers long after her "caprice was over," as she expressed it, was a rare gift. Age made no inroads on the loveliness of Ninon because it was a loveliness which did not depend upon mere physical beauties. In the paradise of her autumn the leaves did not become yellow and the nightingales did not cease to sing. Love still smiled in the tiny wrinkles around her eyes. And young men did not notice that Ninon had lived many years. 

She was seventy-nine years of age when the Abbé 

Gédoyn fell in love with her. And she kept him waiting for her favors until the morning of her eightieth birthday. 78

Aging Gracefully 

What better proof of a woman's triumph over the most dreadful of all antagonists-Time! For the Abbé waited!

After this last love affair, as Saint-Evremond so gracefully says, "She contended herself with ease and rest, having known that there were more lively things in the world."

But there is a reverse to this story, and lest we forget the price of neglect remember Lady Hamilton. She had beauty, but unfortunately she had little else. And it is rather more luck and circumstance that posterity remembers her at all. For she resembled a jewel which did not sparkle unless light fell upon it. Out of the darkness she came when the sun of good fortune illuminated her, shone for a time, and then disappeared again into the bleak night. She, having nothing else but beauty, allowed Time to tarnish her.

Conclusion 

THE years will come and the years will go. 

Will you meet them gracefully? Or will you let life's worries and disappointments, life's luxuries and softnesses of character and physique, carve their tale on your face?

A lovely woman is always a lovely woman. Age can hold no terrors for her. She has friends of each sex, sometimes more friends than she really has room for in her life. And she always has lovers. Nor does it matter at all to her or to any of them that the gentle fingers of time are caressing her, making their little traceries upon her skin, upon her heart, and upon her personality. Because to a lovely 79

Lovely Ladies woman these sculpturing fingers of Time are but finishing the masterpiece that is herself. Youth envies her, but she does not envy youth. Youth could offer her nothing save a smooth face and perhaps fresher roses in her cheeks, and what a tawdry exchange this would be for the gifts she has already had from life! "Woman" should not be qualified by any adjective of time. A woman is a woman when she becomes so. And from then on. And a woman is lovely when she becomes so, and for as long as she keeps herself so. 

Be a woman, and be lovely-no one will ever remember to speculate about the number of years it may have taken you to become so! 80

Chapter XII TOILING FOR LEISURE 

It is necessary for a modern woman to learn the meaning of efficiency. Efficiency will really set you free, for so heavy has become the burden of civilized living that without intelligent organization of all of the little necessary things, one would be forever doing them and have no leisure for anything else.

Therefore intelligent women learn to simplify their duties. Intelligence does it, wise planning, exact and rapid execution. It is the clever woman who gets the most out of life to-day-other women spend all their waking hours just living.

Efficiency the art of doing what has to be done with the least amount of effort in the minimum of time. By relegating every possible gesture to the subconscious and thus rendering it automatic, much time and relaxation can be found. You would be astonished at the number of things you can render automatic by consciously trying to do so! 

An Actuality 

EFFICIENCY is not an abstract virtue. It is an actual, mechanical thing, depending upon skill and speed of automatic actions-and a certain point of view that realizes the value of getting things 81

Lovely Ladies done and out of the way to make room for more worthwhile things. Mechanical, I say-although, in order to establish routines and habits of efficiency one may have at first to do certain things consciously. Soon they become habits. 

Picture to yourself, for example, teaching a savage, however willing, to brush his or her teeth. Can you see the laborious gestures, the mental attention to every detail, that this person would have to resort to in order to learn this gesture in which you, without thinking about it at all, indulge several times a day?

One becomes efficient by dividing whatever is necessary to be done into "routine and judgment factors." Routine factors are those things which can be made a matter of habit and therefore accomplished very quickly. Judgment factors deal with those tasks which require some thoughtnew problems. A judgment factor which arises more than once can always be turned into a routine factor, if it is judged correctly the first time. And it is wise to turn everything that you possibly can into routine. Efficient

Homemaking 

IN THE home, a lovely lady must be an intelligent and efficient manager. Men talk about the modern woman and feminism 'destroying the home." It is up to us to put the home on a respected and respectable basis, to make of it an organization, neither owned by nor owning its manager. 66 The modern woman, with her ability and her intelligence, can manage anything to which she puts her mind. 82

Toiling for Leisure 

And the modern home, managed by her in all its efficiency and comfort, has a place in society that it never before occupied. For the modern home contrives to give those who live in it the maximum of comfort with the minimum of slavery for the wife and mother who manages it. 

Intelligent women realize that the success and happiness of their families depend upon them, and upon the kind of an environment they provide in the home. There is a very old Japanese saying, credited to Nichiren, to the effect that the woman is the bow and the man the arrowand upon the strength of the bow depends the length and accuracy of the flight of the arrow. Is it not true?

The home is the factory of humanity-in which the machine is fashioned and sent forth to do its work, to which it returns for the repairs and recharging that rest and comfort and food give the human body. 

But efficiency in the home does not consist of making the home into a machine. So a modern woman puts her house and her household upon a labor- and time-saving basis which gives those who are in it the most of comfort and the least strain-and does it in a way that contributes to their happiness and a general atmosphere of peace. A woman is a queen in her own home but not all queens were Catherines! Catherine ruled like a man and had to have an army to enforce her edicts. Other "lovely lady" queens ruled as women, and because they were loved they were obeyed. For love is an instinctive tribute to charm and loveliness.

Learn to do the mechanical things by schedule, making 83

Lovely Ladies slight changes when necessary. Realize that efficiency in the home does not mean endless hours of housework, which in fact indicates a lack of efficiency.

Turn to the chapter on the housewife, where you will find a schedule that you can change somewhat to suit your needs. Try it out, not once, but several times, until you go through your housework rapidly and with precision. Remember the daily routine factors of housework should be made automatic, leaving you plenty of time for the judgment factors. 

You will be surprised how easily all the little harassing things get done, and how much time you have left for yourself!

Business 

IN THE other phase of life now open to women-business-efficiency has its roots.

Many a married woman conducts her household the better for her business experience.

In business one must train one's self to minimize effort, both mental and physical, and consequently there is less strain upon one's nerves and strength, and upon the nerves and temper of one's employer.

Being well groomed in business, without fussing unduly about it, and without devoting a great deal of time to it, is but an indication of your general efficiency. The reason that a smartly clad woman gets a position in preference to any other is merely because her clothing indicates her general attitude of efficiency. Selecting one's business clothing with this in mind, and reducing the caring of it to a minimum, is being efficient. 84

Toiling for Leisure 

I have written at length on this particular aspect of modern life in the chapter on Business. There you will find suggestions as to the neatest and most economical clothing-in short, the most efficient clothing you can wear for business-as well as some other ideas that make for efficiency and poise in the business world. 

Conclusion EFFICIENCY in your work, in your home, in your life! It is the intelligent and the only means by which you can free yourself from the petty round of all that modern life requires of you and still have time for whatever else constitutes your idea of happiness in living.

But-and this is very important-do not sacrifice charm to efficiency. For efficiency without charm in a woman defeats its own purpose. Machines must be efficient or they are worthless, and that is all that is required of them. 

But women are not machines, and if a woman is efficient in a cold, mechanical way, her efficiency is antagonistic to those around her, upon whose coöperation she depends. 

Better inefficiency with laughter and charm, than efficiency at their expense! 85

Lovely Ladies 

Love, laughter, play, study, reading, art-what matter your talents or inclinations if you have not the intelligence to free yourself from just living in order to obtain them? 86

Chapter XIII PERSONALITY PERSONALITY is the active mani festation or expression of your character. 

Do not suppose for a moment that "character" means one must be what the world calls a "good person." The Borgias, certain famous queens of Egypt and Africa, other ladies of the

French court, could certainly not be called called "good" by even the most sophisticated and tolerant, yet they had personalities that have caused their names to echo to this day. 

You must definitely express what you are in order to have personality, for personality expresses the inner individual, the core which, sensed but not seen, often leaves its lasting impression on the world after the person is gone. It does this regardless of whether a person is a saint or a sinner.

Everyone has a certain amount of personality. But it 87

Lovely Ladies varies in the proportion that one has character. A person of vacillating mind or soul cannot have a very strong or interesting personality, which is the reason why young people usually lack this quality, while the woman of twenty-nine or thirty, having developed more definiteness of character and having learned the better to express it, is much more interesting and usually more popular than the débutante, despite the wearing away of the first bloom of youth.

In order to express what you are and make it interesting you must be interesting. One may be beautiful, one may be cultured as well, and yet not be interesting. 

Being 

Interesting 

How to be interesting, then! Being interested in the many fascinating sides of life and the things that human beings have done, are doing, and may do will make you interesting, make you a vital part of the life you are living. Smug people, they who brood on the edge of life, fattening in mind and body, they who do not burn like a clear white flame that cannot be quenched by circumstances or the supreme flame extinguisher, ennui, can never be interesting, cannot have personality.

Personality is a very definite thing, since it expresses a definite thing, which is you. And one can develop an interesting and pleasing personality.

A WORD first about a pleasing personality. Being interesting because you are interested in things, as I have 88

Personality already said, helps you to develop an interesting personality. But it also saves you from something else which makes for an unpleasant personality—it saves you from being self-centered and an egotist.

The Pleasing 

Personality

A one-sided interest in life, in just one phase of it, for instance, has a tendency to make one an egotist, but with wider interests comes a more philosophical outlook, a humorous appreciation of the smallness of one human being in one of the smallest of countless planets, the one called "earth." This perspective precludes the possibility of egotism, and an egotistical personality is always unpleasant. 

Yet it is quite as easy to have a pleasant personality as an unpleasant personality. Only stupid people are unpleasant, stupid and egotistical people who refuse to realize that there are other people in the world with quite as many problems and irritations as they have themselves. 

It is quite true that one can have personality without being especially pleasing to others, but no lovely lady wants to develop such a personality. It is much easier to go through life conforming whenever possible to the rules that civilization has laid down for pleasant living with one's fellow man than not to do so. But remember one thing-conform where and when it doesn't cramp, and when conforming approaches cramping, stop.

IN A further effort to understand what makes for interesting personality, let's analyze our friends. Who is and who is not interesting? 89

Lovely Ladies Analyzing Personalities 

First of all there is the girl who is undeniably sweet, pretty, well bred, and altogether lovable. There is the young man who is well groomed, courteous, well bred, even handsome, more or less witty, and possibly even a little cocksure.

Neither of these two fairly average types have interesting personalities. You are forced to assume, then, that beauty or the lack of it has little to do with personality; that good breeding and courteous manners have little to do with personality; that good intentions and kindness have little to do with personality.

Now a step further. The town character. He may have been a prospector, a pioneer, an immigrant who polished shoes for the privilege of living in a new country, or an adventurer. Let's eliminate the qualities that he has not. 

He is not handsome. Not, certainly, educated or cultured, not well groomed. Certainly not well bred and courteous. Perhaps not kindly. Not especially brilliant mentally, though not dull. He is even an old reprobate! 

But there we have something. He's sure of himselfsure to the point of stubbornness in his opinions on everything, from the fact that if the groundhog sees his shadow on groundhog day winter will be six weeks longer, to the fact that all "furriners" should be run out of the country.

And, he is witty. Perhaps in a dry, taciturn sort of way rather than in a gayly infectious way, but nevertheless he has sagacious humor.

He has personality, this one, even though it sometimes 90

Personality becomes a little tiresome. This is because he limits his interests by stubbornness. But were he more elastic, he would have a more interesting personality.

Dullness Is Out sought after! 

BUT suppose you are dull? You know it, but oh, how you would like to have personality, lots of friends, your opinion The answer is that you can't, and continue to be stupid and dull. You can have personality, though, if you start at the root of things and consciously and intelligently develop your mind and your interests.

Minds respond to exercise just as do any other muscles in the body. But a little exercise to-day, none to-morrow, and a little some other time, is worse than useless.

Reading novels and seeing movies do not in themselves constitute mental exercise. Both of them can be mental exercise, though, if you use them to provoke analytical thought.

Constructive 

Thinking 

CONSCIOUS, Constructive thinking, not just daydreaming, wool-gathering, wandering in the Elysian Fields of desire and imagination, is mental exercise. Understand, I am not now speaking to the mentally overworked type of person who must learn and practise this very wandering, but to the woman who has forgotten how to exercise the muscles of the brain and wonders why she is being left behind by friends, husband, and children, by a hurrying and eager life that is ever moving, ever on the way to do and be. 91

Lovely Ladies 

Did you never wonder why some children are so bright?

They have no experience, we guide them in the ways of living, but they are quick, eager to question and to learn. 

Often we must reprimand them and tell them that they are impertinent in order to hide our own slowness or dullness! The reason is that they are learning, are exercising the muscles of their minds. These, by the way, are the children who are on the way to developing interesting personalities. 

How About 

You?

WHAT have you done with the muscles of your mind in the last ten years?

You have read magazines, perhaps even several novels, hurriedly, probably not understanding them, but reading them because it seemed the thing to do since everyone was talking about them. You have been a "good wife and mother." (So you say, and perhaps think.) 

And what? Good heavens, that's about all! Bridge, you insist, is a mind game. Right, it is, and you are at least one mental step ahead of your sisters who do not play bridge. 

You have read of, if not seen, the Hindu fakirs sitting on the roads and highways of India that lead one and all to Nirvana and the Himalayas, with an arm raised above their heads suspended in air, and totally withered, resembling an old dried limb of a tree, just a skeleton thing with the outline of the bone covered with brown, dry flesh.

How did this happen? In just one word-disuse. In this 92

Personality instance, calculated, determined disuse, but had the arm been tied and immobilized by force the result would have been exactly the same. Muscles

Deteriorate 

YOUR brain is like a muscle. What can you expect of any muscle if you do not use it? 

That's the only reason why the bright boy or girl who wins all of the contests and takes all of the honors at school so often ends up selling newspapers while the corner newsboy ends up a "steel maggot." They both have a brain, only one exercised his and the other didn't. 

Any kind of constructive study is mental exercise. But it must be constructive study. Exercise for your muscles violently indulged in for an hour or so one day and then not again for a week achieves less result than does an exercise conscientiously done for five minutes every day.

Once not so long ago a man found this interesting enough to experiment with. For five minutes a day he slowly and with great force and determination exercised the muscles of one arm. Twice a week he violently exercised the muscles of the other arm. The result was that the five minutesa-day arm developed smoothly and beautifully, while the violently exercised arm wavered between active soreness and flabby despair—and kept on doing it!

Developing Intelligence 

REMEMBER, I am speaking to you of mental development because you cannot have an interesting personality without intelligence and because anyone can develop his or her brain. But I do want you to understand that this development has to 93

Lovely Ladies be a determined and regular development-and whether it consists of merely adding columns of figures for five minutes every day, or studying a new language, or trying to comprehend the philosophers, it will benefit you in the long run if you persist in it. 

Wisdom of Restraint

DEVELOP your mind, enlarge the scope of your interests, and then have the wisdom of restraint. Personality need not be insisted upon to others. A quiet assurance, a spiritual strength and intelligence, gives one a very interesting type of personality that does not have to depend upon verbal evidence to exist. "I don't know whether she is profound or just dumb,"

I once heard. The person is an enigma who speaks very seldom, seems always to be either taking in everything and everybody and digesting them for his own reflection, or to be moving in a delightful half world that is neither here nor there, in which the habits, customs, places, and petty irritations of this world are of absolutely no consequence.

Ah, but the difference between one who is profound and one who is dumb! The person who really puzzles others and provokes them to speculation and conversation has personality. The quiet yet very assured person with intelligence can never be mistaken for "dumb" by intelligent and discerning people, because intelligence lights up the expression and the eyes, and the lack of it is the most noticeable thing about a man's or woman's face. 94

Personality Conclusion 

LEARN to restrain the manifestation of personality, to withdraw from people who do not understand or appreciate you, and keep this precious core of yourself ready for those who do. 

Speaking of people not understanding you, never gauge your personality by the opinions of those around you. For there are degrees of personality, and some of them cannot possibly be appreciated by certain people. Always consider the possibility that the people in your immediate circle do not quite understand your particular type of personality. Nor let this lead to an attempt on your part to change it. Any personality that can be altered by the opinion of others is not real personality.

The world is a big place. People and their little opinions are not so very important. You can safely develop your own personality, and although not always understood by people around you, nevertheless you may be not disliked by them.

Try to give people their due share of consideration in your life and no more. "What do I care what they think!" 

Don't swagger when you say this, then shed tears in the privacy of your boudoir, but develop the understanding and spiritual strength that will make you believe it yourself. It is far better to have a personality that is deeply loved by a few and heartily disliked by a few others than one that is superficially liked by all. 

Develop your mind, widen your interests, and know how to express your character, then restrain it. Mental development, spiritual strength and definiteness of char- 95

Lovely Ladies acter, and a great deal of humorous understanding and tolerance for those around you will help you to the interesting personality that is part and parcel of a lovely lady. 96

Chapter XIV INDIVIDUALITY 

INDIVIDUALITY is closely allied to personality, because a woman with sufficient courage to be individual, and sufficient mentality to be individual at no sacrifice to the accepted rules of good taste-in other words, who has the intelligence to be individual without being bizarrecannot but have personality.

One is individual in exactly the degree that one has the courage to deviate in character from the mob. And in order to have the character strength to do this you must, as explained in the chapter on Personality, definitely express what you are. This definiteness of character is not stubbornness, and it does not mean being hard boiled or aggressive. Stubbornness always indicates a certain stupidity, and for a woman to be hard boiled or aggressive robs her of loveliness.

The intelligent realization of what you wish to be and have a realization reached by conscious thinking and reasoning-then the direct march toward them, regardless of anything or anyone's opinion (trying not to hurt, of course, but with the courage to hurt when hurting is inevitable) are the first steps toward individuality and, inevitably, personality. 97

Lovely Ladies 

Courage 

IT TAKES Courage to be individual. Spiri tual strength does not come in a day.

Individuality is always criticized by people who conform to pattern, and it is difficult to acquire the point of view that brings with it the conviction that the opinions of others are not worth fearing. Quietly establish your right to individuality. Spend no time talking about it, no time trying to convince others of your privilege to be an individual, but do as thinking and intelligence dictate. 

Dare to be free, dare to act according to your lights, dare to live according to your ideas of harmony and beauty. 

COULD I only impress upon you the value

Be Individual of being individual! To be honestly and truly yourself, your nice self, as heredity and environment and conscious determination and thinking have made you, to be free from all of the soul-cramping shackles of outworn conventionality, to have the courage to live and do and be according to the dictates of your own intelligence-what a truly worthwhile ambition!

Individuality 

That You See

AND now let us look at the appearances of individuality.

Individuality is made evident by outward appearances as well as by the personality of a person, and it is very well to have individuality of appearance as well as mental and spiritual individuality. Be individual in your clothing, your coiffure, your jewelry. Study your 98

Individuality type and emphasize it. The day is past when this or that "was the latest mode" and women flocked to it as cats to catnip. The past two years have seen remarkable strides away from the shingled, marceled, cigarette-smoking, pearl-draped, hipless, and bosomless female who made a simultaneous appearance in Paris, America, China, and Russia. There will always be general trends, yes, in ideas, in silhouettes, in coiffures. But with what latitude for individuality!

Understanding and dressing with regard to your general type, the knowledge based upon personal coloring and figure, will lend you a certain air of individuality. But how can one be a type and be individual? I can hear you ask.

One can, because the danger to individuality was in the universal type-the international bobs, pearls, styles that insisted upon being the mode and were resorted to by the softly fluffy, sweet-faced blonde, or a titian-haired beauty with long green eyes, or a robust brunette with dark hair and brilliant complexion. That is the danger to individuality!

But knowing the certain colors, lines in clothing, coiffures, jewelry, and gestures synonymous with one's personal type and color will often make one distinctly individual and sometimes even a beauty. 

So closely are personal coloring and figure allied with certain points of view, tastes, and gestures that it is not an affectation to develop the latter. Whether your general atmosphere is enhanced with laces, ruffles, and wispy 99

Lovely Ladies chiffons, or the severe tailored variety of things, or exotic colors, lines, and daring jewelry, is something every woman should at a very early age decide upon. And there is such a wide choice of all of these things to-day that it is no longer necessary for the girl who is perfect in out-of-door clothes to be uncomfortable in evening clothes, nor for the person who is striking in exotic pajamas and extreme evening things to be lacking in lustre on the golf links. 

Your first step toward individuality is knowing your type and dressing to it. The very mental freedom that this affords will give you as well courage to be individual in other ways. 

What, if not the desire to be "different," has caused women for centuries to spend hours and money pursuing rumors of the latest style? They wanted to dress differently, think differently, or have a haircut that differed from their sisters, but being timid creatures they wanted it to be sanctioned by the mode, to know that it would be the style and that the others perforce would follow in due time.

With individuality of such importance in an age of standardized tailor-made living, the number of women who fall so far short of it is astonishing. The reason is fear. 

And one must have the strength of character not only to be free, but to cause others to acknowledge the right to this freedom. You are bound to encounter opposition from others if you deviate in character from the mob, yet if this deviation is not an unreasonable or unpleasant one, you can make others accept it as your right. 100

Individuality Exercise Restraint 

BUT to attempt to be individual at the sacrifice of one's knowledge of the fitness of things can be nothing but stupid. Such individuality is forced, artificial, and incongruous. It is never necessary to be individual at the expense of accepted good taste. Certain tenets of good taste, certain rules and ideas for behavior, are based upon the exigencies of civilization, and people who have the intelligence and courage to be individual should know that these things cannot be ignored in the name of individuality. 

One can deviate from the accepted just as far as good taste permits and no further. Never allow yourself to be rude in the name of individuality or eccentricity. "Don't mind me, that's just my way."

Evidently. But the point is, Dare why is it just your way, and why should you think that the mere admission of the fact that you are rude or gauche excuses it?

MASQUERADING as eccentricity or individuality, rudeness has become a fad among the younger generation and the ΙΟΙ

Lovely Ladies A Modern Menace so-called intelligentsia of to-day. Any breach of manners, any lack of them, any abuse of them, is supposedly condoned if the individual has twisted spoonfuls of mud into the semblance of a pipe dream or a handful of words into a poem.

There are perhaps a handful of men and women in the world to-day whose genius makes it almost possible to overlook the fact that they are otherwise savage and barbaric. 

It is natural that their imitators, unable to achieve their mental brilliancy, should content themselves with aping their faults, overlooking entirely the fact that such genius exists in spite of and not because of this cultural degeneracy. Popular 

Rudeness 

THERE are certain very popular forms of rudeness in which women indulge more often than men, which they foolishly seem to think lends them some sort of distinction or individuality. One is an undue sophistication that makes everyone else feel uncomfortable, another is an exaggerated reserve which, seeming to be "snobbishness," is usually either shyness and lack of poise, or an exaggerated ego shielding self behind taciturnity.

A certain reserve is very desirable, but it can be carried too far. Shyness, while it does not offend, is an apathetic admission of a lack of culture, and indicates that one is without the poise and ease of manners which spring from a cultural background, and lacks as well the ambition to make the necessary effort to acquire this culture. 102

Individuality 

Taciturnity is the indication not only of a lack of cultural background, but of an egotism that will not disturb itself with what it calls "affectations"-meaning culture and good manners, and above all kindliness and appreciation.

Shyness is ineffectual, but taciturnity is rude. And rudeness is a relic of uncivilized days that has no excuse for existing.

Reserve 

RESERVE that is courteous and kindly is The Place of desirable and often gives one a definite personality. But reserve that indicates either shyness or egotism has no place in the make-up of a charming woman.

Sophistication is supposed to be an indication of culture, but since culture in itself is based upon kindliness it can no longer be truly called sophistication when a woman's sophistication makes everyone around her uncomfortable. "Snobbishness" has never been an indication of good breeding but invariably an indication of the lack of it. 

Don't be unpleasantly reserved to people-you can shelter your precious ego behind quietness, you know, behind a well-bred reserve, without making people ill at ease.

The exaggeration of individuality is eccentricity. And, as I have said before, it is not necessary to make individuality obvious. Eccentricities of dress, of speech, of ideas are usually incongruous and inharmonious. Don't, in an effort to be individual, cross this very fine line between 103

Lovely Ladies individuality and eccentricity. One can be slightly eccentric, of course, if this eccentricity is within the bounds of good taste. If it makes you conspicuous, then it is not. 

Develop individuality, both character individuality and its outward appearances. Lovely ladies are never patterned!

TO4

Chapter XV THIS POPULARITY 

Was there ever a book for women, especially young women, that did not have its section or chapter on popularity? 

Advertisements laud the pop ular man, woman, girl, or boy and condole with the "wallflower," the while offering the cure for unpopularity. Never once have I seen or heard that popularity might have its own pitfalls. The philosophers alone have dared to extol the joys of solitude, even though, to any thinking person, it is evident that solitude is the only opportunity one has to know one's self, and knowing, to create from one's understanding.

Now any lovely lady is popular. Quite as popular as she wishes to be. More often than not more popular than she wishes to be, necessitating a real effort on her part to keep from hurting people she does not want to hurt, in order to maintain solitude. 105

Lovely Ladies 

There is your answer to the question, "How can I be popular?" Be a lovely lady as she is interpreted in this book!

It's a Point of View 

BUT in order to be more explicit, I must ask first the question, "What do you construe as popularity?" If popularity to you means having hordes of people around you, regardless of their worth as individuals, regardless of their interests or interesting personality, then, as one long-suffering husband of a parvenue once remarked-"All you have to do is feed 'em!" 

If, lulled by a false conception of popularity into thinking that you are a very, very popular woman merely because your home is ever cluttered with people, there's your recipe for that kind of popularity-feed 'em!

If, however, your sense of humor and of harmonious spiritual balance shows you the stupidity of such a course, and you would truly like to know how to gather about you a few interesting and worthwhile friends, then read further.

PARADOXICAL as it may sound, aloneness is 

Know Thyself the first thing which you must understand and indulge in, in order to acquire the personality which will result in making you popular.

Aloneness is a word of my own-nor does it mean solitude. 

Why are human beings so afraid of being alone? In primitive times there were reasons why people should not be alone, but no longer is it necessary that we band 106

This Popularity together against hostile tribes, or against Siva, the God of 

Darkness and of Fear. 

To-day aloneness is not only something not to be feared, but something to be sought. Yet thoughtless men and women seek mere physical nearness to people in order to avoid the necessity of coming face to face with their own egos!

Try to realize that this is the day of exploring egos. 

Knowing that nothing man produces or creates is due to chance, luck, the generosity of a philanthropically inclined god or momentarily propitiated gods, and that everything man creates or achieves comes from himself, you must realize that only alone with yourself can you plumb the depths of your imagination and understanding, can you bring forth, from the unknown facets of your conscious and subconscious mind, the memories that can be pieced together to make a composite result which you can use for your own needs.

What Are 

Your Interests? 

WHAT are you interested in? Being vitally interested in anything indicates a mental alertness that cannot fail to appeal to others who are mentally alert, and attract them to you. 

But there are pitfalls here! For one has to be more interested in really interesting things than in personal events; the boss, the wife, Cousin Carrie's marriage to a ne'erdo-well, this-was-the-house-I-lived-in-at-such-and-such-atime, et cetera.

Be careful-it is so easy to be a bore! And being a bore 107

Lovely Ladies your company will be shunned as the plague. Quickly ask yourself, are you a bore? Do you project your personal and only-interesting-for-you affairs on your friends? That may be the very reason why you are unpopular. 

Not, understand me, that one must simulate interest, pretending an interest in your friends' affairs that you do not feel. But among congenial and not intellectually limited minds, the only kind of minds that belong to the kind of people you want around you, there is no reason for any person to pretend interest, because each of you can find something mutually interesting and thus enjoy one another's company. 

Remember, no other type of friend, no popularity with other than interesting people, is worthwhile. Far better to turn to books and things and places. Hit-or-miss camaraderie is useless popularity. Friends who fade away at the first breath of misfortune are certainly not worth wasting any time over, unless possibly they amuse you for the time being—and while permitting them to do that be sure to waste nothing more on them. 

You Are Your Masterpiece 

AND now here is a thought. Although a rose by any other name would surely smell as sweet, it would not look as lovely in a recently opened tomato can as it would in a slender 

Sèvres vase. Could women but realize the value of an appropriate background! A lovely woman must have background, just as jewels must have settings, flowers have vases, and paintings have frames. 108

This Popularity 

'Tis true that a Demosthenes or a Mark Twain might have held court in a bathtub or in a treetop, but after all, the average lovely lady is not quite comparable to them. 

And though a background alone is not sufficient to assure one of worthwhile or enduring friends, how it helps even the most interesting personality! The quietness or comfort or charm or beauty of a certain home in which dwells a congenial friend-we would be surprised to realize how often they beckon us to that friend. 

Every woman should create a frame for the masterpiece that is herself. It is not a matter of money but of good taste. 

This frame can be one room or ten, but if it expresses you, is definitely yours and belongs to you, it will strengthen your position with others and with yourself. Your background, quite as much as yourself, will contribute to your popularity. Create an atmosphere into which others will desire to enter. 

Sincerity Is Attractive

AND then, in the recipe for popularity, last, but not at all least, is sincerity. Why does it so often happen that a person who seems so very interesting, even compellingly fascinating upon first sight, begins to wear thin after the first few meetings, like a popular melody which loses its charm by repetition? While a less fascinating person may, as the saying is, grow upon one? The answer is obvious-that one is sincere even in his apparent dullness, the other is either a poseur in his brilliancy or else lacking in the spiritual depth which inspires an enduring affection. 109

Lovely Ladies 

Be sincere in whatever you are or do or are interested in, and this sincerity will attract to you people of the same temperament.

Conclusion 

You should not consider a lack of popularity in the light of defeat—actually, rather, it may be only a lack of appreciation on the part of those around you. Be very sure, though, before you are convinced that nobody understands you, that nobody appreciates you, and that everybody else is stupid but yourself, that you are not overestimating yourself. Personal analysis is so awfully important! 

Popularity is one thing that a person can achieve only by being interested in the means and not the end. If you broaden and enrich your own personality and interest and exercise a wise restraint in discussing these interests with your friends, remembering that these things are only interesting to others if they stand by themselves as interesting things that do not scratch too deeply the surfaces of others' sensibilities, you will be as popular as only an interesting, lovely lady can be popular. 

Popularity will come to you when you have something of interest to offer those whom you wish to attract. And before you can become an interesting personality, you must learn to deny the atavistic urge that causes you to fear to seek the company of your own self or of books. 

Be interesting, sincere, and lovely, and you will be popular.
