BOOK II BEING the revelation of the inner loveliness of lovely ladies as outwardly portrayed by their varied types of color, form, and feature -with the unfolding of the secrets each must know, and the rites each must practice to achieve her own perfection.

Chapter I 

COLOR KNOWLEDGE 

Too little is known about color. Technical as the subject is, we all recognize it as being invaluable to the woman who takes the slightest interest in her appearance. 

Imagine, if you can, a world without color, funereal and forbidding. To many people the world about us in its various and varying tints and shades of color is really colorless. They are so accustomed to color that they pay no attention to it. And that is of the first importance. We must watch color, we must notice color, if we are to learn anything about it. 113

Lovely Ladies 

Color will either be slave or master. It can help you to express yourself; it can enhance your natural coloring; it can furnish an attractive and flattering background for you, or it can do the opposite of all these things. I am going to give you in this chapter the perfectly natural, simple laws of color, and a simple, easily understood way for you to use the knowledge.

First we must understand what color is and learn important things about its values without applying them personally. Later we will analyze our own coloring, the reasons for it, and then find out how to put colors and our personal coloring together.

Rudimentary Color 

Knowledge 

PERHAPS you are saying to yourself, "Yes, this may be interesting, but I don't need to know it. I pick the correct colors for myself naturally and instinctively." Perhaps so. 

But there are laws in color as there are laws in chemistry —definite, rigid laws which are very important to know. 

Then What Is Color? 

I THINK I can best start by explaining to you simply what color is, not technically, of course, but in a way that will be the most easily understood. Color is a vibration. To help you to understand, let us compare each color with a note of music. As a matter of fact, color is but a higher vibration than music, and persons who have trained themselves to do so can actually distinguish the color of each note of music. 

Do you know, for instance, that there is such a thing as a 114

Color Knowledge color organ, an instrument which combines and shifts colors, using each distinctive tint or shade as a note? 

A note of music is a vibration of sound which, striking upon your eardrum, is from there transmitted to the brain, where it registers and enables you to know that you have heard a sound. Now think of color as a vibration which is exactly like a note of music, but strikes on the retina of your eye instead of on your eardrum, and is then transferred from your eye to your brain in the form of color, instead of from your ear to your brain in the form of sound, as is a note of music.

It is not necessary for me to beg you to believe that color is a vibration. The thing I would like you to do is to realize what color, being a vibration, means. 

Now for the

Colors 

EVERY color known, excepting three, can be produced from a mixture of colors. 

These three are called the primary colors. 

The combinations of two primary colors are called secondary colors.

Red, yellow, and blue are the primary colors. (The authenticity of this is disputed by certain chemists, but for the purpose of this book we had best not go into that detail.) The secondary colors are orange, green, and violet. 

So that you can better understand what I mean by secondary colors, imagine two notes of music struck on the piano at the same time; although there are two notes, you hear only one sound. Thus a secondary color is one color, even though it is composed of two primary colors. 115

Lovely Ladies There Remains 

THE remaining colors we have are called the tertiary colors. Here color knowledge gets a bit vague and involved. Let me try to simplify it for you, bearing in mind that this is not a technical color lesson and that I am therefore entitled to some latitude in my effort to simplify the explanation.

The so-called tertiary colors are brown and gray. 

Brown is simple to explain-it is the combination of the three primary colors. Yet it can only be produced when combining these three in a transparent shade. The moment white, or opacity, is added, or should any of these primary colors be opaque-meaning mixed with white, which renders them opaque-you will have gray. 

Here's where it gets involved. Although gray is a tertiary color, and is made by combining the three primary colors, exactly as is brown, it is not brown. Why? Because two different effects of color are achieved by the difference between transparency and opacity. Try to think of brown as being produced by the three primary colors when they are all transparent, and gray as being produced by the three primary colors when they are all opaque. (You do not get a true gray this way, of course, nor a true brown, but the reasons for this are too involved to explain here.) 

Black and

White 

LAST of all are black and white, which, as we have said before, are technically not considered colors.

Black is the total absence of all colors. 

White is the luminous combination of all colors. 116

Color Knowledge 

It is true that you can produce black by mixing all colors (if there is no opaque white in them), but black is still the total absence of all color, inasmuch as the mixing of them in heavy proportions neutralizes each single one and results in no color.

All colors are in black, but cannot be seen because it is not illuminated. Illuminate black and you have white, the luminous combinations of all colors. 

A ray of sunlight is apparently colorless, though it is certainly luminous. Yet it contains all the colors. When a ray of sunlight is broken up by means of a prism, each side of the prism catches one color and, separating it from the rest, refracts it. You have seen a ray of light strike a crystal chandelier, and all of the different colors reflected on the wall from this? The ray of light is colorless, and the crystal is colorless-then from whence came those colors you saw on the wall?

They were refracted out of the ray of sunlight through the prism-shaped crystals of the chandelier.

Now gray, a true gray, is produced by That Inexpli- cable Gray! mixing black and white. Gray actually is black lightened with white or white darkened by black, shades (not tones) of gray depending upon the amount of one or the other. 

Yet, we are seeing light, for in black you have all colors, unlightened, or unilluminated and "all colors" can mean only the three primary colors. Therefore, gray must be a tertiary color, a mixture of the three primary colors. 117

Lovely Ladies The Dah-Ray Color Idea 

THE artist's conception of color differs from that of the scientist and the chemist. 

I am going to presume to give you my color theory, which is artistic and insists upon having you visualize color as a cycle rather than as a progressive thing starting "here" and ending "there." That makes it simple to understand the intricacies of black and white, the apparent contradictions in gray, the reason why gray and brown are both produced by the three primary colors. 

Just think of a circle, or cycle. Black is white, and white is black, but they are different phases of one another. (Illumination.) All colors make black and all colors make white. Yet all colors make no color (black) because they neutralize one another. And all colors make what appears to be no color in white, for the same reason. 

Jewel and

Pastel Tones with color. 

You must understand one more thing, and that is what is meant by "transparency" and "opacity" and their connection

Think of two glasses of water. Now mentally pour powdered chalk into one glass, enough to make it impossible to see through the water. One glass will be transparent, and the other, the one with the chalk, will be opaque. The opaque glass transmits less light than the transparent one. Buttermilk is opaque, water is transparent.

Now we will take some pure red color pigment (which is the powder from which liquid color is made) and mix 118

Color Knowledge some of it in each glass of water. In one glass you will have transparent red, and in the other opaque red. The transparent red will be what is popularly known as a "jewel tone" because it is clear and jewel-like, and the opaque red will be what is called "pastel" because it is like a pastel crayon, or chalky.

Or imagine a sheet of red glass. No matter how many more sheets of red glass you put behind this one, you will always have a transparent red. But if you put a sheet of thick white cardboard behind one of these you will at once have an opaque red.

A Shade of a Color 

You understand now that color is a vibration affecting your eye and that each different color is a different vibration, even as music is a vibration affecting your ear, each note a different rate of vibration. Strike one note on the piano, and strike it very hard. Then strike this same note again and strike it with less force. Now strike it very lightly. You have produced different sounds, all of the same note. 

Really, different shades of sound have been produced from the same note.

A shade of a color is exactly the same thing. It is not another color, it is simply the same color, but of a different degree of intensity. It is lightened or darkened, transparently or opaquely, by more or less of its own color pigment. 

Suppose you have ten little dishes of water, and in the first you pour all of the red pigment it can possibly hold, so much indeed that the water finally disappears and you 119

Lovely Ladies have nothing left but damp red pigment again. Then in each of the other dishes you put a little less pigment until finally the last one has hardly any pigment in it at all. 

You have then ten different shades of red, one in each little dish, and all transparent reds. Repeat the same thing with ten little dishes containing powdered chalk and water, to which you add your red pigment, and you have ten different shades of red again, but pastel shades. Each shade has a different rate of vibration, varying with the amount of pigment you have used.

Tones Are Another Matter 

THERE is another thing-tones. A tone is neither a color nor a shade.

The combination of red and yellow, for instance, makes orange. If that orange is simply a bright orange, it is a shade of orange; but if it is a reddish orange, because there is more red in it than yellow, then it is an orange of a reddish tone.

Thus any color, and any shade of any color, may have a tone. It is easier to use gray as an example, however. 

Gray is either a soft rosy gray, or a cold metallic gray, or a dull slate gray, or a gray that verges slightly on cream or brown. Why? It is still gray, isn't it? And it is the shade of gray it is, either dark, light, or medium gray, according to the amount of white or black in it. Then whence the differences I have just mentioned? These differences are in the tone of the gray; in the rosy gray is a predominance of red, in the metallic gray a predominance of blue, in the creamy gray a predominance of yellow. 120

Color Knowledge 

A primary color cannot actually have a tone, but all the other colors can.

Résumé 

THERE you have all that, for our purpose, you need to know about colors, shades, and tones. I am going to illustrate this a little more personally in the next chapter.

Above all you must be color conscious. Color is important. It can heal and it can destroy. A hundred volts of electricity may not kill one man, and may indeed act as a stimulant to him, while the same hundred volts would kill another man instantly. Thus the vibration of certain colors is fatal to certain disease germs, while the same vibration assists other disease germs to grow. After one understands what color is it is not difficult to believe this and understand the effect it has upon the mind, the emotions, the personality, and the health.

Color knowledge, masquerading as it does under various names, has the aspect of a fad, which is unfortunate, as it confuses many people and causes them to neglect the study of something that is unquestionably of importance. 

Please turn to the back of the book and see the Dah-Ray Color Chart, which will simplify for you the color information you have just studied. 

Observe the chart. I have indicated the outer fringe of a circle in a way to give you the impression of light, luminosity, or radiance (white). In the very center of the circle you see a spot of darkness (black). There is one triangle with black spots indicating three corners (primary colors), 121

Lovely Ladies another triangle with little lines indicating three other corners (secondary colors), and shaded lines between the black and the colors, which indicate brown. 

To explain: Starting with the center of this chart, we have black, which is the total absence of all color. And yet we know that all colors together make black, since all colors together neutralize each other. We cannot see them because they are not illuminated. 

Now light begins to illuminate this black circle. This makes brown, a combination of all colors which differs from black in that there is illumination there, so that it is possible for us to see brown.

In the triangle indicated by black spots at its corners each spot points to one of the primary colors, red, blue, or yellow.

Next, there is the triangle with the little lines at each corner. The corners point to the secondary colors, violet, orange, and green, in their proper place between each primary color.

Then come the shaded lines, indicating gray, because all the colors when illuminated by white make gray. 

White, around the outer fringe of the colors, is pure radiance, or luminosity, and contains all the colors. 

Could this chart be in colors I should show you tones as well. For example, between the red and yellow point, each indicated by a black spot, there is orange. But you must understand that the nearer to the red the orange is, the redder tone it has. And that the nearer to the yellow the orange is, the more yellowish tone it has. 122

Color Knowledge 

Between the black spots that indicate yellow and blue is the secondary color-green. As this green comes nearer to the blue you have a bluish green. As it comes nearer the yellow you have a yellowish green.

The same applies to gray and to the brown which encircles the black spot. Where this circle is nearest the red the brown is reddish, richer, and more decidedly brown. 

Where it is nearest the orange it is more orange. Where it is nearest the yellow it is more golden. Where it is nearest the green it is more olive. Where it is nearest the blue it is darker and colder, more black, really.

This is nature's color scheme. 123

Chapter II COLORS AND YOU 

WITH less knowledge of color than this it is not possible for you to go unhampered through so colorful a world. And now, just where and how shall we use color in our daily life? 

Don't you think that it is logical, since your life revolves around you, to begin with yourself? You see, you also have color in yourself-in your eyes, hair, and skin. These colors you are constantly presenting to the world, and you cannot possibly avoid doing so. 

Nor should you. You have heard of and seen people to whom the adjective "colorless" has been applied. They are in fact and in personality colorless. If they knew about color what you are going to know, they would not only be colorful (and, what is more, correctly colorful) to others, but to themselves as 124.

Colors and You well. And since there are actual physical and chemical reasons for your coloring, your particular coloring is indeed an indication of your personality.

The Three Aspects of Color 

RIGHT here I want to impress upon you the fact that there are three aspects to the use of color in your life. 

A color, when combined with your own personal coloring, can be entirely neutralizing and have neither any harmful nor any beneficial effect upon you or the people who see the color in connection with you. 

Also, a color used in combination with your personal coloring can be actually a shrieking discord and have a very harmful effect upon you and upon everyone who sees you and this color together. But, used correctly, a color can enhance your own natural coloring and have a very beneficial effect upon you and upon everyone else. 

There are times when the first aspect of color is desirable, and the last is desirable at all times, but the second, never. 

WHAT type are you? Your type is based

Types upon the coloring of your hair, of your eyes, and of your skin. Of course, it is impossible to put all women into rigid "type" classifications, but in a general way I have simplified this seemingly insoluble problem. There are blondes, there are brunettes, and then the so-called "red-heads," and lovely white-haired women.

But there are also shades in each of these classifications, as there are shades of the seven colors. 125

Lovely Ladies 

For an example you will easily understand, picture the vivid brunette type which is the type of coloring with brown hair, brown eyes, and rosy cheeks and lips. One woman will have black hair-intensely black hairswarthy skin, dark eyes, vivid red cheeks and lips. Another woman will have chestnut brown hair, brown eyes, and softly colored rosy cheeks and lips. They are the same type; but they are decidedly different shades of that type.

You are a certain type. The colors that are becoming to that type are becoming to you, and the colors that are not becoming to that type are not becoming to you. But you are to select the shades of your becoming colors according to the shade of your type. The two shades of the vivid brunette type that I gave you above as an example would each wear a different shade of red. The dark, vivid red that the first one should wear would absolutely snuff out the other one!

Type Classifi cations and

Color Combinations 

I AM going to give you the type classifications, and the colors that go with each type; and then in another place I shall give special hints that allow for personal exceptions from these types. 

Ascertain your type from the color of your hair, eyes, and skin, and whether or not you have coloring in your cheeks. Naturally we have color in our lips; if not, we apply it. And whether or not the color in your cheeks is natural or applied makes not the slightest difference, so long as you are consistent about the rouge you use. There 126

Colors and You is a difference between a white, colorless complexion, and an olive, colorless complexion, and either of them with artificial coloring in the cheeks, and this difference will determine the becomingness of colors to be worn. 

There are six brunette types and five blonde types. We are not going to try to consider the numerous shades of each type in the following type charts, because the color information in these charts is the same for every shade of the type. You must judge for yourself what shade of the type you are and select the shades in these colors correspondingly. Here are the names of the types, which practically describe themselves. Brunette

This type must take particular care to avoid colors that have a sallowing effect upon the skin. A warm, creamy complexion, like ivory, can be very lovely, but a sallow complexion can never be.

Proper shades are of paramount importance for this type. The deep, rich, dark shades of the following colors are not only becoming, but suitable to the personality as well. It should not be difficult to see at once that a pastel shade would be unbecoming to darkish skin, and that a too 130

Brunettes vivid shade would be inconsistent with this type of personality and quite drown it. For this type must depend more upon its personality than upon its actual beauty of coloring.

RED: 

Very becoming in the deep, rich, wine and rose shades, but decidedly unbecoming in any shades of pink.

ORANGE: Not becoming, except in very soft burntorange shades bordering on henna.

YELLOW: Decidedly unbecoming to this type, as well as to any type with dark, colorless skin.

The discreet use of rouge is always flattering to any type with dark skin and no coloring in the cheeks. But remember that if you use rouge in your cheeks you are not the Madonna Brunette type, and the color suggestions above do not apply. If you use rouge to simulate color in your cheeks you belong to the Vivid Brunette type which follows. 

The Vivid Brunette 

MANY poets down the ages have sung this type as the most beautiful example of natural feminine pulchritude, although the majority give preference to the auburn and titianhaired. Be that as it may, the Vivid Brunette type is undeniably attractive.

This type has dark hair and eyes, and may have either 132

Brunettes fair or olive skin, but with a decided coloring in the cheeks and lips. You can see that either one of the other two brunette types we have analyzed could make themselves this type were they to simulate coloring in the cheeks. 

For it is coloring in the cheeks that distinguishes this type rather than the actual color of the skin. 

For the Vivid Brunette type the shades of their colors must depend upon the fairness of their skins and the intensity of coloring and vivacity. For example, a person with soft brown hair, hazel eyes, and rose-pink cheeks, who belongs to one of the shades of the Vivid Brunette type, cannot wear as intense shades of red as can another shade of this type who has black hair, olive skin, and carmine cheeks. The Vivid Brunette, too, must be careful not to wear shades and colors that make her outstanding to a degree that borders on loudness. To remain within the bounds of good taste, this type should use only the simplest colors and softest shades.

RED: 

Extremely becoming in all shades, governed by the intensity of coloring and vivacity of the wearer. Pink and rose are shades of red, of course. 

ORANGE: Becoming to this type if it does not have a sallowing effect upon the complexion, and only in shades that are not too vivid.

Brunettes The Demi- 

Brunette 

THIS type has been called the true American type, because of the mixture of coloring and consequent mixture of personality. 

The general idea is, I believe, that it shows a mixture of foreign bloods, the result of which we are pleased to call American. 

This type has dark hair and light eyes. By "light eyes"

I mean gray, green, or blue eyes, not light brown eyes. 

For those "intermediate" eyes that can be made to appear either brown, gray, green, or blue according to the color one wears, we have some special suggestions later on. 

The Demi-Brunette type has hair decidedly brown enough to be called dark, and eyes light enough to be called light. 

This type shows to particular advantage when the skin is very fair and there is no coloring in the cheeks, because this lack of coloring accentuates the eyes and makes them the more interesting in contrast to the dark hair. Of course, if you have some natural color in your cheeks it is not to be scorned, but if you have not I would not suggest that you use rouge. However, it your skin is very dark, it may always be flattered by the use of rouge.

Since the particular beauty of the Demi-Brunette is in the contrast between her dark hair and her light eyes, more than any other type she should wear colors which deepen and accentuate the beauty of the eyes, even though it be quite true that this type can wear almost any color. 

First of all, select colors that flatter the eyes; then look for those that, if they don't actually flatter the complexion, at least are not actually unbecoming to it. This type is 135

Chapter IV AND THE BLONDES 

Now suppose we analyze the three definite types of blondes. It is quite true that they must, if such a thing is possible, be more careful in the colors that they wear than are some of their brunette sisters. They must be particularly careful in selecting the shades of these colors, for their very blondness gives them an ethereal, spirituelle personality that they must take care to preserve. 

What exact combination of coloring makes a blonde?

First of all, any hair lighter than light brown can be called blond hair, unless of course it is white, titian, or auburn. 

There are several distinct shades in blond hair, ranging from the vivid, golden-blond hair, to the soft, darkishblond that is just a shade lighter than light brown.

Many blondes find their hair darkening as they grow cutting and weather conditions may also alter the color. 

This type will find it an advantage to try to keep the hair light, even by artificial means. The same is true of hair which is undecided between darkish blond and brown. older;

Generally speaking, blondes must select the shades of their becoming colors according to the shade of the hair, deepening and softening the colors a bit as the hair grows darker. 138

The true blond types have blue, gray, green, or violet eyes, light, golden-colored hair, and fair skin, usually colorless. It is greatly to the advantage of a blonde to maintain this fair skin at all costs, and it is generally best not to use rouge to simulate natural coloring, unless very, very lightly, although at times a tan or sallow complexion is flattered by rouge. 

There is another blond type with golden hair and brown eyes. This is given some special consideration later. This type also is at its best if the skin is fair, and a special effort should be made to keep it so. 

The personality of a blonde is soft, sweet, spirituelle, in keeping with her coloring. Very vivacious blondes usually have decidedly golden hair and vivid blue eyes. This is not strange when one understands that the chemical makeup of the body determines the coloring as well as the personality. Regardless of the possible becomingness of certain colors found in the first half of the spectrum, blondes should affect those found in the second half. 

The Golden 

Blonde 

THIS type of blonde has fair skin, blue, gray, violet, or green eyes, and decidedly golden hair. She must select her shades in the following colors, according to her shade of hair, 139

Lovely Ladies remembering that if her hair is extremely golden she can wear slightly vivid and jewel tones, whereas if it is not she must wear softer shades. Generally speaking, any blonde should wear colors that accentuate her delicacy and fragility. Although some decidedly dark colors, such as black, navy blue, and a few shades of brown, are becoming to a blonde, for the most part the dark, rich shades and deep colors should be avoided. All of the light pastel shades, on the contrary, are becoming, and to the decided blonde, clear jewel-tone shades as well.

RED: 

Not becoming in any of its shades, including pink and rose, although often worn by blondes. Exceptionally vivid editions of this type can wear a few clear pinks. 

ORANGE: Entirely too vivid a color for any blonde, and never becoming to her. 

YELLOW: Not flattering in a lighter shade than the hair, and in a deeper shade too intense, making the hair look dull by comparison.

Therefore, when worn at all by any blonde it must be chosen with special consideration for the hair.

GREEN: 

In all of its lighter shades is particularly becoming to this type, and more so if the eyes are of a greenish color. But this color must always be selected with an eye to the danger of its sallowing effect upon the skin. 140

Ash Blonde type is very rare and very lovely. It has soft, light hair with very little yellow in it-more of a whitish cast, so that the hair is often mistaken for gray. "Ash 

Blonde" exactly expresses it-the ashes of moonbeams.

The skin of this type is generally colorless and should be kept very fair. The eyes are blue, gray, green, or violet. 

The same chemicals that give a woman this combination of coloring usually give her at the same time a soft, demure personality. This type corresponds in personality to the 

Madonna Brunette type. 

It is quite evident that an Ash Blonde would be entirely washed out by vivid coloring, and would be made to look faded or dull with light, transparent shades. She must always affect soft, deep pastel shades.

This is a very difficult type to analyze because it lacks decided coloring, which is, however, its very charm.

The Demi-Blonde has brown eyes and decidedly blond hair. This does not mean brown eyes and lightish brown hair, which would simply be a lighter edition of one of the brunette types, nor does it mean a blonde with hazel or greenish eyes. It means decidedly brown eyes and decidedly blond hair.

Chapter V "CARROT-HEADS" THIS chapter will be de voted to those whom we call in an affectionate, teasing manner, and somewhat enviously, "red-heads" or "carrot-heads." "Carrot," according to some learned and undistinguished person, comes from the Greek word kara meaning head; and we all know that "carrotheads" are very heady people. Poets have sung the glory of this type for gene rations. Helen of Troy and 

Cleopatra are said to have had hair this color. 

What color crimes are committed by these types! They seldom seem to realize that their hair is their "scream and its glory" at one and the same time, and that this is the very reason why they should be conservative in the colors that they employ. 147

Lovely Ladies 

I can put the "red-heads" into four types, and those four types are: 

Auburn Brunette

Titian Brunette 

Auburn Blonde

Titian Blonde 

Any hair that is vivid enough not to be called blond is titian hair. Any hair that is too bronze and alive to be called brown is auburn hair. But auburn hair and titian hair are the same color; merely different shades of one color. And the brunettes with both auburn and titian hair wear the same colors, and the blondes with auburn and titian hair wear the same colors, merely different shades.

Therefore, I think it will be much clearer if I just put these four into two types and call them: 

Titian or Auburn Blondes 

Titian or Auburn Brunettes

But first, before I give you the colors for each of these types, I must give you some general suggestions that apply to them both. 

Suggestions for

Both Types 

THE charm of these types is their natural coloring, and though every color that they wear must be especially selected to play up to this, it must never under any circumstances be as intense and as vivid as the coloring it serves. Who cannot remember the "red-headed kid" in school whom fond 148

"Carrot-Heads” mamma used to dress in a screaming blue green, the color that for years seemed to be considered the only proper complement to this color of hair?

Occasionally, yes, if you have the personality, and are safely sheltered behind the doors of your own home, you can wear this color and get away with it. Occasionally, too, you can wear it in the evening in public, if the gown is simple, sans metallic glitter or brilliant trimming, and beautifully draped, and depends entirely upon its color and its material for its charm. 

The titian and auburn-haired types should never use rouge to simulate color in the cheeks. A white, colorless skin shows them to their best advantage. Not only that, but almost any rouge that could be used would be inharmonious with the hair. Rose cheeks, as well as red and pink in any form, is simply not desirable as a color harmony.

These types seem to have a special attraction for freckles. 

It is one of the prices that must be paid to Beauty. (See section on Beauty.)

Chapter VI WHITE-HAIRED TYPES 

THERE is no such thing as an elderly woman. Nowadays years add only interesting beauty and enrich the personality as can nothing else. 

One remains the same type as one grows older, and need only take into consideration those subtle changes in coloring that come with the years—graying hair, darkening complexion. To these one should give especial attention as one selects the shades of one's colors; the colors do not change with the years.

When the hair is quite gray, then colors that once were becoming to one may not be so at all, and vice versa. 

When hair is quite gray one must select colors especially that flatter the eyes, and then take into consideration the complexion. Generally speaking, if the eyes are brown and the hair is gray or white you wear best the colors in the first half of the rainbow; and if your eyes are blue, gray, or green, and your hair white, you wear to advantage the colors in the second half of the rainbow, but only the shades of these colors that are flattering to your complexion.

It is of more importance that the woman with gray or white hair dress to her personality rather than to her ac- 154

White-Haired Types tual coloring. This is usually achieved through styles and fabrics rather than colors. A forceful, vivid woman with gray hair would naturally wear a different type of clothing than would a quiet, retiring little woman with a soft, sweet personality, even though they might have a similarity of coloring.

I want to mention here again the complexion. It is especially desirable that the skin be kept as fair as possible when the hair is white or gray, yet this is difficult to do because by the time hair is this color the skin has usually become darkened. If your skin, despite all efforts, is dark, then it is flattered by the use of rouge, and this likewise will enable you to wear certain colors that you otherwise could not wear. It also offsets the sallowing effect of certain otherwise becoming colors. 

Remember that your type does not change until your hair has become completely gray or white. Only then will you have to decide whether you are a White-haired 

Brunette or a White-haired Blonde. Which has nothing 155

Lovely Ladies whatsoever to do with what you were before you changed your coloring.

All of the things I have written above may assist you, although by the time you are white-haired you will have probably so developed your own good taste that you will know most of it already. White-Haired Brunettes

Chapter VII SOME SPECIAL HAIR, EYE, AND COMPLEXION NOTES FOR ALL TYPES 

BEFORE you completely learn your becoming colors and shades you must know a few special things. Indeed, you must learn them even before you look for your type at all, because the following hints will help you decide what is best for your own personal and peculiar self. 

In the first place, the most desirable colors one can wear 160

Hair, Eye, and Complexion Notes are colors that deepen and enrich the color of the eyeswhich seem to give them life and intelligence, and at the same time bring out the coloring and life of the hair, yet do not in any way have a harmful effect upon the complexion. Your next choice will be those colors that are really only flattering to your hair, or only flattering to your eyes, but are not unflattering to your complexion. Any color that is harmful to your complexion should be avoided, although the chances are that if this color is otherwise desirable, adds to the attractiveness of your eyes and hair, you can find a certain shade of it that is not harmful to your complexion. 

Regardless of the color suggestions that follow each type a little farther on, each one will have to be modified to suit the requirement of the skin. Dark skin may mean anything from an olive complexion to a heavy coat of tan or sallowness. A tanned or olive complexion is not undesirable, but a sallow complexion is. Therefore, if your skin is dark you must carefully avoid colors that would make it seem sallow. And if your skin is sallow you must avoid colors that would make it seem more so. Rouge Im- broves Sallow Skin

ANY dark skin, and especially any sallow skin, is improved by the use of a bit of rouge. Yet if your skin is dark or tanned, and you must use rouge to enliven it, you are detracting from the beauty of your hair and eyes. 

For most of the brunette types a bit of rouge on dark skin is not undesirable, but rouge is undesirable for blondes, 161

Lovely Ladies and especially undesirable for the auburn and titian types. In fact, the titian types must never rouge at all. 

The following colors are those that are really unbecoming to a dark skin, and that, even for a lighter skin, must be carefully selected and watched for their possible sallowing effect: 

White, Yellows, Browns, Tans, Greens, Blues, Purples, and some shades of

Orange and Henna. 

However, there are shades in all of these colors that do not have a sallowing effect because they have a tone of some other color. 

Freckles may be "adorable" but they are not beautiful. 

You can keep them away by protecting your skin all of the time from the sun, and using a good freckle cream. In addition to this, realize that all browns and tans, orange, white, and certain shades of yellow tend to make them more evident; never wear these colors if you are inclined to freckle.

To repeat a former statement, something that very few women seem to realize is that while their hair is turning gray, indeed until it is quite gray, they are the type they always were. They should wear the same colors, except that in general all the shades of these colors should be deeper or richer. Pastel shades and white are not becoming to a woman whose hair is more or less streaked with gray. 162

Hair, Eye, and Complexion Notes Out with 

Browns and Tans!

At this stage you must discard ruthlessly browns and tans and keep them out of your life from then until your hair is white. Tans and browns only serve to make more evident the fact that your hair is streaked with gray.

Another matter that is often a stumbling block in finding one's type and choosing one's colors is the decision as to whether one's hair is light brown or dark blond.

You will have to make up your mind which you want it to be, and since Nature failed to do her part in giving you the decided color note that you deserve, help her slightly with your art. Certain harmless rinses that can in no way be called bleaches will serve to lighten your hair and keep it light. In between shampoos there are hair tonics that will do the same. Keeping your hair quite free from natural oiliness with frequent dry shampoos is another way to keep it light. 

And by the way, if you have been a blonde and your hair shows a tendency to darken with age, or with a change in climatic conditions, assist it in resisting. 

On the other hand, if your skin and eyes are such that you prefer your hair to be dark, certain other rinses and tonics, as well as a very mild henna pack once every month or so, will assist. But you must make up your mind just what you want your hair to be and then stick to it!

ANOTHER rather aggravating thing is "intermediate eyes," which is my pet name for those eyes of no particular color 163

Lovely Ladies and depth that borrow color and depth from the colors consequently rove around from one color to another in the most disconcerting fashion. one wears, and

A Word About 

"Intermediate Eyes" 

Your eyes are you, the indications of your intellect and personality. Unless one can remember you by your eyes one has never really known the inner you at all. Therefore, it is desirable to affect the colors that you prefer your eyes to be and avoid those others. 

Say that you have pale, light amber, or yellow eyes.

Never wear all white in one ensemble, as this gives them no depth or color at all. If the rest of your coloring warrants it, and you are as fascinated by green eyes as I am, affect greens, and green color notes in the way of jewelry and accessories to wear with all neutral colors, such as white, black, beige, or gray. 

If you prefer brown eyes your eyes will borrow a velvety depth of brown from browns, tans, orange, burnt orange, and henna. 

If you prefer blue eyes your eyes will borrow blueness from all shades of blue. And you may be able to affect that rarest of all eye colors, violet, with purples and mauves, while shades of gray will of course give your eyes a deep, calm grayness. 

Every woman is her own masterpiece. What wonderful things you can do with the colors you wear! You can subdue all of your bad points and bring out all of your good ones. You can flatter your complexion, you can deepen your eyes and bring out all of their natural beauty, and even 164

Hair, Eye, and Complexion Notes change their color. You can bring out the life and luster your hair. All of this you can do with the colors that you wear. of You have just had all the types, and in

All you have to do is go back and see which type has the color of hair that you have, the color of eyes that you have, and the complexion that you have, and that will be your type; you will be either a light or a dark shade of this type, which will not at all alter the color suggestions that accompany the type, but will affect the shades that you wear of the colors becoming to you.

Chapter VIII TYPES AND COLORS FOR TINY TOTS 

Jo THOUGH to some people babies and children all look alike, they aren't alike. Children can be divided into various types, too. 

It is true of course that the types are fewer, but because juvenile types are fewer and more categorical, color mistakes in children's clothing are more glaring. And as the adult is held accountable for her own taste or lack of it in her clothing, the child reflects the taste of the mother. 

All children can be divided or grouped into six types. 

There are but three distinct hair colors found in children, and two eye colors. According to their combination, these provide the six types. In each are found shades of the type: darker, medium, vivid, light. The colors becoming to the separate types do not vary, but the shades of the colors do, and must correspond to the shade of the type. A child with very light and delicate coloring, for instance, should wear pastel shades whenever practical. One with intense 167

Lovely Ladies coloring can wear vivid shades, and one with dark, rich coloring should wear deep, soft shades.
