To Achieve Slenderness 

AND now for the thin ladies. Slenderness is highly desirable, but not awkward skinniness or painful thinness. First of all, you have suddenly become very thin you must consult a doctor, and preferably a gland specialist. if

We will assume that you have nothing the matter with you, but are thin in spite of efforts to put on weight by overeating. This is all wrong! There are certain things that you can eat, but if you eat too much of anything that may be the exact reason why you are thin. You cannot force yourself to put on fat by overloading your system with fatproducing foods. 

What you can do is this: You can, in the first place, eat five or six times a day, but a little each time, and of the richer foods. You must be as careful of your elimination, 276

Eating Wisely to Retain Beauty your diet, as your heavier sister. If you eat too many starches and fats in an effort to get heavier you may be laying the foundation for a serious illness. 

Thin people usually do not eat very much. They think they do, but they really don't, just as fat people think they eat very little, whereas they really eat a great deal. Establish at once the habit of eating five or six times a day, and do it! 

For example, for your breakfast you can eat as much as you wish, but see that you do eat fruit, toast-or fried cakes with butter and jam, if you wish—and drink hot milk with cream if you can. 

Then halfway between lunch and breakfast take one or two glasses of rich milk with cream and graham crackers.

Luncheon can be selected from the list we advised the heavy ladies against, but not necessarily avoiding the non-fat-producing foods on the other list. Arrange your menu to include fruits and vegetables, and balance it properly all the time, especially if you are eating too much of starches and fats. 

Now between luncheon and dinner eat fruit and drink more milk and cream. Then for dinner practically repeat your luncheon and let your two meals during the day be fairly substantial. 

Just before you go to bed at night, not only to aid you in gaining weight, but also to aid sleep, take a large glass of warm milk and cream with a pinch of salt in it.

If you are painfully thin in particular places, as on your upper arms, your thighs, your knees and elbows, your 277

Lovely Ladies neck, your cheeks, a daily massage of these parts with lanolin or cocoa butter, patting in all that the parts can absorb, will fill them out in no time-only persist in it. 

One of the most beautiful mannequins in Paris is so slender that she looks as though she would break in half from the weight of her little head, yet every angle is exquisitely rounded. I asked her how she had kept such a lovely figure, despite her slenderness, which was beyond the point of thinness, and she told me that she did this very thing for half an hour every night. 

And calmness and tranquillity of mind will help you.

Women who are inclined to be heavy usually are stolidminded, so this is a hint for you to copy them. 278

Chapter XIII THE VOICE OF LOVELINESS IN ANCIENT books one reads the story of Psyche, daughter of a king. So beautiful was she that men worshipped her for a goddess, and of her even Venus, Queen of Beauty, grew jealous. 

Because Venus could brook no rival, Psyche was left on a mountain-top to die. Yet Cupid, seeing her there, fell a victim to his own fatal dart and took her to a certain place where she was ministered to by unseen hands. 

But she might not look on the face of her lover, for he was a god. Yet, as is the way with women, curiosity drove her to wanting the one thing that was forbidden. And because of this she shattered her happiness.

Long she wandered the world through hollow lands and hilly lands, seeking her lost love. So great was her pain that it led her into the shadows of the world of death, past 279

Lovely Ladies the three-headed dog of hell and across the dread river Lethe. Finally the father of the gods took pity on her and made her a goddess, and thus did Psyche join the Immortals and win back her love.

She was beautiful enough even for the God of Love. 

They tell that her mouth was like an early rosebud, and her voice like that of the stars when they sang togetherlike opals poured from pitchers of sweet brass-like amber honey dripping in a silver dish.

Only Immortals can hear the music of the spheres, but Psyche still speaks to us from the ancient and lovely story. 

The Greeks, perhaps because they were so fond of oratory, appreciated a beautiful voice. But to-day we are no less fond of conversation, and a beautiful voice is always something to be desired and sought. 

A Beautiful 

Voice Is an

Essential of 

Loveliness

AN INDISPENSABLE part of a lovely person ality is a beautiful voice. Yet how few voices are really lovely! How few have that desired quality, richness of timbre, depth of expression, clear, firm, quiet enunciation. And the human voice is the sound heard most universally and reiterantly; it is the most obvious evidence of character! 

Criminally little attention is paid to voice development in children and to its correction and development in adults. Actual vocal defects are the exception and not the rule, and so also are trained voices for the theater. But the everyday, average voice is seldom given any attention. 

We have a vague idea, if any at all, that a voice is one of 280

The Voice of Loveliness those things with which we are born, and if it is beautiful we are lucky; if not, too bad!

No one is actually born with any certain kind of a voice. 

We usually develop the type of voice that we hear the most frequently, namely, that of our parents or that of the locality or the country in which we live, with no conscious thought to its beauty or harmonious possibilities. 

Have you a beautiful voice? Truthfully now, how does it sound day after day, year after year, in your own ears? 

How does it sound to others? Do you slur your syllables, and drop letters in a way that strains the attention of your hearers to exasperation? Is your voice weak and characterless, seemingly indicating a timidity of personality which you know you do not possess? Do you speak too rapidly and in a high key that is torture to those who hear you, and unconsciously to your own nerves as well?

Do you speak harshly and loudly in a manner that would indicate a lack of refinement?

A lovely voice has undertones and overtones in it like music. We all take offense at an unbeautiful voice, and harsh ones strike us as would a blow in the face-but in general these reactions are subconscious. It is unfortunate that we are not more conscious of them. Almost every person has at some time or other had the distressing experience of being rudely disillusioned by the speaking voice of some otherwise lovely person.

SPEAKING in too high a key is one of the worst irritations in the world. It is a habit and nothing more, and one that 281

Lovely Ladies will respond easily to a little decisive correction. Another fault, that of speaking too rapidly, is usually a matter of nerves. This is a little more difficult to 

Voice Faults correct, but it is merely a matter of training your subconscious self to be on the lookout for you, figuratively to stop you when you are chopping words off a mile a minute, and say, "There, there, now—take it easy, take it easy."

Then there is the loud, harsh voice. The speaking of words can be a melodious art, and should be, not an earand throat-splitting business. It is all so unnecessary, since one speaks to convey an idea to someone else, and the attention is held so much more successfully by a beautiful voice than by an ugly one.

For a loud, harsh voice you will need more than mental correction, so later I am going to give you some exercises for this. But the first step toward attaining perfection in anything is the conscious realization that perfection is needed and desired.

One speaks to be understood, yet every day we encounter persons whose very business it is to speak, whose livelihood depends upon that speaking, yet who slur their syllables and drop letters from words in a way that annoys us so greatly that we cannot but lose interest in them, and naturally in the thing in which they are trying to interest us.

YOUR first step in acquiring this voice of loveliness is the exercise of deep breathing, which, as you can readily see, 282

The Voice of Loveliness is by no means of value merely to your voice but of inestimable value to your general health, beauty, and poise as well. Three long, deep breaths every

Correct Breathing morning and again at night, just before you pop off to sleep, have tremendous results.

These few breaths must be taken in this manner: Lie flat on your back, fill the lower part of your lungs first, very full, then hold this for a few moments. Next, without taking in any more air, lift this air to the upper part of your lungs. You think you can't take any more, but after you have lifted this air to the upper part of your lungs, while still retaining it, you can take as much more again into the lower part. Now you are full of air. Every tiny little cell in your lungs is absorbing all of the good things that it needs from this air. Indulge this new and very interesting sensation for a few moments, and then open your mouth and exhale very, very slowly. 

You must do this three times every morning and three times every night for at least a week before you begin the second exercise for the acquiring of a beautiful voice.

After you have filled yourself with air and have practised opening your mouth and expelling this air with properly relaxed jaw and throat muscles, commence expelling the air in a prolonged a-a-a-a-h sound, and try to make this musical. 

Now the exercise for relaxing your tongue and throat, which is so simple that it is almost childish, but, childish or not, certainly brings results. It is simply the rapid uttering aloud of tongue-twisting sentences, not only for 283

Lovely Ladies the skill one obtains in uttering difficult sounds, but for the actual tongue-twisting and hence tongue-relaxing effect. "Sister Susie's sewing shirts for sick soldiers." "Peter Piper picked a peck of pickled peppers." 

And any others that you can think of that make sense and that start with different letters, not all "s's" or all "p's", and by the way, you must say these things aloud, preferably while you are dressing and going through your morning beauty attentions.

Your Neck and Jaw

NEXT comes an exercise that must be done while standing. Let the head drop forward as loosely as possible, and with it quite relaxed, move it to the right side, then erect, then to the left, and then forward again. Repeat this rotating movement several times. This is one of the real secrets for keeping neck muscles young, firm, and lovely, too.

Stage 

Whispers 

IT is the clear enunciation of syllables that counts in voice beauty rather than the amount of air plunged through vocal chords. A whispering exercise is especially good for correcting the nervous habit of speaking too rapidly. 

Someone must help you with this, but not jestingly. 

Have him stand across the floor and whisper to him. 

Don't simply say, "Can you hear me?" but read a bit of a morning paper, or a book. Keep doing this until you can be heard. And when you can be heard at that distance 284

The Voice of Loveliness move farther away. In a few weeks you will have developed your enunciation so that a whisper of yours can be heard at the back of a large theater. This exercise subconsciously impresses you with the fact that you need not speak loudly or harshly or with great effort. 

The most important element in a beautiful voice is its low-pitched, well-modulated tone. The carrying quality of a voice depends upon resonance and enunciation, and not upon loudness. After you have practised whispering to someone at a distance speak to him in a low-pitched, quiet tone. The only difference now between your speaking tone and your whispered one will be in the matter of vibration, and it is in this vibration that you get beautiful undertones and overtones that make your voice a thing of beauty and harmony. 

When the

Nose Speaks 

THERE are some voice sounds that must be produced through the nose. Practise these by expelling a long breath (you are still taking them morning and night) through your nose with your lips closed on an "M-m-m" and then an "N-n-n" sound. Hum these to yourself while you are making your toilette, or doing the housework, or studying, or even when you are typing or filing. 

Next practise with such words as "morning," "noon,” and "night," "mamma," "murmur," and see if you sound your "m's" and "n's" through your nose or your throat. They must sound in your nose, but the rest of the word must sound in your throat. 285

Lovely Ladies 

Last but not at all least is to learn to say "a-hem," letting the sound hit the roof of your mouth and drawing it out very long. This is in order to get a general vibratory and humming quality in your entire speech, and is not speaking through your nose. It makes for a richness of tone which designates a richness of personality. 

By giving these exercises your attention every day for such a short time as a month or six weeks, and practising each one not only until you understand it but until it begins to produce the results for which it is intended, you will have a beautiful voice, one that lingers long in the minds of its hearers after you have passed from their sight, repeating in retrospect the pleasure that it gave in actuality. 

Even a hopelessly plain woman can produce a lasting impression of beauty by the magical force of a lovely voice. 

Have in your voice that streaming loveliness remote

Chapter XIV ROMANCE OF PERFUME PERFUME is one of the most ancient of luxuries. The archæ

ologist, that hunter of the past who finds the spoor of his prey in musty tombs and the ashes of long-forgotten cities, can tell you that a race which knew not perfume was not civilized. If you enter a museum and walk through the rooms containing those curious relics from which may be conjured the pageant of humanity, you might well remark, "All is vanity." For you will find rings and earrings and nose rings; pins, brooches, and bracelets; and jars upon jars of clay, of stone, of precious rainbow-hued glass in all sorts and varieties of quaint and lovely shapes which my lovely lady of the past used for her oils and unguents and perfumes.

There have always been lovely ladies, and lovely ladies 287

Lovely Ladies and culture and refinement are always together. Perfume -and the correct use of it-is a mark of refinement, and has been for ages past.

The use of perfume has its roots deep in the beginning of all things. The first man depended upon his sense of smell for his food. Long before he either saw or heard his prey, he smelled it. By this sense of smell he found roots and those vegetables that he finally learned he could eat. 

By it also he discovered water, another essential. It did not take him many centuries to learn that there were odors in the world that could be enjoyed, odors other than those of food.

Finally, with the progress of civilization, man no longer had to depend upon this sense of smell for his life. But he had learned to appreciate this one of his five senses and was reluctant to let it atrophy entirely. So he kept it, and refined it, until he came to the ultimate refinement, perfume. 

Oh, the immortal cherry blossoms that yearly offer their fragrant souls to deathless gods in the gardens of Kinkaku-ji! 

Oh, the burdened boughs of orange blossoms sighing in the still moonlight!

Transient and beautiful, things of the moment and of the locality. Man longed to capture them, to imprison them in oils and unguents, in liquids and powders, for his enjoyment at will. And so he did.

On the earliest tablets of history are engraved the results. Oils and incense, potions and poisons-sometimes made sacred and reserved only for sacrificial and religious 288

Romance of Perfume use, always held mysterious and divine, and permitted only to those who could appreciate them.

Perfume and Elegance PERFUME is the ne plus ultra of elegance, and its proper use is an art. 

Almost anyone can appreciate the odor of flowers, of fresh hay in the spring, of the woods after rain, and of flower-laden air tinct with the salt breath of the ocean as one finds it on the shores of the Mediterranean or on the coast of California or Florida. But unless a person has cultivated and refined this least understood of the senses, the sense of smell, he can neither appreciate the innumerable subtle odors and scent harmonies that arise from the braziers of the world's beauty, nor bend them to fancies.

The true elegante makes a fetish of her perfumes. She has developed the art to the extent of knowing how to use, throughout her life, a certain odor in all of her cosmetics, which subtly imparts to her personality an intangible sweetness that people always associate with her. 

Perfumes and

Personality REALIZE that it is not always the perfume that one likes that is best suited to one's personality. For there are as many personalities as flower odors—yes, and even more perfumes than both of these. Yet on analyzing perfumes we find that they are all built upon a very few basic odors. Similarly in analyzing personalities, one finds only a very few basic characteristics. 

Would you put a tiny, fragile, blue-eyed ash blonde 289

Lovely Ladies into an elaborate dress of purple velvet and brocade? 

You might, but you shouldn't! Not even if you thought she needed more richness of color to bring out her coloring and personality, for that would snuff out the delicate charm that is there. Similarly your perfume must be selected with attention to personality.

The way to select a perfume for yourself is to study your personality, your appearance, and your moods, and then to select a perfume that you know expresses you. It may take years and one must be ruthless with all of those fascinating, intriguing little bottles which prove so deceptive as to contents-when the contents comes into contact with yourself. 

Merely liking a certain perfume odor does not mean that one can use it. If you should be passionately fond of a perfume that does not mean you in your own mind or in the memory of your friends have a lovely bottle of it on your dresser so that you may sniff it to your heart's content-but never use it upon yourself!

Intelligent 

Analysis PERFUME chemists when analyzing a per fume put a few drops of it upon a piece of blotting paper and smell it at intervals over a period of days. Some of the ingredients evaporate more rapidly than others, and as the scent changes they identify the different ones. This would be an excellent way for you to select your perfume.

If you have studied your personality you must know how it changes according to your mood and what effect it has 290

Romance of Perfume upon others. It should not be difficult for you to select a perfume that expresses this personality and one that will not clash with any of your moods. If you are exotic you can safely affect an exotic perfume. But if you are not exotic, your use of an exotic perfume would be ridiculous.

A quiet and not entirely modernistic personality requires a scent that conjures up a picture of crinolines and little paper-frilled bouquets. If you are tiny and flowerlike you cannot indulge in exotic perfumes. Whereas there is nothing sillier than a statuesque woman affecting the fragile odor of jasmine or lilies-of-the-valley!

Secrets! 

THERE was a day when it would have been simple to suggest the perfumes that might possibly suit certain definite personalities. But today a perfume may be a blend of fifteen or twenty odors, blended according to a secret formula (this formula carefully guarded by the parfumeur who makes it), and christened with an exotic name that is no clue to its real nature.

Therefore I can only show you upon what to base your selection of a perfume. You must select the basic odor that interprets you, and then study to learn what scents harmonize with this odor.

Harmonies of Smell 

JUST as there are certain colors that blend harmoniously with one another, just as there are notes of music that harmonize, so there are scents that belong together. All are vibrations 291

Lovely Ladies transmitted to our recording brain through the different senses.

To some it is as instinctive to blend odors cleverly and harmoniously as it is for musicians to strike chords that invariably harmonize. Others must study and experiment and analyze.

Des Esseintes, a character in a great French novel, so developed his sense of smell that he invented a perfume organ for his personal delight, upon which he played "unheard" melodies, songs without notes. But a Des

Esseintes is a rarity. If each of us could only pick out the one perfume note that is appropriate to our personality we accomplish something extremely valuable.

Limit Your Desires 

ONE can hardly use more than one or two, or at the very most, four odors. I don't mean four at a time, but four for the entire perfume repertoire-simply because a scent is not the transient thing that we think it is, and no woman in the world can have four complete wardrobes, each one to be worn with a different perfume.

Scented 

Necessities 

THE things that one might use that are scented are face powders, talcum powders, sachet powders, toilet water, soap, bath salts, brilliantine, cleansing cream, skin food, powder base, lipstick, and hand lotion. When one stops to think that each of these is usually scented, and that each 292

Romance of Perfume of them has a different scent of a more or less lasting nature, one realizes in what a discordant symphony of odors one daily indulges.

Yet all of these can be found in either a scentless preparation or one that has a light, indefinite odor that quickly leaves when it is exposed to the air. You cannot always afford to have all cosmetics made in one especial odor. 

Therefore, use scentless or neutrally scented ones, and confine the use of your special perfume to the atomizer, or to the cosmetics you can obtain in your particular scent.

Perfume 

Usage Now when you have a perfume that suits your personality, moods, and type there remains the extremely important matter of using it properly.

It is of importance to elegance that you do not use many conflicting scents. Finding a perfume that exactly suits your personality and affecting it in all of your scented cosmetics by no means limits you to one odor. It limits you merely to perfumes with a similar base. For example, the best known perfume bases are amber, musk, and chypre; you can affect either of these three as your basic odor, and build upon that with three or possibly four scents which, when all the other ingredients have evaporated, will leave but the intangible sweetness of this base. 

If you do attempt any such subtlety, be very sure of your art. And if you are being thus subtle with your perfumes you must be more than ever careful that your cosmetics have an odorless base. 293

Lovely Ladies 

Intangibility 

REALIZE that a perfume should be sensed and not smelled.

Not until one has left a room, and perhaps forgotten a handkerchief or glove, should anyone know that you actually use a perfume. That is the impeccable way of using it, so that neither you nor anyone else can define it at any time. In other words, it is you who should be effective-not your perfume. 

The safest and most successful way to assure yourself of this is by the correctuse of sachet powder. Your handkerchief box should be lined with cotton batting covered with silk and filled with sachet powder, or should hold a large flat sachet bag which imparts its subtle and delightful odor to the dainty linen within.

A LOVELY lady will have many boxes, Scented Boxes since they alone keep all of her dainty things separated, and with the aid of scented linings or sachet pads permeate them with the subtle odor of her favorite perfume. There will be a scented box for handkerchiefs, another for gloves, and several for different types of lingerie. And then there is the clever touch of putting sachet powder into the hem of lighter dresses, into the hem of a coat, into linings of fur collars and pockets. No 294

Romance of Perfume one but a truly dainty and instinctively lovely woman thinks of these things. She even puts sachet in all of the sofa cushions and window drapes. 

AND another important perfume hint: Restraint

Many perfumes are harmless in their way, yet dangerous in their potentialities if mishandled. There are certain perfumes that a woman of refinement will avoid, unless she is sure that she has the self-control to use them with the utmost economy. Heavy perfumes are dangerous to loveliness! Too, you can yourself become so accustomed to the odor you are using that you fail to realize what an impact it makes upon the sensitive nostrils of those around you.

Our sense of hearing is apparently more highly developed than any of our other senses. An insistent noise, however pleasant or however faint it may be, will irritate us. Yet a wrong color combination, or an inharmonious blend of odors, will pass by us unnoticed. And when you realize that they are all three the same thing-vibrations that are transmitted to the brain through the sense that received them—it is rather difficult to understand why that is so. Nevertheless, these inharmonious vibrations are affecting us as an actual attack on our nerves. 

And do you know that a man's sense of smell is more highly developed than a woman's? An atavistic trait, no doubt, that can be traced back to the days when a man had to go forth to hunt food for himself and his mate. And women seldom realize how often they offend people around 295

Lovely Ladies them by the use of too much perfume and too many mingled odors. 

Four perfumes are the limit of a lovely lady's perfume repertoire. And if you are sufficiently versed in perfume law to be able to have and to use this many, you can certainly feel that you have attained the ne plus ultra of perfume art. 

One of your four perfumes should be an out-of-door perfume. Another your general perfume, the third your slightly more exotic but subtle evening perfume, and your fourth a quite exotic boudoir perfume. 

If you have never really been pleased with a perfume, and succeeded in blending it harmoniously to your personality and moods, or if you have never been able satisfactorily and harmoniously to blend perfume odors, go to a reliable chemist and instruct him to make formulas for you based upon your favorite perfume. Once these formulas are satisfactory, they are forever and individually yours, and you can have them made up wherever you might be. And you will know that they harmonize with one another.

Individuality 

THE average woman still allows herself to be literally pulled around by the nose by perfume manufacturers and clever advertising writers who have discovered how to intrigue thoughtless women into buying perfumes that are supposed to be à la mode for the moment. In this we are far behind our clothes sense, because not for half a century have we allowed 296

Romance of Perfume 

Dame Fashion to herd us like sheep into one style fad and then out of that into another. 

And an intelligent woman never uses a perfume simply because some kind friend gave it to her for Christmas or merely because it is popular. 

Perfume is a refinement of civilization. Learn to use this refinement in your life, in all of the little ways that subtly express you to yourself and to those who come in contact with you. 

Analyze your personality, your moods, and your outward appearance impersonally. Carefully study and analyze the certain perfumes that appeal to you, and view them in conjunction with yourself. Then study further to ascertain which scents harmonize with the odor finally selected for your very own.

And use those scents and no others in all of your cosmetics! Thus will you be a true artist of the most subtle and mysterious of the senses, of the most ancient and elegant of artificialities-perfume. 297

Chapter XV DAINTINESS IS REFINEMENT 

OF THE abstract qualities which contribute to real beauty and loveliness there are few more important than daintiness and refinement. Usually we expect to find these two virtues as the natural heritage of culture, and in the strict sense of the word there is no culture without them.

Occasionally, however, we meet men, and less frequently women, who have achieved remarkable things in worth-while directions-great writers, painters, engineers, students-but who lack these two qualities. 

Always we are disappointed. We leave them with a sense of emptiness of bewilderment. For their achievements seem cold, impersonal—and, yes, unimportant, somehow, because the people themselves have not a degree of refinement commensurate with their station in society and the magnitude of their achievements. 298

"He's a diamond in the rough." You've heard that expression many times. Superficially it seems to be laudatory. On the surface it says, "There's a valuable man, that fellow; he has good stuff in him." But what it's really saying somewhat contemptuously is: "Too bad—a good man, but no poise, no manners, no refinement." "Refinement" is a much abused word. And its worst abuse is its interpretation as a tangible thing, instead of an effect, as it surely is.

What Refine ment Is

Refinement is a spiritual thing, a cultured thing-a product of conscious breeding. 

And the outward appearances of refinement are expressed in sartorial daintiness in a woman, and in careful grooming in a man. Nor does this mean the ultra fussiness which actually characterizes the person who is not well bred. It means the quietly assured neatness and careful grooming which, with conscious practice, soon becomes in tinctive and as such permits one to dismiss the details of it from one's mind when the necessary outward gestures are made. 

What lovely lady is not fastidious about herself and her surroundings? This fastidiousness does not depend upon wealth, time, or lack of it, or anything else but the promptings of her inner nature. For even the woman with a personal maid is not always dainty, even though she may be superficially neat-until something comes along to muss her. If a woman is not fundamentally dainty, no number of maids can keep her so. 299

Lovely Ladies 

Instinctive daintiness is sometimes a gift, but far more often is it the result of years of conscious practice which make it a part of one and not an affectation. 

ONE of the least known and used and yet 

An Old Secret most simple and valuable aids to daintiness is-boxes! You have no idea what delightful things they are, what indispensable aids to that daintiness that is the essence of loveliness. Once you learn to use them, you will never again put away your pretty things, however carefully and nicely folded, into dresser drawers. 

Any kind of box will do-plain cardboard ones, a little heavier than the average cardboard box, or what is ever so much better, light-weight wooden boxes. The best are those that are hinged on one side, but they must close down tightly. 

Then line them like this: Cut pieces of cardboard to fit all six sides of the box-the top, the bottom, and four sides. 

Now lay flat cotton wadding on this cardboard into which you have just put your sachet powder, and then cover the cotton wadding with crêpe de Chine, taking big, tight stitches across the back of the cardboard. Glue these with an odorless glue onto the sides of your box. Or if you do not wish to line the boxes, although I advise it, have sachet bags made, as large as the box, and keep one in the bottom and one on top. Should you not have time to do this immediately, as a temporary measure sprinkle perfume on squares of white blotting paper and tie several squares together. 300

In one box keep all of your gloves. Whether "all of your gloves" means one pair or ten, keep them in this scented box and keep it closed. In another box keep your stockings, and never anything but your stockings. Then another box for handkerchiefs. And as many boxes as you wish for your lingerie, probably one for each different type of lingerie that you wear.

Another box will be for your evening vanity, where you will keep also your fan, or your corsage, or any of the lovely things that you want to keep fresh and dainty, unsoiled and uncrushed. You can't have too many boxesyou will always find something to put in them. 

How delightfully the scent permeates every article, and the boxes keep the various articles from rubbing against one another and getting those little snags and snarls that are otherwise unavoidable. Not only that, but your glove and your handkerchief boxes may be very ornamental and can be kept on your bureau. 

And what about a little scented box for your love letters and souvenirs!

Next on the path that daintiness treads toward refinement and loveliness one comes to such things as padded clothes hangers, padded shoe trees, padded hat forms. 

Upon every one of your clothes hangers, no matter how ordinary they may be, slip little covers padded with scented cotton. But do by all means put black covers on some and white on the others, and always put your dark dresses on the dark ones and your light ones on the light hangers. 301

Lovely Ladies 

Hat forms will be a little harder to find, and you may have to have them made by a carpenter. But in one year they will more than pay for themselves in what you save in hats. These also you cover with silk and scented cotton. 

And now dress covers. They also will be somewhat of an expense, but they save your dresses, and last forever. Make yours of heavy, .durable linen or cotton, in neutral shades that will not rub off. They save frocks from dust, from possible sun fading, but most of all from rubbing against one another in closets. If you do not have one of them for every dress, at least have one for each of your evening dresses and best frocks that are not worn very often.

WHEN Little Trifles you have this far on the road gone to loveliness it will have become automatic with you to snip off a frayed ribbon here, to take a few moments with the hot iron to iron out collars and cuffs, wilted ribbons, wrinkles across the back of your skirt; to brush your shoes with a bit of cotton dipped in gasoline; to sew new lingerie straps to your lingerie frequently, since they get the most wear and cannot be laundered back to their original freshness. 

Speaking of lingerie straps, there is no excuse for a lingerie strap to slip down over your shoulders. You can easily have little bits of tape put in the shoulder seams of each gown as soon as it comes from the store, or, if you prefer, have little gold safety pins to pin all your lingerie straps to the under shoulder seam of your gown. 302

Now we come to those subtle, more intimate niceties of daintiness to which every woman owes it to herself and to those about her to pay strict attention-for daintiness comes also from an inner, personal refinement, a bodily cleanliness and sweetness.

A dainty woman cannot be careless about her personal hygiene, because by attention to it she keeps her clear complexion, lovely hair, and unclouded eyes. For instance, you must be careful when your "tummy is out of whack" to give it a rest. And you must be extremely watchful of daily correct elimination, because it is from faulty elimination more than anything else that one has unpleasant body odors, which unfortunately are more noticeable to others than to ourselves. Body odors cannot be eliminated by the use of scent. In fact, there is nothing more unpleasant than the combination of perfume and body odors. A truly dainty woman understands perfectly that it is not enough merely to bathe a great deal. One has body odors, and often very unpleasant ones, that a bath an hour could not correct but that could be corrected by some inner personal hygiene. 

Personal Hygiene 

ARE you very careful of your elimination, of the amount of water that you drink, and of the eating of certain foods which your body may throw out in the form of unpleasant odors from the pores? If you must eat odorous foods, by all means do so, but don't think merely saying, "You must excuse me because I had onions to-day," is sufficient excuse for you 303

Lovely Ladies to go on blowing your unpleasant breath in another's face.

There are so-called deodorants on the market which neutralize body odors. If you have the idea that they are injurious to you, and that by suppressing excess perspiration in a certain place it will only appear somewhere else, then use one of the powder deodorants which neutralize all body odors without in any way suppressing elimination through the pores. 

Deodorants, talcum powder, astringents, mouth washes, the proper treatment of the skin areas that are too active in their functions of eliminating waste will remove all possibilities of unpleasant odors. If the area under your arms, for instance, is overactive in its eliminating functions, you can gradually and harmlessly close the pores and make them smaller and consequently less active with the use of a mild astringent after bathing. You can completely neutralize foot odors and in time quite correct any such abnormal condition by bathing them night and morning in a saturated solution of boracic acid and then dusting the powdered boracic acid into them in the place of talcum powder. Of course, you will not have to do this for longer than a week or two at the most, because after the condition is corrected just using the powdered boracic acid instead of talcum will keep them in a healthy condition. 

In the chapters on Beauty you will find detailed hints about caring for your hands and nails, hair and teeth.

But here are some extra hints that perhaps you should impress upon yourself. Sometimes just the average every- 304

day care is not enough, particularly if you have neglected yourself for a while. But after these abnormal conditions are corrected the average daily care is sufficient. 

Your hair, for instance. Of course, no dainty woman could tolerate dandruff, and should this unpleasant scalp condition arise she takes the necessary steps to correct it immediately. 

But even from the healthiest and cleanest scalp will fall particles of cuticle and dust, particularly on dark dresses, at the back of the neck, and all around, every time she has run the comb or brush through her hair. 

If you have superfluous hair anywhere on the body you simply keep it removed with the persistent use of depilatories or a razor, as I have already told you in the Beauty Rites. Some brands of depilatories actually destroy the hair roots in time. Truly offensive and evident hairs require immediate use of the electric needle, which is not too painful and indeed is a small price to pay for daintiness. Be sure to get a reliable operator, though. Any but a skilled person with an electric needle might scar you for life. 

Of course you take care of your teeth by giving them the average daily care and a semiannual trip to the dentist. 305

Lovely Ladies 

But are you very careful to give them a quick brush after eating? Nothing so mars their beauty and luster, for instance, as that piece of candy, which you put into your mouth just when you think you are perfectly groomed and ready to leave your boudoir for the evening. An occasional light brushing with your toothbrush dipped in powdered pumice will be very effective, but you must be extremely careful that you do not brush too hard, as this will scratch the enamel-something that never can be repaired. Daintiness in Eating

EATING is a fundamental gesture in which every living being must indulge. And custom sanctions eating en masse. Yet real elegance in eating is known and practised by very few. 

Most of us are given a smattering of rules when we are young, and after these are learned the gestures of eating become more or less automatic and careless. 

It is of no especial use to mention bad or ugly table manners. No cultured person can have ugly table manners. 

Eating food too quickly or too noisily, bending over the plate like a snapping turtle every time something is to be conveyed to the mouth, drinking noisily are all unforgivable things that cannot be condoned, and not to do them furnishes one merely with the fundamentals of good table manners. But the elegancies of table manners, which are a matter of inherent refinement and daintiness, are all too seldom found.

Nor is this instinctive elegance in eating a matter of manners and custom. There is a story of the wife of the 306

Doge, Dominico Silvio, of Venice, who was said to have been too dainty to touch food with her fingers, and who was therefore given a small gold fork to eat with; she was described as being "luxurious beyond belief." So you see, even in such a day and such a time, there were really dainty women who did not conform to the accepted manner of eating food, because the standards of the day violated their instinctive daintiness. 

The Art of 

Eating 

Macaroni she smiled at 

NOT so long ago, I saw a lovely lady, who had real assurance in her impeccable good breeding, ask the bewildered waiter firmly for a small sugar tongs, the while her escort and explained that she simply didn't want to stain her fingers with artichoke leaves! 

Do you think that anyone who saw her doubted or questioned her manners or thought her "affected"? Certainly not!

Some of us are still near enough to the days of eatingwith-the-fingers to rejoice when the rules of etiquette allow us certain "finger foods" and we can revert to primitive methods. But a dainty woman doesn't delight in them at all, not even in the privacy of her own boudoir. 

A Spaniard writes a very amusing paragraph inspired by observing what might have been a lovely lady eating macaroni. He writes: "D'Annunzio, in collaboration with 

Mussolini, should issue a decree to the faithful on the way to eat macaroni, for it is certain that people do not know how to do so with any degree of elegance and decorum. 307

Lovely Ladies 

When you cut it with a fork or a knife, it is very difficult to pick up the pieces; if you do not cut it, but launch an enveloping movement with fork and spoon, a movement much favored by the Diaz and Cadornas of macaroni, the strategic move is not sufficient, and you find yourself half eating and half drinking, with the macaroni hanging from your mouth like white tapeworms-a sight unworthy of Ruskin, D'Annunzio, and Mussolini."

Undisturbed by these æsthetic considerations, the lady who inspired this outburst continued to "half drink and half eat three quarters of her macaroni," while the other quarter decorated the nether portion of her face and upper part of her bosom. 

Food and

Lipstick ALL of which may be a little far-fetched, but do you not eat corn on the cob lavishly anointed with dripping butter, salt and pepper, to which, while eating, you add the decorative finishing touches with smears of lipstick? The only dainty way to eat macaroni, spaghetti, or vermicelli is through a funnel, and the only way to eat corn on the cob is in the bathtub, which last I have plagiarized from the Hawaiian saying about the eating of mangos.

Then what about the heavy lip coloring that it is nowadays permissible to use? Since you know that your lip rouge is going to become very badly smeared and inartistically displaced as you eat, don't you think that a dainty woman would have the imagination to wipe the most of it off before she commenced to eat? And then there is the matter 308

of consideration for your hostess's napkins, even the napkins in the restaurant, because it is almost impossible to remove most lipstick from linen when it is laundered. 

But apart from this altruism, a really dainty woman will, as I said, remove the most of her lip coloring before she commences to eat, simply because she knows from experience and from observation of other women, if not herself, that it will smear very sadly during the meal. 

Good Manners

Are Instinctive MANNERS which lazy civilization condones are not always those which instinctive refinement accepts. A lovely lady in any country, no matter how she has been taught, or how little importance has been given to her table manners, cannot be ungraceful and lacking in daintiness with her food. 

The table manners of many of our European sisters are quite shocking to the Anglo-Saxon mind, yet even we, who have the most graceful table manners of any country in the world, condone certain gestures that an instinctively refined and elegant person would not indulge in. 

The Perils of 

Smoking 

APROPOS of gestures of elegance, may I not mention that latest gestural acquisition of femininity-smoking? 

There is no reason why a woman should not smoke. But there is no reason why she should handle her cigarette like a man. When women will learn to lend their dainty gracefulness to the so-called masculine gestures and pre- 309

Lovely Ladies rogatives that they wish to incorporate into their lives, men will no longer resent their so doing. But when they try to appropriate these gestures, and do as men do, they not only fail as imitators, but men resent what they consider poaching on their preserves, and women lose an opportunity to be graceful in a new field. 

Handle Your 

Cigarette Gracefully

HANDLING a cigarette has charming possi bilities for graceful gestures that do not in the least interfere with what seems to be the pleasure of smoking. Cigarette cases, holders, and all of the other accessories, useful or desired, are indispensible to elegance for the gentlewoman who smokes. It takes but a moment to fill your cigarette case from your package of cigarettes, and but a moment to put a cigarette into a holder. These gestures should be automatic and based upon an instinctive desire for elegance and daintiness in all things. 

A woman should never speak with a cigarette in her mouth. There is something mirth-inspiring about it, and the action is extremely ungraceful. 

And why, when cigarettes cost as little as one cent a piece or so, smoke one to the bitter end? Nicotine-stained fingers are unpardonable! And the price you pay in lost loveliness is far too great for any pleasure one can possibly get from a cigarette. 

If you must smoke, do not overlook the fact that tobacco leaves an unpleasant odor on your breath, in your hair, and on your hands. If you must indulge in a habit that has 310

definite drawbacks to your loveliness, recognize them and guard against them. 

Daintiness and womanliness go hand in hand. You cannot be a real woman unless you are dainty; and a woman who is not dainty arouses in one much the same feeling that would be aroused by a mud-spattered and trampled orchid. 

Remember that daintiness is indispensable to loveliness. 

It is the absolute quintessence of femininity. Everyone is born with this instinct-even kittens. But sometimes daintiness must be tended before it will flower of its own accord. 

All civilized people know the average, expected gestures of civilization. Lovely ladies improve upon them! 311

THE DAH-RAY COLOR CHART 

Indicates the three Primary colors, Red, Yellow, and Blue. 

Indicates the three Secondary colors, Orange,

Green, and Violet, which on the chart fall between the Primary colors that make them.

NOTE: Notice that each Primary color on the chart points with arrows to a Secondary color. The nearer a Secondary color is to a Primary color, the more tone of that Primary color it will have. Hence a 

Yellowish-Green has more Yellow in it than Blue (i.e., is nearer the Yellow than the Blue) and a 

Bluish-Green has more Blue in it than Yellow (i.e., is nearer the Blue).

The Tertiary colors are Brown and Gray, and are the colors produced by the mixing of the three 

Primary colors.

Brown, indicated on the chart between the three Primary colors and Black, is the Tertiary color that is produced by the three Primary colors and unlightened by White. 313

The Dah-Ray Color Chart 

Gray, indicated on the chart in a circle outside of the three Primary colors, is indicated as being near to White because it is produced by the mixing of the three Primary colors, and is lightened by White. 

White is the luminous combination of all colors and indicated on the outer edge of this chart as being the combination of all the colors in it, and luminous.

Black (in the center of the chart) is the total absence of all color, or the combination of all colors unilluminated and hence unseeable, or Black. 

NOTE: Brown is the actual color produced by mixing transparent Red, Blue, and Yellow, but when 

White or opacity is added, the result is Gray. 

Actually a true Gray is produced by mixing Black and White. This is comprehensible when it is understood that Brown comprises all colors, yet not in sufficient proportions to neutralize them, as does Black, which comprises all colors and yet unilluminated is impossible to see. Hence Black (or all colors, each one of which is neutralized), illuminated by 

White, makes what we see as Gray. 314

BOOK III 

BEING suggestions for the acquiring of a modern and intelligent perspective on the most important phase of a lovely lady's life, such as, perchance, the adventures or misadventures that attend friendship, courtship, marriage, motherhood, divorce, and other similar experiences with which a lovely lady may be blessed or afflicted.

Chapter I IT'S CALLED SEX APPEAL 

THE farther we progress in this textbook of loveliness, the more often do we find one word staring at us out of the printed pages. The word is "intelligence," and coming at long last to a necessary explanation of socalled sex appeal, I find that I need more than ever to call upon your intelligence, this one emancipating quality which 

Nature has given humans to set them above the animals. 

I write primarily for the American woman, and if ever there was a land hedged in and smothered under fetishes and phobias it is the United States of America. Indeed, half the land seems threatened with reformer's conscience, while the other half has blown itself free from this blighting influence with such vigor that it has become grotesquely reactionary and dangerously broad. A lovely lady cannot belong to either of these groups. 321

Lovely Ladies Sex Is Not 

Vulgar 

SEX appeal is neither vulgar, sordid, nor salacious. It is not something to be considered only in camera, but rather it is a subject upon which we need the brightest light of intelligent discussion. More than that, sex appeal is as important and needful to the normal woman as food and sleep. 

Freud has tried to make sex the corner stone of life. I would not quarrel with Freud, yet it is not necessary to agree with him entirely. He attaches a bit too much importance to it.

One has a world of shifting points of view to deal with when examining sex appeal. A hundred years ago the whole subject of sex was carefully hidden away in the oblivion of things not understood. And in this century thousands of neurotics have appeared to drag it from that oblivion, only to sink it in the mud and mire of abuse. But all false standards and attitudes melt away before cold facts-and here are a few facts for your consideration:

The whole scheme of life, and particularly that part which embraces the relationships between men and women, is worked out on a positive and negative basis. You all have heard the rule: "Unlike signs attract; like signs repel."

And so we may consider men as represented by the familiar plus sign, women by the minus sign. 

It is a part of the cosmic scheme that men should attract women and that women should attract men, for the continuation of the species. And so an all-wise Providence has 322

It's Called Sex Appeal made the matter interesting, attractive-in short, the most wonderful thing in life when properly understood. 

But this chapter is not concerned with fundamental sex urges, but with sex appeal as a specific attribute of loveliness, and more particularly with the proper use of sex appeal. 

Proper Sex Understanding 

What is sex appeal? It is nothing in the world but the strength of your attraction for members of the opposite sex. It has nothing to do with your own propensities for la grande passion. It is not concerned with your possibilities for incalescence under a tropical

Is an Obligation moon, nor your concupiscent proclivities à la Salome. 

Every woman born into the world has sex appeal.

There are differences in types of appeal, and differences in degree, but we all have it as a fundamental. You might not appeal, for example, to a Casanova, but on the other hand a Don Juan might have gone wild over you. 'Twas always thus, and your problem is not to acquire sex appeal, but to enhance your appeal so that you attract the right person. 

Your grandmother may tell you that it is "improper” to develop your sex appeal. It not only is highly proper for you to give all possible attention to this effect of your charm and loveliness, but it is more than that. It is your duty. 

IF MATINGS are properly undertaken, each succeeding generation is better fitted to cope with life than the last. And 323

Lovely Ladies you owe it to any children you may have to give them the best start in life possible. One can do nothing so important for them as furnishing them with sound mental 

Use Sex Appeal Intelligently equipment. You yourself may be ever so intelligent, but you must attract an intelligent and satisfactory male if you would follow life through to its logical and beautiful consummation.

I have said that we all have sex appeal. I use the term "sex appeal" because it has become firmly intrenched in the vernacular. "Personal magnetism" is just as good a term, and, in fact, from my point of view it is a little better. Because, while sex is the keynote of attraction, many other qualities are concerned in the ultimate appeal. 

Culture, breeding, refinement, intelligence, and a host of others. And they all combine to draw to the lovely woman the many things which she needs in her life. 

But a woman must know how to use her personal magnetism, and the first thing she must be careful of is not to overshoot the mark. A natural evolution in dress, for instance, which may easily be traced and explained, but which I will not stop to discuss here, has given women a license in dress which may become a dangerous thing.

In fact, if a woman so chooses, she may almost approach nudity in dress without engendering serious criticism. And some women do this. They make a great mistake.

SUGGESTION is much more powerful than stark reality. 

The next time you find yourself in a gathering of smart 324

It's Called Sex Appeal A Little Ex periment You 

May Try men and women, ask your escort, casually, to select the woman he considers loveliest. If he is a gentleman of taste you may profit by his selection. Because ten times out of ten he will select-not the woman who opens the whole battery of her pulchritude on every man she meets, and discloses as many of her intimate physical charms as decency permits, but some reserved, charming, competent woman, who achieves a remarkable magnetism through the suggestion of physical charms rather than through their revelation. And she will have about her the air of mystery. 

Men are boys. Boys take watches apart because they do not understand them. If they understood them they wouldn't be interested. And when a man thoroughly understands a woman-knows all about her and has nothing for his imagination to feed upon-he is not interested.

Another error which women too often make is in the use of perfumes. Perfumes should be "sensed and not seen.” 

Don't use a blatant perfume. Let your scent be subtle, and let it intrigue by its mystery.

Make-up- 

Civilized and Savage

AND then, finally, make-up. Here is where most young girls err woefully! They don't seem to understand the purpose of makeup. Or they have a savage conception of it, which uses make-up to achieve a startling and often grotesque effect. 325

Lovely Ladies 

The savage girl is like all of her sisters. She knows the same things and nothing more. She has seen the same sights, can do the same things, has the same background, and, in fact, a dozen such women might be rolled into one so far as individuality is concerned. And so she casts about for something to make her distinctive—to make her stand out from the rest and capture the attention of the eligible male. And she hits upon the obvious artifice of painting her face, or putting a ring in her nose, or wearing an anklet.

But you are in a center of civilization where refinements of adornment are a prerequisite of good taste, and where the garish make-up repels and revolts rather than attracts. 

And so I say that you must realize that the purpose of make-up is to simulate Nature—to make up for her little lacks and oversights, and to set off her masterpiece as the matrix sets off the beautiful diamond.

Vivid make-up does not do this. Instead it smothers real beauty and charm. Try this: Wash your face every morning and every night for a week, and apply your make-up very lightly and faintly. You'll like it so much, and all of your intelligent friends will like it so much, that you will never again use excessive make-up.

Actually, the farther you progress toward your goal of being a lovely lady, the more subtle sex appeal and magnetism you will have. Beauty is not enough. Emerson says truly: "Beauty without expression tires." Nor do you need beauty to make your way through life. But if you have beauty and add to it other character qualities based 326

It's Called Sex Appeal upon intelligence you will actually be that most perfect expression, a lovely and womanly woman. And as such, with your personal magnetism you cannot fail to attract to yourself everything that you need and wish to complete your life. 327

Chapter II MODERN WOMEN AND MEN 

WHAT part do men play in your life? 

Men must play some part in your life, and their rôle must be an important one, though it is not the same to-day that it was for many centuries. When mother was a girl, if a woman didn't marry she was shunted off from one relative to another, where indeed she was probably twice as useful as she would have been anywhere else in life, but where she was nevertheless always made to feel that she was a burden. 

It simply never occurred to women that they could be financially independent and as such morally independent. 

Our First Men 

Now custom still has it, and indeed quite rightfully so, that a man supports us from the time we are born until the time we have reached a sufficient growth and intellectual ability to take care of 328

Modern Women and Men ourselves. Which is presumably about the time that we leave school. And in that interval likewise we depend, if we have them, upon our brothers, who, however much we may disagree with them or they with us, are always ready to defend us in a serious pinch.

But after she stops depending upon her father for support, and her brother for protection, an intelligent woman should ask herself seriously just exactly what further part men are going to play in her life. And she should answer this question sensibly and fairly. There is a definite place for men in every woman's life, and to deny it is ridiculous, even as to magnify it is stupid. The modern intelligent woman knows that men are no longer the most important factors in her life. That while men are important, they should not be made a goal of life.

A Fight 

Against

Tradition 

LET us open our attack by a broadside against the shortsighted woman who still believes that her one serious purpose in life is to "get a man." She dares to call herself a modern woman-the woman who carries on with the idea of snaring a male, by fair means or foul, and looks upon the place of a parasite as her proper station in society-but she certainly is not! 

Look to Your

Values 

IT IS poor business indeed to sell your birthright for a mess of pottage, and to hand over your heritage of a full, a glorious, an independent life for three square meals a day at the whim of some male. And so I am going to counsel you. 329

Lovely Ladies 

Put away your bag of tricks. Forget your silly artifices.

Put behind you all that is unworthy-all that smacks of the dark past when your sisters were in bondage to an irrational ethic, and understand the proud, the modern, and the decent conception of the relationships between men and women. Don't try to "play down" to the men you meet, and don't try to trick them. Instead, meet them on their own ground, and through your intelligence, personality, and depth win their respect and admiration. 

Do this, and I promise you just as surely as night follows day that, to use a Biblical phrase, "all these other things will be added to you." "But suppose I am beautiful, and suppose I meet some wealthy man who admires me. Suppose I marry him and he affords me a protected and sheltered life in which I find every comfort I could wish without struggling and striving for it. It seems to me that such an existence would be very pleasant." 

A Metaphor 

WOULD it? I am going to answer you in metaphor. Sickly, pale, flaccid in fruit, leaden of leaves, the mistletoe draws life from the tree to which it has attached itself. The tree stands alone and hates the parasite which sucks its blood. And the parasite, subconsciously, hates and resents the full and abounding life and strength and freedom of the tree.

Otherwhither two trees stand side by side, laughing and whispering in the wind. Free, strong, and beautiful, they toss their streaming branches in the face of the dawn 330

Modern Women and Men and murmur in the quiet stretches of the night. The stars inspire them, and the storm is their dance. Advancing years enrich them-make them more stately beautiful. Entwining their arms, their lips, their branches, they grow old in beauty, strength, and companionship.

And for the mistletoe-no matter how fat and sleek it may be, there is no realization of the joy of being a tree! 

If you love, respect, and admire the rich man, yes. If you would marry him regardless of his financial status, if you are prepared to enter into the institution of marriage with him, prepared to manage his home, have children, accept this particular place in the scheme of things, yes. 

The struggling and striving has its compensations. 

Struggles make us strong, not hard. And strength is beautiful. When women realize this, when girls are taught this, there will be no more scheming and contriving by women to attach themselves to some man. Hand in hand, men and women will again wander free over the rocks of ages and their footprints will make marks in the clay of custom-together. 

You Must

Avoid 

Exaggeration 

AS WOMAN's intelligence emerged from the inertia that custom imposed upon it her first instinct was that of exaggeration. 

Her very disgust for the attitude she had always been forced to take toward men caused her to try to deny them any importance whatsoever in her newly found and beautifully independent life. 331

Lovely Ladies 

But we have progressed. We no longer attempt what is not only impossible but even ridiculous and a bit dull.

There was at one time the woman who, in an attitude of defiance, adopted a masculine attitude in her manner and gestures and, worst of all, in her clothing. But this type of woman is rapidly disappearing.

It was inevitable that we should have swung to an extreme in our frantic effort to take our intelligent place alongside of men, but these exaggerated outward gestures are no longer necessary, and an intelligent woman should not fail to realize that they are actually harmful to her and impede her progress through life. 

The Real Modern Woman 

AS ELLEN KEY points out so perfectly in all of her writings, the truly modern woman is not this creature of the masculine gait and the deep bass voice, but the woman who has made it quite evident to the world in general, and to herself in particular, that she is entitled by her intellect to take her rightful place in the world without losing a fraction of her womanliness and charm. 

Charming womanliness, plus a developed intellect and a sense of fairness and sportsmanship, make the true modern woman. And this is the woman that men respect. She has all of the worthwhile feminine qualities and arts that have ever been known to woman, together with those masculine ones which men hitherto have considered their own sole rights and privileges. She does not have to affect intelligence. 332

Modern Women and Men 

Only as a woman can you command your rightful place in the world. And only by according men their proper place in life can you win their respect and assistance, their recognition of your intellectual equality and possible superiority. Study your masculine psychology, and while catering somewhat to its whims, never overlook your biological importance in the sociological scheme. 

Never for a moment consider men of any more importance than woman; neither try to make yourself, a woman, more important to life than you are. 

Men and women are intellectual equals. Life offers us the same invitation to glorious combat that it offers any man. Your mentality is not less than his, and if you can't bend steel bars across your chest or bite chains in halfno matter! There are other and more important things to be done in life, and these things you can do. Why attempt to oust men from their rightful place in the scheme of things? Intelligent women don't want that place! They want their own place—and need men's help in getting and keeping it! 

There Must Be

Men in Your Life 

IF YOU choose marriage and motherhood for a career, then men, as they are expressed to you in one man, are going to be very important in your life. If you have chosen business as a career, then men, as they are expressed by the men for whom you work, must likewise have a certain importance for you. In fact, as life is now, intelligent women must admit, however much they may 333

Lovely Ladies dislike to do so, that men are indispensable to heir success and happiness. We can no more exclude men from our world than they can exclude us from theirs. 

If you are one of those women who antagonize men you are making a serious mistake. The modern woman's life does not revolve around men. Nor does she fashion her character and appearance to please them. She is charming, beautiful, and womanly for her own sake-but in so being it is inevitable that this should appeal to her men—“her men" meaning all of the men with whom she comes in contact. 

And being a charming and lovely woman with the intelligence to accord men their proper place in her life, with the sportsmanship to appreciate and act upon her biological duty to the race, she inevitably has the love, success, honor, and appreciation that is rightfully woman's. 

More than half a century ago Olive Schreiner wrote:

I thought I stood on the border of a great desert and the sand blew about everywhere. And I thought I saw two great figures like beasts of burden of the desert; and one lay upon the sand with its neck stretched out, and one stood by it. And I looked at the one that lay upon the ground, for it had a great burden on its back, and the sand was thick about it, so that it seemed to have piled over it for centuries. 

And I looked very curiously at it. And there stood one beside me watching. And I said to him, "What is this huge creature who lies there on the sand?" 

And he said, "This is woman; she that bears men in her body." 334

Modern Women and Men 

And I said, "Why does she lie there motionless with the sand piled around her?" 

And he answered, "Listen, I will tell you! Ages and ages long she has lain there, and the wind has blown over her. The oldest, oldest, oldest man living has never seen her move: the oldest, oldest book records that she lay there then, as she lies there now, with the sand about her. But listen! Older than the oldest book, older than the oldest recorded memory of man, on the Rocks of Language, on the hard-baked clay of Ancient Customs, now crumbling to decay, are found the marks of her footsteps! Side by side with his who stands beside her you may trace them; and you know that she who now lies there once wandered free over the rocks with him." 

And I said, "Why does she lie there now?"

And he said, "I take it, ages ago the Age-of-dominion-ofmuscular-force found her, and when she stooped low to give suck to her young, and her back was broad, he put his burden of subjection on to it, and tied it with the broad band of Inevitable Necessity. Then she looked at the earth and the sky, and knew there was no hope for her; and she lay down on the sand with the burden she could not loosen. Ever since she has lain here. And the ages have come, and the ages have gone, but the band of Inevitable Necessity has not been cut." 

And I looked and saw in her eyes the terrible patience of the centuries; the ground was wet with her tears, and her nostrils blew up the sand. 

And I said, "Has she ever tried to move?”

And he said, "Sometimes a limb has quivered. But she is wise; she knows she cannot rise with the burden on her." 

And I said, "Why does not he who stands by her leave her and go on?" 

And he said, "He cannot. Look—” 99 And I saw a broad band passing along the ground from one to the other, and it bound them together. 335

Lovely Ladies 

He said, "While she lies there he must.stand and look across the desert." 

And I said, "Does he know why he cannot move?" 

And he said, "No." 

And I heard a sound of something crackling, and I looked, and I saw the band that bound the burden on to her back broken asunder; and the burden rolled on to the ground. 

And I said, "What is this?"

And he said, "The Age-of-muscular-force is dead. The 

Age-of-nervous-force has killed him with the knife he holds in his hand; and silently and invisibly he has crept up to the woman, and with that knife of Mechanical Invention he has cut the band that bound the burden to her back. The Inevitable Necessity is broken. She might rise now." 

And I saw that she still lay motionless on the sand, with her eyes open and her neck stretched out. And she seemed to look for something on the far-off border of the desert that never came. And I wondered if she were awake or asleep. And as I looked her body quivered, and a light came into her eyes, like when a sunbeam breaks into a dark room. 

I said, "What is it?” 

He whispered, "Hush! the thought has come to her, 'Might 

I not rise?"" 

And I looked. And she raised her head from the sand, and I saw the dent where her neck had lain so long. And she looked at the earth, and she looked at the sky, and she looked at him who had stood by her: but he looked out across the desert. 

And I saw her body quiver; and she pressed her front knees to the earth, and veins stood out; and I cried, "She is going to rise!"

But only her sides heaved, and she lay still where she was. 

But her head she held up; and she did not lay it down again. 

And he beside me said, "She is very weak. See, her legs have been crushed under her so long." 336

Modern Women and Men 

And I saw the creature struggle: and the drops stood out on her. 

And I said, "Surely he who stands beside her will help her?" 

And he beside me answered, "He cannot help her: SHE MUST help herself. Let her struggle till she is strong." 

And I cried, "At least he will not hinder her! See, he moves farther from her, and tightens the cord between them, and drags her down." 

And he answered, "He does not understand. When she moves she draws the band that binds them, and hurts him, and he moves farther from her. The day will come when he will understand, and will know what she is doing. Let her once stagger to her knees. In that day he will stand close to her, and look into her eyes with sympathy." 

And she stretched her neck, and the drops fell from her. And the creature rose an inch from the earth and sank back. 

And I cried, "Oh, she is too weak! she cannot walk! The long years have taken all her strength from her. Can she never move?" 

And he answered me, "See the light in her eyes!"

And slowly the creature staggered on to its knees. 

Thus wrote Olive Schreiner more than fifty years ago. 

And a half century has passed, but passed many times more rapidly than any previous half century. Woman's first awful struggles are over. She is yet weak, centuries must unroll before her footsteps will again leave marks alongside of man's.

A half a century has passed and another woman takes up the Dream. 337

Lovely Ladies 

But man did not help her.

His face rumbled into a laugh because he did not understand. She was futile and ugly in her struggles. 

And she looked at the earth and the sky, and at him who stood by her, and she lay down on the sand as before; as before, the ground was wet with her tears, and her nostrils blew up the sand, and in her eyes was the terrible patience of the centuries.

But without the weight of the burden of subjugation tied to her back with the broad band of Inevitable Necessity the thought came again, "Might I not rise?"

And slowly the creature staggered onto its knees, slowly, but a fraction less slowly than before. 

And he who stood beside her watched her; but when he saw that she was lifting one knee to put her foot on the ground he became frightened and moved away, tightening the cord between them and dragging her down. 

And again and again the two creatures trod this cycle. 

And every time the moon rode high in the sky, the creature bowed her head nor even tried to struggle. And every time that her young cried out she lay quiet and attended to his want. And always in her eyes was the terrible patience of the centuries.

Yet her struggles made her stronger, and the time came when he who stood beside her could no longer throw her down. 

And when she was strong enough to stay on her knees the thought came to her, "Might I not stand?" But when she lifted one knee she drew the band that bound them, 338

Modern Women and Men and he who stood beside her moved farther from her, and tightened the cord between them so that she fell upon her knees again. 

And every time the moon rode high in the sky the woman bowed her head, nor even tried to struggle. And every time her young cried out she stopped to attend to his wants.

But she stayed on her knees and did not lie down again. 

Slowly the creature struggled onto its feet. Then she looked at the earth and she looked at the sky, and she looked at him who stood by her: but he looked out across the desert. 

And she looked down at her feet, and saw that which she had left on the ground in her struggle, and heard its cry. Then she stooped low to attend to it. And she was infinitely weary. And the hollows in the ground where she had lain so long, and the soft sands thick about it were so inviting that she laid herself down again. 

And the light died out of her eyes, and her nostrils blew up the sand. 

And he who stood beside her looked at her, and was pleased. 

But in a little while she lifted her head and saw him gazing out across the desert. And she remembered the pure air she had breathed when she stood that little moment beside him, and the light came again into her eyes like the breaking of a sunbeam into a dark room. 

And slowly the creature staggered to its feet, nor forgot this time to keep the child in its arms. 339

Lovely Ladies 

And the years rolled by, and the moon rode in the sky, and the child cried for attention. But her struggles made her strong, and by the time she could stand on her feet the child was no longer a burden to her, but stood beside her and helped her.

Then man ceased to pull against her, and laid his hand upon the child's shoulder.

When she had stood upon her feet awhile she took a step. 

And the veins stood out, and the drops came out on her, and she staggered.

And at last man stood close to her, and looked into her eyes with sympathy, and took her hand. 

But the way was strange and hard, and often she would look back at the outline of her body in the sand. And in her eyes would be uncertainty and doubt, and fear of the unremembered and unknown.

And at such times he who walked beside her would push her away, and she would stumble and stand still.

But she did not lie down again, and man would find it lonely without her, and take her hand. 

And again and again the two trod this cycle. 

Yet although the light in her eyes often wavered, it never died, to be replaced by the terrible patience of the centuries. 

And although the creature wearied, and often looked back longingly at the outline of her body in the sand where she had lain so long, she did not forget the pure air she breathed as she stood, nor again laid her down to 340

Modern Women and Men again blow up the sand with her nostrils and to water the ground with her tears.

Slowly and often painfully the creature moves on. 

Through the Age-of-nervous-Force, toward the Ageof science that will free her from the tyranny of the moon and the physical burden of her young.

And in the centuries to come, she who now walks haltingly will again wander free over the rocks with him who walks beside her, and, as in the uttermost beginning, her footsteps will mark the clay side by side with his. 341

Chapter III ENGAGEMENTS AND TRIAL MARRIAGES 

MARRIAGE is a profession, and failure in the profession of marriage will leave more lasting scars than failure in any other profession.

It is exactly because the matter was that desperately serious that some wise old heads of vanished generations devised the custom of engagements. Engagements are novitiates-periods of trial for both men and women. 

All of the religions of all of the world have found it necessary to have a novitiate, trial periods of spiritual testing, to strengthen the decision of the novice, or point conclusively to the fact that he or she did not wish to enter the institution. Trial marriages, wisely advocated by many 342

Engagements and Trial Marriages to-day, serve this same purpose-to assure two people that they are suited to live together. 

Let's discuss that period of intimacy between a man and a woman that all intelligent people make a prelude to marriage. 

Such a short while as twenty-five years ago a discussion of fiancés and engagements would have seethed with carefully thought out schemes for the prolonged deluding of future husbands-the while intimating that when once a man was safely led to the altar this unnatural strain of being sweet, charming, winsome, courageous, helpful to

Mother, always neatly and attractively dressed around the house, et cetera, could be safely lessened. 

Perhaps to-day this idea may continue to exist in the "backwoods"-and in backwoods' minds. But it never has and never will exist in the mind of any true woman who has even a grain of character and sportsmanship in her make-up!

And that's not all. We modern women with our better understanding of sportsmanship know that putting the "best foot forward" to our fiancé is dishonest. And its being a trickery and deception it will eventually turn on you, what can you gain by doing it? 

When you are engaged don't be a wax image. Don't be an angel. Be yourself. Remember, the engagement is to give you an opportunity to know your fiancé, and to give him an opportunity to know you. How will he know you if you pose for six months before you marry him?
