Suppose you do pose. You're fooling yourself every bit 343

Lovely Ladies as much as you're fooling anyone. For if you pose before marriage you can't keep it up long afterward. You must let down sooner or later and show your real self to your husband. And then, horrible thought, he'll probably discover that he's in love with the girl he thought you were. 

But you aren't that girl. She doesn't exist, and your pretty romance in all likelihood will end with mutual expressions of regret, rather than strengthen into a beautiful, lifelong love of real inspiration and worth. 

GIVE your fiancé an opportunity to know Fairness you as you truly are, or as you are truly determined to be because of your love. Make the period of your engagement as romantic and charmingly lovely as you can possibly make it, for it is a time that has a sweetness of its own unlike any other sweetness that you will ever know. Try not to deny either yourself or your lover its full beauty, but see that it is a real and not a false beauty that will only shatter into drops of bitterness after marriage. Turn the beauty of your love into the strengthening of your character. Gratefully soon this strength will become a part of you, if you but determine upon it. 

Oh, the shattered lives that would be saved if young people could but realize that the intimacy of marriage does not work miracles! True, it often has a temporarily soporific effect upon the faults of one party and the critical faculties of the other, but this effect is soon dispelled by the serious light of reality.

And the realization of this leads me to make a very 344

Engagements and Trial Marriages emphatic statement. It is this: Love alone, among intelligent, courageous, and clear-thinking people, is not sufficient justification for marriage! 

You should love your husband, but you shouldn't marry the first man you happen to fall in love with. Love is terrifyingly beautiful and bewildering. But it is in no wise a disgrace to admit an attraction for a person who is temperamentally unsuited to entering into marriage with you. There are so very many more things than love that must be taken into consideration when two people plan to be together for life, to have a home, to raise children. 

Modern Means To-day 

How long it takes us to realize that the safe-conduct rules for life and living propounded by philosophers and wise men of the past day for the masses who could not or would not think for themselves are no longer applicable to our modern intelligence!

If you are one of the mob, if you are unable to think for yourself and plan your own life in an intelligent, reasoning way, then by all means follow the accepted ideas of love and marriage as they have been blindly followed by your unthinking ancestors for centuries.

Marriage a Profession 

BUT if you have education and intelligence adequate to the Twentieth Century, realize that love and physical attraction are things apart from the very serious profession of marriage. To marry merely for what you hysterically justify as love in the intensity of your temporary desire, in spite 345

Lovely Ladies of really serious reasons why the marriage is detrimental to your own life or to that of the man you are marrying or to the children that will come from this union, is selfish, stupid, and unworthy of a modern, intelligent woman. 

Of course, in saying that love is an insufficient reason for marriage, I mean "love" liberally interpreted. And "love" thus interpreted may mean merely physical attraction. 

It may mean admiration. It may even mean nothing more than awe. To be quite candid, when the average woman decides that she "loves" a man she more frequently is using this idea of love as an hysterical justification for the intensity of her desire to know the man better.

Beware of this. Realize that marriage will outlast physical desire. It will outlast admiration based upon pose rather than true understanding. It will outlast any petty and mean desire for material security or comfort. 

And engagements—as trial marriages-are quite simply opportunities for two people to know each other better. 

Certainly this is necessary. Two people may use the same words but give them different shades of meaning. Each viewing a subject in a different light, they might yet think, until they understand one another well, that they are viewing it in the same light. And so one finds understanding necessary to any prolonged intimacy. 

The consideration of fiancés not only is extremely important, but it probably is one of the least understood problems which the modern woman must meet and solve. 

I've heard a hundred abstract and extremely logical 346

Engagements and Trial Marriages reasons for the high percentage of matrimonial barks which yearly break asunder on the rocks of discord. And it seems to me that the single important fact is that not one man or woman in ten seems to have the slightest conception of how to go about selecting a mate. They simply leave it to chance or passion-or greed-or some other equally absurd force. No wonder they get themselves all tangled up in ridiculous circumstances!

Consider the temperament of your fiancé. Consider his background, his character, his likes and dislikes, his social position-everything. And while you are doing that give him the opportunity to see you as you are-not as you would be.

Sugar-coating character ugliness certainly can never eliminate it. 

Marriage is a partnership. Let your partnership be an honest one, cost what it may. For any other kind is base and deceitful, and a greater dishonor than the breaking of any convention. 

Do You Marry the Man?

THERE is just one more point which we must consider. The engagement is a period of trial. Things which are perfect at the start do not need trials. The hypothesis is plain. Having come to the end of your period of engagement you are faced with two courses of action. Either you marry the man-or you don't.

Women, again, have allowed themselves to be made the victims of an irrational and tyrannical idea. They have, against their own consciences and better judgment, per- 347

Lovely Ladies mitted the growth of a convention which almost requires a woman to marry the man to whom she has been engaged, and, in fact, views a breaking of the engagement as something of a black mark against the woman. 

Nothing could be more absurd. If you're going to.let custom oblige you to marry the man to whom you're engaged, whether you find him suitable or not, or indeed whether or not he finds in you his conception of an ideal mate, why bother about an engagement at all?

A broken engagement is not a disgrace, nor an admission of defeat. If you have the slightest suspicion that the man you are marrying is not quite the man you thought him to be, is not, after all, the companion you wish for your life, is not the man you wish to be the father of your children, you must have the courage and strength of character to break off your engagement. It is far better to break an engagement than to go through all the heartache of a divorce.

After 

Marriage 

It is well to remember that nowadays the return from the altar is not considered a prelude to a pleasant relaxation on a bed of hard-won laurel leaves. Well does the modern woman know that, with life and laws as they are to-day, merely marrying a man does not keep him. You can gain nothing for yourself or for your future happiness, nothing for your marriage and its permanency, by deception or by deluding yourself regarding the nature and temperament of the man you are marrying. 348

Engagements and Trial Marriages 

But you can gain everything, for yourself, for the man you love, and for your children, by directing the current of love toward the strengthening of your own character and disposition, and by analyzing clearly the personality and the disposition of your fiancé. 349

Chapter IV HONEYMOONS 

June comes and the moon comes 

Out of the curving sea, To hang like a golden bubble 

High in the lilac tree. Jove 

JUNE and honeymoons! 

Is there a woman alive who has not, in the after years, wished that she had made her honeymoon even more beautiful and romantic than it was? 

Retreading the dream - filled hours, hand in hand with the beloved, one smiles again, sobs again, thrills again at remembered joy or pain. No matter what the years that follow bring, the honeymoon lingers ever in the mind and in the heart. 

If only all young brides could realize the indelible impression that the honeymoon will make both upon themselves and upon their husbands! Actualities must pass as Time swings with easy stride over meridians, but the memories of actu- 350

Honeymoons alities will linger on and continue beautiful for many, many years. Therefore let your honeymoon be so completely perfect that it always will remain one of your most beautiful memories, and one of your husband's as well.

Only with ignorance and lack of poise on your part could your honeymoon possibly be unbeautiful. And if you allow this to happen, you will always regret it, some day realizing that you overlooked a single opportunity that could never come again, to paint an exquisite memory picture.

Only a few short years ago a honeymoon was a dreaded bridge which must be crossed between girlhood and wifehood. It was anticipated with thrills, but nevertheless it was dreaded. The modern girl anticipates her honeymoon with thrills-but she does not dread it.

The first coming of two different personalities into intimate contact should for no reason be a terrifying experience, fraught with embarrassment and fears. Such it very often was in the past, and many a woman has suffered shocks on her bridal night that resulted in psychological scars which completely warped her life, and in the sequence of consequences, the lives of her children. 

Ignorance Is 

Not Purity 

THE modern girl avoids this because she knows what life expects of her as a wife and a mother. And if she does not know she finds out. Nor does she, as did her grandmother, delude herself dangerously into believing that ignorance is purity. Ignorance is not purity. 351

Lovely Ladies 

If, for any reason, you do not know theoretically what the marriage relation is, find out from a woman friend or your physician. Do not burden your husband with ignorance. There can be nothing beautiful or romantic in ignorance, and only with the knowledge of what is expected of you as a wife can you make your honeymoon the beautiful interlude that it should be. 

Do not allow the relationship between a man and woman to be presented to you vulgarly, neither too idealistically. But insist that things be told to you simply and directly, and be sure that you understand.

A Present 

BEFORE you start on your honeymoon I am going to give you a present. It is a very valuable motto and one which I have been saving for this very moment to speed you on your happy way. 

Learn it and apply it always. "The Desire to Understand is the Only Virtue!” 

Think it over, and don't ever forget it! 

Mentally prepared for your honeymoon by intelligence, you must, of course, prepare yourself materially. This will take some real thinking on your part, but let's show your new husband that you can be efficient by taking just enough of the right things-not too much of anything. 

Take a sufficiency of pretty underwear and accessories, but not too many, and not all impractical ones. Diaphanous negligées decidedly have their place in the honeymoon wardrobe, but their place certainly is not going to 352

Honeymoons and from the dressing room of a Pullman, or to the bath on a boat.

And do have the good taste to wear a chic but practical traveling costume even though one less suitable might be prettier.

Careful About 

Luggage 

A LOVELY lady would never be without every necessity for comfortable and intelligent traveling, and especially on her honeymoon! And here is a secret. Men hate to be cluttered with a lot of little pieces of hand luggage, so if you are going to a definite place for a definite length of time be sure to take a trunk or even two. I assure you that he would rather attend to baggage checks than to wrestle with "parrots and pups, hat boxes and flups.' 99 Surely it is not necessary to remind you of your personal daintiness. But it may be necessary to remind you that your efficiency and poise, your sportsmanship, and especially your sense of humor, must never fail you at "crucial moments." There never yet has been a tense moment which could not be shattered into laughter, nor an unpleasant situation which does not have its humorous port of escape.

I wish to mention here the beauty of intimate silences. 

A honeymoon is neither a visit nor a limited exhibition of personal charms. It is a prelude to a long, sweet song, and should set "a tempo." Do not let your excitement or nervousness cause you to flutter or chatter, and above all do not feel that you have to entertain your husband as though he were a temporary guest. 353

Lovely Ladies The Success of Your Honey moon Is up to You

A SUCCESSFUL honeymoon is one from which the husband returns more deeply in love with the woman he has taken for a wife than ever before-more appreciative of her, more determined to do all in his power to insure her happiness and the permanency of the union into which they have entered. 

The success of your honeymoon is in your own hands.

Of course there are many things which spring to my mind which I might suggest to your future husband, but then, this book is not written for him!

There are no rules for behavior. Just make every effort not to disappoint in any way, remembering that you are two entirely different personalities that are coming together, and that a million sensitive little tendrils have their roots in your separate heredities, environments, educations, and ideas. They are going to reach out and try to combine, and some may recoil, and others hurt as they touch. Remember that, and be tolerant. 

Lend to your honeymoon all of your grace and charm and ingenuity—that loveliness not of face and figure alone, but of character and soul.

Be modern, intelligent, and deeply wise. Doing this you may fashion, with your own free will and understanding, a beautiful thing which will be forever a treasured memory and an exquisite picture both in your own heart and in the heart of the man you marry. 354

Chapter V MARRIAGE OF TO-DAY 

pose, nevertheless.

ONE of the first gestures of outward defiance against the conditions that had been imposed upon women by men since the world began was one of pretended contempt for marriage. 

I say "pretended," because this actually was only a pose. Not a conscious pose, I'll admit, and one inspired by reaction, but a

The assertion by a woman that she does not want to marry and emphatically will not marry is only a protection against the world's opinion of her as a failure should she not marry.

Of course there are exceptions to this, but so few of them! 

The world's Bernhardts, Duses, and Isadora Duncans are not in the majority. But in other than such exceptional cases, when a woman says she does not want to marry, this statement is only a protecting and convenient cloak under whose kindly folds she can hide her thwarted feminine instincts should the time come when she would find it necessary to do so. 355

Lovely Ladies Annoyances and Responsi- bilities Now a woman can sincerely say that she does not wish to assume the responsibilities of marriage. This is quite true. But given the opportunity of marriage free from the burdens of diapers and dish-washing, with a man possessing congenial qualities in harmony with her own, what woman can honestly and truthfully say that she does not want to marry?

Every normal woman wants love-love in a mate who will enable her to express her womanhood in the feminine biological functions of reproduction. And it so happens in this day and age that marriage is usually necessary to this. 

Lovers and

Careers 

MANY modern women feel that careers either in business or in the arts, and a lover or lovers, are preferable to marriage and its possible complications. 

Are they? No career has ever or can ever supplant fundamental, instinctive motherhood. (Again I remind you that there are exceptional cases, such as when, for some physical or psychological reason, a woman cannot have children.)

The modern woman must realize that, no matter how great her intellectual development, she cannot in one century or ten thousand subjugate her natural feminine instincts, and that her life cannot be really complete until she has fulfilled the urge of her womanhood. And though there may be some question as to just what "natural feminine instincts" are, there can be no question 356

Marriage of To-Day as to whether motherhood is a natural feminine instinct or not.

Woman must reproduce herself or the human race perishes. But she need not do so to the exclusion of everything else. There is no more reason why a woman, any more than a man, should give up her entire life to the having and raising of children.

Modern Marriage MARRIAGE and having children and rais ing them can and should be part of the modern woman's life, not her whole life. 

Just as marriage and raising children is a part of a man's life, never his whole life.

Look at marriage sensibly, not hysterically. Accord it its proper place in your life, admit it as the means of fulfilling womanly desires and instincts. Man did not assume an upright position in a day-let us modern women use some of this intelligence of ours to realize that we cannot thwart Nature nor bend her to our ideas in a single century.

Sex and 

Intellects

LET us develop ourselves physically as well as mentally, physically as women, mentally as sexless intellects. And let us use our intelligence to show men that we are quite willing to fulfill our biological duties of womanhood, that we are, and want to be, the mothers of the race, but that we realize at long last that being the mothers of the race has a parallel with their being the fathers of the race. When they are ready to help us to accord marriage and motherhood its 357

Lovely Ladies proper place as a part of our lives, then we are ready and willing and eager to be their wives. "So You're 

Going to Be Married?" "SO YOU'RE going to be married?”

The question, or rather the statement, for it's more of a statement than a question when they put it that way, may be freighted with a hundred shades of meaning. Some sardonic friend, perhaps slightly embittered by a previous unfortunate experience, or even lacking the ability to rationalize clearly, will fire this broadside at you with a weakly humorous twinkle in his or her eye and a wry drawing up of the corner of the mouth. Each of your friends or relatives will view your coming marital excursion according to his or her lights. Not one of them will suggest that you shouldn't marry, mind you, but they'll give you only about one chance in a thousand to make a success of the business. 

All of which should give us pause, should it not? Is marriage, after all, such a serious job that only a genius can make a success of it?

You Can Make a Success of

Marriage 

YES-and no. Marriage is a serious business, of course. But it doesn't require genius.

The institution of marriage is an old one, and perhaps you never have had the time to sit down and give a little calm thought to just what it is and just what it means. 358

Marriage of To-Day 

Marriage is essentially a social institution for civilized people. A successful marriage is a high privilege on one hand and a badge of intelligence on the other. For in the institution of marriage, more than in any other institution, it is necessary for the partners, husband and wife, to realize their obligations to each other and their joint obligation to the rest of mankind-which latter is the same as saying their obligation to the institution of marriage. 

Civilized human beings have reared the institution of marriage and endorsed it for their own protection, to insure the propagation of the species as Nature intended. 

Certainly, therefore, any two people who invoke the benefits of this institution are thereby obligated to support it and to insure its permanence.

Why a Woman 

Should Marry 

WHY should a woman marry?

The answer is because, according to present-day standards, it is compulsory with having and raising children.

Cannot a woman have and raise children without marriage? Certainly she can. But, as we have said elsewhere in this book, no person has a right to impose his or her ideas, however sane and clear-thinking they may be, upon others who have not yet reached that plane of thinking. Marriage is necessary, then, according to presentday standards, whenever your relations with a man are such that they may have any effect upon others. Let me make that clear. Your personal conduct is your own particular business so long as it does not influence others. Whatever 359

Lovely Ladies you do of your own volition, so long as you can reconcile it with your own conscience, and so long as it does not interfere with or injure any innocent person, is right. 

Yet nothing is right that hurts others, and unions that result in children without the men and women having gone through the ceremony of marriage do hurt others, however stupid and unfortunate it is. 

Not to marry cannot but hurt your parents, your relatives, your children, and all others who are incapable of understanding your interpretation of the ceremony of marriage. Therefore, we, even the intellectually free, marry.

Now that we understand marriage and 

Your Marriage see it as a very important cog in the great machine of civilization, let us take up the question of your own particular marriage. You see I have said "your own particular marriage." Perhaps that will surprise you, and you may resent the seeming liberty of my taking it for granted that you want to get married or perhaps are married. The fact is, however, that you do want to get married. And so do all of your women friends.

Marriage is as natural to the intelligent modern woman as it was to the inhibited female of a hundred years ago. 

The only difference is that the modern woman realizes why she wants marriage and truly divines what it means to her, while the other woman accepted marriage much as she did the fact of her being born into the worldas something which must be, although she had nothing to do with it. The old, rusty iron gates of tradition are creak- 360

Marriage of To-Day ing more loudly on their hinges. They are opening outward, and revealing to women the promised land of the right to elect her own career. But despite the fact that women no longer have to marry to be respected by mankind and to take their proper place in the community, the fact also remains that the career of marriage is the one which makes the strongest appeal to the intelligent woman. Free to choose her path, she naturally selects marriage, because it offers her a more glorious and complete future than any other. No career can be of such tremendous importance, nor give to a woman such complete satisfaction, as the instinctive and fundamental career of motherhood. 

From now on this chapter will be particularly apropos to the woman who is already married. 

A MAN gives a woman his name, his pro- Coöperation tection, and his support. It isn't so much that he actually gives all of these things to her as it is that he gives them to the institution of marriage. The man supposedly provides the necessities, and the woman presumably handles them wisely.

A man has a certain part to play in marriage, and a woman has another. But she is not doing it for him, and he is not playing his for her. They are doing it for marriage and for what this represents. If more people would only realize this, fewer marriages would be unsuccessful. 

There is another thing that a woman should realizethat she must pay attention to her side of the bargain and give her husband the chance to do the same. If he is the 361

Lovely Ladies good sport that she should have known that he was before she married him, he will. 

Are you giving to marriage all that good sportsmanship requires? Were you cognizant of just what this was before your marriage, and if not, have you since learned it, and are you doing it? That is all that need actively concern you. Too often we are so busy telling ourselves sadly what our husband is doing or is not doing or should be doing some other way that we entirely overlook our own part of this matter. Do your own mental housecleaning and let your husband take care of his.

Our 

Obligations to the Institution 

JUST what is it that a woman owes to marriage? What is it that good sportsmanship requires of her in regard to it? 

First, before you can answer this question you must realize that I said: "What is it that a woman owes to marriage," not, "to her husband."

A woman's part in marriage is quite naturally to make a sound, permanent institution of the thing! To make and preserve the sanctity of the home and therein to perpetuate the race, mold, feed, and spank her little part or parts of it and set their tiny feet firmly in the path that they should go.

Specifically, a woman has a certain part to play in the institution of marriage. Such platitudes as "A woman's place is in the home," are as obsolete in their entirety as the bustle. Yet the actual essence of the thing stands, that a woman's place—a married woman's, of course-is 362

Marriage of To-Day in the home, managing it, making of it a frame around her own personality, and, unless unusual circumstances warrant her not doing so, raising her children in it and making of it a sanctuary for her husband. Yet always remembering that these things should not take all of her time and intelligence.

A woman's part of marriage is always the same, nor does it matter in the least if some man loves and desires her greatly enough to assure her that "you can do anything that you please when you are my wife." His ideas on the subject do not in any way mitigate her obligations in marriage.

Understanding the Keynote 

MARRIAGE requires understanding. With understanding, for instance, there can be no such thing as jealousy.

A jealous woman is a woman who is not sure of herself. 

Jealousy is inferiority. You simply cannot be jealous of another woman in your husband's life unless you distinctly feel that she is in some way superior to you. You resent this, and the result is jealousy. 

Jealousy is indeed a fundamental primitive instinct, but so also are many other atavistic things which culture has taught us to eliminate from our lives. If you cannot reason jealousy away, you can at least keep it to yourself. 

When you show your jealousy to another person you have given that person a certain power over you. A clever woman will never show herself to be jealous of anything or anybody that is interesting to her husband. Just as surely 363

Lovely Ladies as she does, it will make it twice as hard for him to relinquish the thing that is hurting her. 

And jealousy is a thing that you need not tolerate in your husband. If only you had, before he was your husband, and at the first instance of it, very definitely let him understand that you would not tolerate jealousy in your life, it would have died a natural death. But if at the time you were not experienced enough to do this, then you will have to do it after marriage, by the simple expedient of not paying attention to it whatsoever. I don't mean by laughing at it or sneering at it or talking about it. I mean actually not paying attention to it, not even to the extent of laughing at it.

Evolution of Personality 

Fascinates

INSURING the permanency of your mar riage is very important. What woman, or what man, either, does not realize the importance of this, in these days! "Hearts do not break, they sting and ache❞—until anyone of the many million thrilling new interests that life holds for her children of the Twentieth Century will creep in, and they do not ache quite so much. 

Beware allowing some other interest to supplant you in your husband's mind. But beware also of gagging him with your personality. Real companionship, the absolutely indispensable part of marriage, is based upon a mutuality of interests and a continuance of their development. It is no one's fault but your own if you lose your husband's interest; of course, you cannot be more than one person 364

Marriage of To-Day or more than one type of woman, but avoid the personality which, like a phonograph record, however interesting and beautiful it is when played the first few times, becomes tiresome and even exasperating after it has been played from here to there and from there on. 

An intelligent woman can never be so well known to her husband that there is a danger of boring him, simply because she herself never ceases to be interested in things and is consequently interesting to everyone around her. What was good enough for her father not only is emphatically not good enough for her, but what was good enough for her last year is no longer good enough or interesting enough for her this year! 

And then, my dear friend husband, don't fail to ask yourself every now and then if your interesting wife, who so pleases you by not boring you, finds you as interesting!

And now I wonder if it is necessary to remind a lovely lady here that her loveliness, being such an essential part of herself and of her life, cannot under any circumstance or for any reason be neglected? The things that I have told you in the first part of this book, and which you now realize and have incorporated into your life, should not for an instant be relinquished by you when you are married. But if for any reason your instinctive fineness is not sufficient to urge you to be ever lovely and dainty and womanly, then you must look at it from the angle of sportsmanship too. 

Your husband married you because you appealed to him as you were. Do you not think that you owe it to him 365

Lovely Ladies to continue to be that way in your appearance, if not to improve it? And do you not think that you owe it to him also to demand that he continue to maintain the wellbred appearance that he had before you were married?

You cannot demand that he continue the little everyday courtesies and thoughtful attentions that he once gave you, but you can inspire them. Think that over. 

Happy Mar riage Not En tirely Dependent on Money to make and 

POVERTY or wealth are merely accessories to marriage. We are inclined to accord them too important a place. The success of the marriage does not depend upon material things. Naturally it is not as easy keep a pleasant home when it seems as though life is just one thing after another-new shoes for Charles, a schoolbook for Mary, Jane's tonsils, Daddy's glasses, and fillings in your teeth that you cannot neglect any longer, and so on, seemingly ad infinitum. But none of these is sufficient excuse for an unhappy marriage. The really worthwhile woman who realizes her obligations will merely give the best of herself and way beyond, as many thousands of women have done and will continue to do for generations to come. 

Unpleasant or difficult relatives by marriage, prolonged illness, an unexpected number of children, ennui, or any other reason for dissatisfaction, is no excuse for a lack of sportsmanship. One must make the best of everything.

Try to handle unpleasant situations in the best possible manner, and be sporting enough not to add more un- 366

Marriage of To-Day pleasantness. When a situation is unendurable there is always some way out, and until you find it there is no use whining about it.

The Married Woman and Her Employ ment

ANOTHER aspect of marriage presents itself to the married woman who works. 

Occasionally this employment is by choice, other times it is necessity. But whatever it is, the mere fact of a woman's being employed a certain number of hours a day is no excuse for her to neglect her home and her husband. Really a couple shouldn't marry at all unless they are certain that they can afford to do so, or are prepared to encounter and laugh off difficulties that they know will arise if they are not economically free. The woman who feels that she must work in order to help out the family expenses and then does so grudgingly is a very poor sport. Our lovely lady couldn't be that!

Modern women give to marriage that which they know is sporting. Play your part courageously, encouragingly, and unflinchingly, bringing your humor and understanding to bear on any awkward situation. If you do all of this, and if as well your husband is the man that you thought he was when you married him, and that you found him to be after your "trial marriage" (modern for engagement), you will assuredly make a success of your career of marriage. 367

Chapter VI MOTHERHOOD 

"A woman has two smiles an angel might envy; The smile that accepts the lover before the words are uttered and the smile that lights on the first-born baby and assures it of a mother's love." 

-HALIBURTON. 

ONE of the favorite excuses for not having children is that there are already too many people on earth, despite the toll taken by war, accident, and pestilence.

This is a very poor excuse, and fine-charactered women know it. They realize that there are not enough people of a certain type, a type that can only be evolved by the inheritance of certain qualities and standards, and by training in the environment produced by culture and breeding. Attempted Excuses

THERE are two other excuses for not having children. One is the fear of physical pain, and the other is a perfectly legitimate distaste for the relinquishing, even though temporarily, of one's individuality, both physical and mental, 368

Motherhood on a speculation that the intellect of the child for which you give it up will warrant it. 

Both excuses are weak. Science has made pregnancy safe and practically painless. The number of deaths from childbirth in America are being reduced daily. This is a point upon which we women should not deceive ourselves. 

That bearing a child is a dreadful, harrowing ordeal is merely a superstition. It has always suited the purpose of women to make a huge hue and cry about "going down into the valley of death," in order to bear a child. Why not admit it to yourself at least?

Every real woman understands and appreciates her obligation, not only to herself and her husband, but to society, in this respect. And there are very few either physical or psychological reasons that actually justify a woman in not having children. Other than these reasons, you know in your heart that you actually have no worthwhile place in the scheme of things if you do not have children. You simply are an enemy to life.

Motherhood is a social duty not to be feared. Medical science has reduced its hazard to a minimum, and intelligence does the rest. Just a simple, easily understood routine will assure you of a not unpleasant nine months and a safe consummation.

As to surrendering your individuality, no modern woman does this. For proof you can witness innumerable examples of successful women who are more than successful mothers, nor has it been entirely a matter of nurses, governesses, and tutors, who can at best only attend to 369

Lovely Ladies the more superficial needs of the little one. The woman who has made a success of her career is sure to make a success of raising her children. 

De Gustibus 

HAVING children is neither selfish nor unselfish. One woman will have as many children as God will give her, with no idea whatsoever of how she can care for them either mentally or physically after she has them, and will spare no opportunity to express her scorn of her sister who has no children, labeling this lack as colossal selfishness. Whereas the clearthinking woman, whatever the reasons for her own childlessness, calls this prodigal child-bearing on the part of the mother selfishness. And so it goes.

Selfishness in having children is due to the happiness that they bring. For it is true, as the moralists say, that there is no greater or more poignant joy known to man than that of seeing portrayed in the little life that he is going to rear the image and likeness of himself, and in weeding out all the bad habits and qualities that he perceives in himself, and in developing all of the worthwhile ones.

Parenthood MOTHER-WORSHIP no longer endures mothers and fathers are respected nowadays because they are worthy of respect as individuals. 

Yet, parenthood has lost none of its sacred responsibilities, even if it has lost some of its mysteries. It has always been necessary to have children to preserve the race, and to-day it is more necessary than ever for cultured people 370

Motherhood to have children in order to preserve culture and increase the intellectual ranks. Well-bred

Children 

AND IT IS the intellectual and talented woman who should have children! The most serious danger to our present civilization is the fact that its cultured class, those who have the means to give children any educational advantage, are the very ones who contrive to find elaborate reasons for not having them. Statistics show that from the union of two individuals of the so-called better class there are seldom more than two children, usually only one. Whereas from the union of two people mentally or financially incapable of giving a child a sound and healthy body and mind, and the inherited and acquired characteristics of culture, there are from three to six children. It is upon the shoulders of those women who have the character and mental development conducive to the bearing and rearing of fine, intelligent children that the present-day burden of motherhood rests. Motherhood has become a glorified necessity, an intellectual undertaking. 

Children are like love, and they are love. They bring with them much joy and much sorrow. Yet without a child no woman can truly round out her spiritual nature and develop all of its loveliness. The need of a child is not purely a selfish physical one, and not purely a racially altruistic one. The need to create, to reproduce one's self, is great, fundamental, and instinctive, and no sophisticated rationalization will ever destroy this need. 371

Lovely Ladies 

To-day 

So you are going to have a baby! 

Perhaps you do not believe, as many scientists do not believe, that you can assist in forming that child's character before it is born. Still, you cannot be sure that this is not true. Therefore, don't you think that, since the possibility that you may be able to do something is tremendous, you owe him at least every mental, physical, and spiritual opportunity that you can give him during these uncertain prenatal months?

First, I think, you must prepare your body for the biological changes it is going to undergo. Starting this very day, you can so prepare it that you will have the easiest delivery possible, the most comfortable nine months, and the most rapid and complete recovery afterward. After all, there is no reason why the having of a child should take your youthfulness from you, or your bodily beauty.

In fact, with the proper care your body will be more womanly and more beautiful after you have accorded it its natural function of child-bearing than it could have been before.

Morning Care 

IT IS understood that if this is your first experience with childbirth you will consult a physician immediately. 

For the first six months of your pregnancy do not under any circumstances, no matter how you feel, neglect your morning exercises. Do not, of course, do any of the exercises strenuously, and only do those that you can do lying in your bed. Certainly do not do any that you know will 372

Motherhood 

prove a strain on your abdominal muscles. But do all that you possibly can to strengthen these abdominal muscles. 

After your morning exercise follow your usual morning routine, and this you must also under no circumstances neglect, for if you do the harmful effects, sadly enough, may be permanent.

The very first thing upon awakening in the morning, drink a full glass of unsweetened and undiluted orange juice and eat one thin piece of dry toast, chewing it very, very thoroughly. Then do not move at all for half an hour. Just about one week of this plus a little mental effort and determination will do away with the morning nausea. You can have your orange juice and toast prepared the night before, and you will just reach over and take it, moving your body as little as possible, and not moving it at all afterward. Morning nausea is distressing, disgusting, and harmful to the child as well as weakening to you. And there is no excuse for it. 

And now, after you have attended to your morning beauty routine and after you are dressed for the day, you breakfast. You must remember that it is a fallacy and nothing more that you must "eat for two." You must do nothing of the kind. You must simply pay attention to nourishing your own body correctly and carefully, not overloading it with too many calories or undernourishing it, thereby laying up for both yourself and the child years of illness. Eat if anything a smaller breakfast than you have been accustomed to, but under no circumstances forget the morning fruit, either fresh or stewed. 373

Lovely Ladies 

Cut down your allowance of coffee a little, but not to the point where it makes you uncomfortable. And eat less breakfast than usual, simply because between your usual breakfast time and luncheon time you drink one or two glasses of rich milk and eat a few graham crackers. With this milk, you see, you are getting part of your two quarts of liquids that everyone should drink each day, but that you absolutely must drink during this time. 

During the

Day 

DURING the day, and preferably right after breakfast, you must take a walk. Of course, you have discarded your Frenchheeled shoes, both to minimize the danger of falling and to assure your body of a more natural posture. During your walk breathe good fresh air, stimulating your heart with fresh blood and your mind with interesting thoughts. 

Take a good walk, but not one that will tire you.

Luncheon must consist of plenty of vegetables and fruit! The instant that you have one experience with any food that does not agree with you, eliminate it from your menu. And do not, above all things, eat very much starch (found mainly in bread, rice, spaghetti, macaroni, pastries, flour-thickening for gravies and soups, potatoes) and do not eat very rich food, very highly-spiced food, or very greasy food. Even if you like these things, control your appetite for these few months-you have all the rest of your life in which to eat them, and only these few months in which to give your little one its chance for a healthy, happy existence. 374

Motherhood 

After lunch, and especially if you have already taken your morning walk, is the very best time for your nap.

You must, of course, sleep a little more than usual during these months, but not to the extent of keeping yourself in a semi-somnolent state of mind and body. Somehow, strangely enough, it's easy to do this, as though old 

Mother Nature just wanted you to dream away these gestative nine months on her ample bosom. Physically, perhaps, this is all right, but mentally it is harmful to the child. If sleeping in the daytime interferes with your night's repose, do not do it.

After your nap, your recreation. These afternoon hours are the ones in which you discipline your mind. Spend them with music, in art galleries, in museums, in studying -in short, doing whatever will help you to form that child within you in the shape of mental and spiritual beauty. 

Impossible? Perhaps. But you are not sure that it cannot be done; therefore why not give it a chance? 

Eat again in the middle of the afternoon. Refreshments in the form of hot or cold milk, fruit, graham crackers. 

Little cakes if you wish, but not too many and not heavy or rich ones. 

Then dress for dinner. During these months you should be closer than ever before to your husband. You can make yourself lovely to him, aided by the aura of mystery that your condition gives you. Instinctively, too, he will realize that after the child is born there will be another to share your affection for him, another to claim a great deal of your time. He will treasure these months before in his 375

Lovely Ladies mind, if you make them months worth treasuring. Make yourself lovely every night, for him, for yourself, for the child. It is not too much effort to dress for dinner, even if dressing consists of being en négligé. 

Your dinner must be supper. Not a heavy mealluncheon must be your heaviest meal during these months. 

And after dinner a little pleasurable relaxation, and to bed rather early, especially during the latter months of your condition.

Before you retire you must give yourself all of the beauty aids that you need, and even more of them. Then drink a glass of warm milk, arrange your toast and orange juice by your bed, and water to drink during the night if you want it. And if you need it, take a mild laxative every night. Then repose, with plenty of fresh air and a tranquil mind tinged with that delicious reverence and expectancy one always feels at the awesome mystery of creation that is being performed within the temple of one's own body. 

Special Suggestions THE preceding suggestions are a matter of routine. But here are some special things that you must consider and do. 

First of all, your teeth. Have all the dental work you need done the first months of your pregnancy-in fact, at once. It is possible that your teeth will suffer from the lack of lime that is going into the child. You can supplement this in your diet, of course, but it is also wise to have all your dental work done at once.

Drinking sufficient liquids to irrigate your bladder and 376

Motherhood offset the various kidney troubles that are the special danger of pregnancy is very important. Oddly enough, water may nauseate you. If so, you must have a not very sweet fruit juice on hand to mix with your water—or charged water, which if you find too expensive to drink outright you can mix with plain water. Also, remember that you can take quantities of liquid in the form of milk, bouillon, tea. But two quarts a day is the minimum for safety. 

This means a glass an hour. And for your own sake and that of the child, see that you do take this much liquid at least.

Elimination is sometimes quite a problem when you are pregnant. You cannot afford at any time to have faulty elimination, this cause of all causes for illness, for embonpoint, for unsafe pregnancy. You must watch this every day. If eating the quantity of vegetables and fruit, which not only act as a laxative, but supply you with minerals, carbohydrates, and vitamines that you and the child need, is not sufficient, you must take a regular and mild laxative like mineral oil, or something that your doctor will prescribe for you. You must not take purgatives—there is never any need for them, either, for you must never allow yourself to be constipated. If all else fails, as sometimes happens, owing quite simply to the pressure of the fœtus on the rectum, you must take a daily enema of warm water, but not much -a cupful is enough. Retain this as long as you can, for an hour or so; this will be less weakening and more natural than a severe enema of a quart of water that is expelled at once, although this too may sometimes be necessary. 377

Lovely Ladies 

This is always preferable to purgatives, however. Watch your elimination, both of the kidneys and of the bowels, as this is very necessary to a safe pregnancy and to the health of the child, as well as to your own future health. 

Two months before your expected confinement you must start hardening your nipples. This will save you literally days of agony when you first commence nursing the infant. Massage your breasts lightly every morning with a little cocoanut oil to soften the skin, so that the skin will not break when they are stretched with the milk, and treat the nipples by pinching them rather hard, daubing them with a saturated solution of salt in water, very cold, which you must allow to dry on them. 

Beginning with the third month of your pregnancy, rub your abdomen, buttocks, and thighs with the cocoanut oil to soften the skin and prevent it from breaking when it stretches. Remember these breaks are ugly and will never go away entirely. Be sure to do this. 

If you take the proper exercise, and especially if you take your morning walk, you can be fairly assured of not having badly varicosed veins. If you should have them, however, do not neglect them but have your physician prescribe the proper and immediate treatment. 

Buy a good maternity corset, even if it is expensive, and commence wearing it at once-one of those adjustable ones which you loosen as it becomes necessary—and wear also a very good bust-supporter during your pregnancy and more especially while nursing: one that actually 378

Motherhood supports, holds up the muscles. If you can't find one write to me and I will tell you how to make one.

After you have finished nursing the child you must give your breasts some rather special attention. There is no excuse whatsoever for their losing their original contour. 

You will simply need to massage them for a few weeks with cocoa butter and slap them rather firmly with very cold water for about ten minutes every morning. Before you use cocoa butter you might rub them with alcohol diluted with water, or toilet water. 

Don't wait until the last minute to go to the hospital.

And do by all means go to a hospital. It is expensive and difficult to deliver a child in your home, especially the first one. And such a thing as "scrambling" babies in the nursery in reputable hospitals is so rare that it has become nothing more than myth. 

Take with you to the hospital all of the things that you will want and need. Don't keep all of your friends constantly bringing you this and that. Make your preparations in a leisurely, intelligent, lovely-lady fashion. 

Then prepare your mind. Efface the idea from your mind that this is any especial ordeal. That idea is nothing more or less than a superstition. Don't lose your sense of good sportsmanship. To be actually philosophical about it, think of it as something that you are going through and have to go through, so why not take it with the least amount of anguish, both mental and physical, to yourself and to those around you?

Of course, it is suffering, and it is quite bad suffering. 379

Lovely Ladies 

And of course it is, as we are so fond of reminding our husbands, something that they never have to experience, and probably "couldn't stand if they did have to." But what do you mean, anyway, by this "couldn't stand" business? There is no such thing as not standing it, because you are going to stand it and are standing it, and it is just about ten times less terrible than you have all your life been led to believe it was going to be. 

And then you must think of the little one at this time, especially. If you lose your nerve and become hysterical and are unable to assist the doctor and the nurse with this, your own problem, the child is likely to have to pay, either with its life or for all of its life, with imbecility or physical scars. Anyhow, losing control of yourself isn't going to help you a single bit, or make anything any easier or less painful, and it's rotten sportsmanship. 

If you were alone on a desert island, perhaps you could allow yourself to be a little beast and shriek your fool head off. But good heavens, you are not, and you must think of the nerves and feeling of those around you, who are there for the express purpose of helping you. We women are too fond of our little ideas, and too successful with our little subconscious tricks, to want them exposed. Have all of the "delightful agony" that you wish over this birth, if it serves your purposes to do so, but don't kid yourself about it. 

You will have a bad few hours, I admit, and you will suffer. But this suffering will not be anything worse than a real bad tummyache; so make up your mind to it this minute-but don't let hubby get hold of this part of the book! 380

Motherhood 

It has been truly said that there is no pain that a woman feels so much and yet forgets so quickly as that of childbirth. Literally, that is not true, because there are other far worse physical pains, and mercifully we forget all pain when it is finished. But for the average woman, yes, I admit that it is pain that she will feel the most deeply in all of her life, and forget the quickest. For there is no such joy as the indescribable one of gazing upon your firstborn.

Conclusion 

THE part of this outline routine that applies to all expectant mothers is first, the part entitled Morning Care; second, all of the diet suggestions; third, the suggestions about sleep, fourth, the paragraph about dressing for dinner; and then all of the things you will find under the heading, Special Suggestions, through to the end.

As outlined, of course, the foregoing is the ideal existence for you before your child is born. But perhaps you have not so arranged your life that you can have this routine? 

In that event, certain parts of it will have to be altered. 

For instance, you may be a business woman and intend to continue your work for a few months. Do not, I beg of you, do this for more than four months, no matter how great is the economic necessity-even if you feel strong enough and think you can do it.

You may already be the mother of a small family and be unable to devote as much time to this new life as I have suggested in the outline you have just read. Or, and I 381

Lovely Ladies hope not indeed, your social life may involve a great deal of your time.

Please remember that you owe it to this little life that is in the making, and which you are bringing to life without its permission, to devote to it the very best of your time and all that is in you, physically, mentally, and spiritually for these nine months. You can turn it over to a governess, if you wish, as soon as it is born; but no one can do anything for it now except yourself. 

Give the very best that you have to this child that you are bringing into the world-your very best and a little more. It is only for nine months of your life, but it is for the whole life of the child, you know. Is it too much to give it this fair chance, physically, mentally, and culturally? 

Certainly it is not. So let's just do it-you will never, never regret it! 382

Chapter VII FOR MOTHERS 

TOPSY "jus' growed," and that plaintive little statement has wrung many a heart-throb from sentimental maternal bosoms whose own children are doing nothing more nor less than the same thing!

For merely taking care of the child hygienically, and then sending it off to kindergarten, is letting it "just grow"-albeit in a slightly different environment than that which little Topsy must have encountered.

Having brought a child into the world, you owe him a great deal more than food and lodging and the education he receives in the average school. You owe to him the molding of his character, his ideas, his fancies, the nurturing of his talents, and his intellect. Habits and ideas that it perhaps took you years to acquire, experiences that you have had at great pain, humiliation, and cost, you owe to this little one. 383

Lovely Ladies Unnecessary Knocks 

FOR the modern mother has not the mistaken and antiquated idea that it requires hard knocks to form character. She has discovered that, more often than not, these hard knocks crush any claims to individuality or intelligence that the person who receives them might have had, and robs him of that fine poise and courageous attitude that is the heritage of the free-born. 

Courage you must give the child, yes. And you must teach him to depend upon his own intellect, in his passage through life. You can make a very great mistake indeed by allowing him to depend so entirely upon you that he does not develop any character or initiative of his own. 

Disciplining 

Yourself and the Child IN A sense the character of your own child is within you. It is believed that hereditary tendencies can be mitigated or strengthened, eliminated or diverted. If you have been able to detect certain character weaknesses either in yourself or the child's father, you will be able to detect them in the child as well, if he has them. And you must develop the strength of will that is necessary to control these in your own child, even though you have not been able to control them in yourself. 

Heredity is one thing, but the effect that environment and early training have upon it reduces its disastrous possibilities. 

IF YOU find a character weakness in your child, your pointing it out to him accusingly, scolding him about it, 384

For Mothers Wise Mothers and perhaps even punishing him for it, is going to do the very thing that you hope it will not do. Any child can have its bad tendencies sublimated and directed into other paths, and all of its good ones developed. But it takes a wise mother to do this. And unless you do so, you are not doing your duty as a mother.

It is nothing short of a crime to let children "just grow. 

Unless unusual circumstances warrant it, it is another crime to send them away to boarding school. You can, of course, put a great deal of their care into the hands of a competent governess, but you must know that she is competent, and you yourself must discuss with her the tendencies that you want averted in your child, and the particular things you want developed. 

Right here I want to say a few words about punishing children.

Corporal punishment is a relic of barbarism. A child who has his own sense of sportsmanship developed in him when young will not need any punishment other than his own conscience. And if he should require sterner discipline, this may take the form of the deprivation of certain liberties. Punishment is intended to cause the offender to think about his error, convince himself that it was wrong, tell himself why it was wrong, and resolve not to repeat it. Punishment is more often a fault on the part of the giver than a benefit to the recipient. If you would realize that the slap you administered to the child was the result of your own irritated nerves it would help 385

Lovely Ladies you to develop your own self-control. Look into your heart and analyze well your reasons before you punish your little one, and, perhaps, inflict upon his soul a scar that will hamper his progress for his entire life. 

Important Years 

THE first seven or eight years of a child's life are the most important ones and the ones to which you must devote your careful attention. Too many mothers think that these few years are just the growing ones, not important from anything but a physical standpoint, and have a rather vague idea that the child's character can be formed and developed after he is twelve years old or so. By that time, alas, the child's habits and characteristics are very well formed, and we realize that we have lost the only opportunity we will ever have truly to mold him. After twelve a child can only be guided.

Dangers 

BUT in this molding of your child's habits and appetites do not overlook the importance of teaching him to use his own intelligence and initiative. Very grave consequences may arise from allowing a child to depend so much upon his parents that he does not develop his own initiative. 

You may, for example, keep the children spotlessly clean with the assistance of the laundress and governess, but personal habits of cleanliness, and in the girl child, especially, daintiness, can be taught a child at such an 386

For Mothers early age that they become actual habits for life. Make cleanliness a pleasant routine rather than a repugnant task, and inspire appreciation of it in the child. The little one can get as healthily dirty at play as any other child, but he is the one who should know that he must be washed before sitting down to the table. And he can and will, if you will teach him to think about it himself. 

The sad thing is that the average mother does not teach her child these things. She does these things for him and then scolds him half-heartedly when he does not do them himself. A child can actually be taught to take care of his own clothes when he is two years old. Consult his wishes in the matter. Ask him if he thinks he is clean enough and neat enough to sit down to the table or go for a walk. In an astonishingly short while you will have taught him to think about it; it will have become a part of his subconscious, and he will actually feel uncomfortable when untidy, even as we do ourselves. 

The Proper 

Perspective- 

Yours and the

Child's 

IT IS very difficult to keep the proper perspective on one's own children. Yet it is very harmful for them not to do so, since the child is unable to appreciate his own merits and recognize his own shortcomings until later years. 

Do not let appreciation of your brilliant son blind you to the fact that he is heading toward an unpleasant egotism. 

Guide him into the paths of self-analysis, so that he will be able to draw the line between self-appreciation and 387

Lovely Ladies conceit. He will never realize that he is an egotist until you bring the fact to his attention. Nor is it wise to bring it to his attention by merely saying to him: "You are such a little egotist!" or by telling your friends in his hearing that "Reid is such an amusing little egotist!"

Help him to know himself-his failings, his abilities~ and he will gain from that knowledge assurance and poise. 

Children should be allowed neither to undervalue their own talents nor magnify their own importance in the scheme of things. And it is the wise parent, indeed, who neither crushes a child's individuality nor permits him to become self-centered. 

Do not let little daughter's attention to her clothes become fussy, thereby restricting her mind to unimportant trifles and details. Teach her from the time she is two years old to take an interest in them. Ask her what dress or what ribbon she would like to wear, and then explain to her why the selection is inappropriate to the occasion for which it is being selected, if it is. But do not allow her to feel that what she wears or how she wears it is of supreme importance in her life. And she will not think clothes a matter of supreme importance if you instill in her correct dress habits early in life. It is only the woman who is never sure of what to wear and how to dress who is over-attentive to her clothes.

Read the chapter on Beauty and the one on Exercises. 

Realize that while probably a number of things are quite new to you, and the habit of them perhaps difficult to acquire, you can teach them to little daughter now. 388

For Mothers 

Formal Education THE mother's responsibility does not end at home. The child, if attending day school, spends little time at home-sleeps and eats there, hardly more. If the child is attending a private school there is even less of that close personal contact between mother and child which may mean so much to the child's future welfare. So many mothers, too, send the child off to school with a sigh of relief and do not even bother to ascertain whether this matrix of the child's character is harmful, let alone beneficial. 

What kind of an education is your child getting?

The fact that you are merely sending him to a good school, or a school with such a reputation because Soand-So's son goes there or because it has been recommended to you by such-and-such a person, should not suffice you for your child.

Schools, as you know, at their best can only be average. 

Naturally they have to cater to such a variety of talents, of intellects, and personalities that they cannot pay special attention to any one child. 

Obviously, schooling is a necessity. Nowadays one seldom has the opportunity or the means to have a child taught at home under private tutorship. Nor is this, for various reasons, consistently desirable. But, for a child with any claim whatsoever to talent or personality or unusual intelligence, the average schooling is not enough. 

American parents have a very mistaken idea that they must not "overcrowd" their children. European children go to school from seven o'clock in the morning until five. 389

Lovely Ladies 

Yet a nervous breakdown is an unknown thing in Europe!

American children speak one language, European children never less than two and usually three or four. American schools overlook or disparage a proficiency in the arts, European children of the better classes are taught the creative joys of literature, the pleasures of music and a knowledge of it, and an appreciation of, if not an actual facility in, art. 

In "Old"

Russia EVERY cultured child of the old Russian aristocracy (which has been replaced by ideas) had a knowledge of all the arts and was proficient in several of them. Why? Simply because music, the bodily expression of rhythm called dancing, the expression of thoughts in words called writing, and the expression of emotions in pigments called painting were considered essentials of knowledge rather than luxuries or superficialities.

Playtime THE average child is permitted to waste hours upon hours in idling and play which is of no benefit whatsoever, physically or mentally. Can you not see the possibilities of so regulating a child's development that every waking hour would be an aid?—a certain amount of time for play in the form of exercise of a specific nature, a specified amount of time for study, sleep, and food.

In their formative years children learn rapidly and easily. A healthy child's every instinct is toward absorp- 390

For Mothers tion, whether this absorption be facts or fancies. And childhood is a period for absorption and for growth, physical and mental. 

Nine to three are the average American school hours. 

Count the remaining hours until bedtime, and you will be surprised to see how many there are. The average child plays more or less aimlessly during this period. What does your child do, and what might he be doing? Music 

HE MIGHT, for one thing, be learning to play one or two musical instruments. And who has not regretted that he or she was not obliged to learn more music? Naturally, as children, we begrudged the hours spent at piano, but as adults we are thankful for them. 

Any child can be taught to play a stringed instrument whether he has marked talent for music or not. Starting him very young on one or two instruments will rapidly develop any latent talents for music, or at least the appreciation that is necessary to the life of any well-bred person. 

Writing for Pleasure

THE child who is taught to write for even fifteen minutes a day in a diary will rapidly and instinctively learn to express his thoughts in writing. Give him confidence in this writing by promising him solemnly that you will never under any circumstances ask to see it, and thus encourage him to put down his most intimate thoughts, reactions, and ideas.

His style of writing and interpretation of ideas will improve 391

Lovely Ladies almost daily, and it will be he himself who will improve them by reading over what he considered from year to year his "childish thoughts." 

And Painting and Sculpture 

ART school, to anyone who has never attended it, is a closed book of mystery. 

Yet the only mysteries attending painting or sculpturing are technical ones. Every child should be given an opportunity to see whether or not his selfexpression lies in this form of interpretation. And an hour a day of time that would be otherwise spent in idle play is not too much of an investment in this field of interest. 

The Wise 

Mother 

OPEN doors for your child!

Teach him the working basis of every art, thus enabling both of you to see whether or not he has any special talent in any direction. 

Do not try to excuse yourself for not doing so on the grounds that you are waiting for him to show indications of a so-called "special talent." Burdened as he is with all of the superficial details of his average education, the chances are that he will never show any. Balanced

Education 

NATURALLY I am not suggesting an exclusive education in the arts. Neither can an education which excludes them or admits the possibilities of only one or two be called complete. It is entirely possible to supplement many of the admittedly useless courses that are given merely to fill up 392

For Mothers credits with worthwhile special studies that will be of inestimable value and pleasure in later years. 

Don't unquestioningly accept any educational system against your better judgment. With just so much time allowed for studying and so many years in which to do it, don't let your child waste these years and this time, which you know from experience can never be recovered, in assimilating useless knowledge. There is, of course, some question as to just what is "useless" and what is not, but that is something which you have to think out for him and for yourself.

Your responsibility to your child does not end by merely giving him good food, a good home, and a generous allowance. The most pitiful children in the world are those who are left to study and "think for themselves" without being taught how to think for themselves. 

You are the one to teach the child how to think rather than the child's schoolteacher. For if you are a proper mother he will come to you with what may seem to you unimportant but to him very serious and grave perplexities and problems-problems that can't be solved by teacher or textbook, as our major problems cannot. 

Try giving your children an hour's class with Mother every day! You will be astonished at its effectiveness.

Remember that modern children respect their parents no longer merely because they are parents, but because they are parents who as individuals merit such respect. 

I want you actually to think about your child, this little life that you have in your hands. It is a serious business, 393

Lovely Ladies this of raising children, the most serious business of life. 

And certainly the most of it falls upon the mothers. Unless you are fully prepared to give that child the very best of your intelligence, unless you are fully aware of your duties toward him, you have no right to have him. And until all women realize these things, and do them, the human race will continue to fumble and stumble toward the light, impeded on all sides by its own follies and inextricably involved in correcting its own errors while making others.

What a wonderful race could be developed, and how rapidly evil would disappear, if every mother realized the important rôle she plays in the progress of humanity! 

Is your child just average? If so, give it an average education, food, clothing, and a bed. Otherwise, think—and teach it to think. Only thus can you develop that intelligence or talent or personality that you see in it, which makes it more than average. Only thinkingly can it be diverted into the ways that will make it, when grown, gloriously fulfill your dreams. 394

Chapter VIII MODERN HOUSEWIVES 

Jare 

THIS is a profession that is actually more important and serious than any other profession peculiar to women. Unfortunately it is not thought of as a profession. 

If you are a housewife you only realize it when you make out a passport, or when the census taker appears at the door and asks your name, age, birthplace, and occupation. Then you hesitate and look blank-while the census man rapidly fills in the last space with the word "housewife."

The young bride hardly realizes that she is accepting the position of housewife probably for life. But it is so. 

And the position grows on one gradually. One is a raw apprentice for a while, then one is efficient, then sometimes one becomes expert. Also the position grows in responsibility-the first year or two only the managing of the little home and the adored hubby's meals; the next 395

Lovely Ladies year the added thrilling and often vexing problem of managing a little one as well; and so on. 

The House wife Must Be a Manager 

WHETHER you have a maid and a cook, or both of these and a housekeeper as well, you yourself must manage. And the chances are that you will have no help and consider yourself fortunate if you can send the laundry out and have a cleaning woman to help once or twice a week.

And so you will find that the business of being a housewife is quite serious after all. You will immerse yourself in cookbooks and spend delicious hours planning tempting meals, and trying new things over the new and shiny stove. You will probably spend other hours sweeping, dusting, and making beds, with your hands carefully protected with gloves, and your hair in a towel. But gradually you will realize that the business of housewifery is not merely in doing, but in doing and getting it over with. 

No bungalow or apartment of the average size should keep you occupied more than the morning if you manage correctly. Then you will have the afternoon to yourself until it is time to get dinner. 

You don't think it can be done, do you? Well, it can be done, and I know it, because I have done it! 

It takes efficiency, and one eye out, as it were, for every little labor- and time-saving device. After all, there really is no reason why you should linger over your bed-making, or shell peas with the care you would bestow on diamond- 396

Modern Housewives cutting. Do them both extremely well, of course, but get it over with. 

How about a little schedule?

First, let's put down the things that have to be done every day. Then we will put down those that have to be done every two days, those that have to be done only twice a week, and those that have to be done once a week. 

Every Day 

EVERY day you must cook, wash dishes, make beds, tidy up, and shop for food. 

That means that you have to start with breakfast.

Breakfast can and should be your simplest meal, but nevertheless your most agreeable and artistic one. The eating of a large breakfast is an injurious habit that can be overcome.

Always have everything prepared the night beforeyour little table set very prettily, your fruit prepared, coffee in the pot ready to put on the stove, or whatever else you have. And this goes for breakfast for two or breakfast for ten.

It is so easy to set a breakfast table attractively! Try to afford a special breakfast set of dishes-they are reasonable and so pretty! And a dainty luncheon cloth with a couple of matching napkins will do a great deal more towards helping Junior with that breakfast cereal than the formidable linen of the day—and perhaps hubby might be inspired to bring you roses, if you put just one in a slender vase every morning at breakfast!

Then breakfast dishes-into the dish pan they go to 397

Lovely Ladies soak, while the teakettle goes on the stove to heat the water for them, unless you have running hot water. 

Of course, when you left the bedroom in the morning you opened your bed wide, the closet doors, and then all of the windows. So the beds are quite aired during breakfast. Therefore you make the beds while you are waiting for the breakfast dishes to finish soaking and the water to heat. Learn to save time and steps. Don't rush to the kitchen to turn the gas out when the water is boiling and then return to finish the beds. If you know from experience that you are likely to have to do this, turn the gas under the water very low to begin with. 

And then after the beds are all made-the dishes. 

There is an efficient way to wash dishes, so you might as well learn the right way once and for all. Start with the glassware and a dish pan full of warm, soapy water, which need not and should not be warmer than is comfortable for your hands. Wash all your glasses and glass dishes and put them in a wire basket to drain while you wash all of the most fragile dishes in the same water. Now put these aside in another wire hamper to drain, or in the same one, if there is room, and wash the rest of the dishes. Meanwhile you have heated more water for rinsing—and you always rinse your dishes, not only to take off the soap but to make them easier to dry. Then your silverware, and this also put in a wire basket to drain. Don't skimp on your wire baskets, and don't crowd things into them. Last of all, your cooking utensils. Now wash out your dish pan and dry it at the same time with the dish cloth, hang it up 398

Modern Housewives on a nail, and then wash out the greasy sink—and wash the grease out of your dish cloth before you put it away. 

Before you go one single step further, wash your hands with clean water and soft soap that you have handy for the purpose. (Castile soap-shaved into hot water, shaken well, allowed to jell, and kept corked in a wide-mouthed bottle is excellent.)

Now pour clean boiling water over your glasses, then dry and polish them carefully with a perfectly clean dish towel, which you then hang up to dry while you use another towel for the dishes. Now more boiling water over the silver, perhaps a dry towel to dry it, and there you are. Your cooking utensils you have dried mostly with the dish cloth, of course, and the least fresh of the dish towels will remove what moisture is left. 

You will have to wash your hands again with the soap and water, and always try to finish with cold water unless you are really too cold at the moment to stand it. Then the lemon juice. Lovely ladies automatically save squeezed lemons for this purpose. Lastly, your hand lotion, a bottle of which you have on the sink always. 

Sounds simple, and a little bit silly, doesn't it? One of the irrelevant and irreverent visitors who insist upon imposing themselves upon me while I am trying to write this book has just looked over my shoulder and remarked in a somewhat astonished voice that it made him feel like washing dishes just to read this! It may sound simple, and may even sound silly to you, but I ask you how many 399

Lovely Ladies lovely ladies actually wash their dishes in this practical manner?

Marketing 

THE best part of it is that it didn't take half as long to do as it takes to tell! You are now ready to do your marketing, which always is more efficient and economical, and really a great deal more fun, than telephoning the order. Your shopping list is of course made up before you start, as well as your mind. Variations in this list will depend somewhat upon the things that you can procure for the day, but not much. 

And now you are back home again, and before you put your vegetables away you will cut off and throw away everything that you wouldn't use at any time and which would only be taking up room in the ice box.

Put lettuce, celery, and parsley into cold water at this point, and peel potatoes for luncheon. Put them also into cold water. This is a very good time to prepare any other vegetables or fruits that you are having for luncheon. 

Everyday routine, this. Unless you have more than three in the family it usually isn't necessary to do dishes three times a day. Twice a day is enough, and you can ascertain for yourself when that had best be-whether you skip the breakfast dishes and do them with the luncheon dishes, or whether you skip those at luncheon and do them with the dinner dishes. 

Immediately after luncheon prepare for dinner. Preparing for dinner and cooking dinner are two different things.
