They should never be done at the same time. Preparing 400

Modern Housewives potatoes, preparing the meat, and cooking fruit-all of these things should be done while you are still in the kitchen; then leave the kitchen and do not do another thing in it until it is time to start cooking dinner. 

An hour before dinner time start cooking dinner, at the same time setting the table, with your eye on the stove all the time, of course. 

After dinner, dishes again, and then prepare breakfast and arrange the table for it. 

I imagine by this time you are all in a muddle with your schedule, wondering when sweeping, dusting, scrubbing, washing, and ironing come in, but you see, we are only outlining the daily routine, the things that must be done every day regardless of these last-mentioned. The following do not have to be done every day, so we turn to them next.

Miscellaneous 

Days 

You do not have to sweep every day. That is, you do not have to sweep the whole house every day. The halls and the bathroom and the porch, if you have one, and perhaps the dining room you have to sweep every day—yes. But the bedrooms get swept one day, and the sitting room and living room may be swept the next. The carpet sweeper or the vacuum does the rest.

Sometime in the morning, whatever time is the most convenient, sweep the rooms that are allotted to that day, then dust the other rooms while the dust is settling in the rooms you have just swept, and then dust these. 401

Lovely Ladies 

Prepare the laundry and arrange for wash days as systematically. You will put the washing to soak in a good washing powder the night before you wash. The ironing the same. It is always sprinkled and rolled the night before. It is this intelligently planned preparation, and then more or less automatic but thorough execution, that makes for efficiency in your job. And in this way you have a great deal more time on your hands for club work, for reading, for entertainment and entertaining, and for all of the cultural things that you want to do, while your neighbor, with the best intention in the world, does the same amount of work that you do, but takes all day to do it in and probably doesn't do it as well. 

If your duties increase for any reason, as for instance the advent of guests or children in the family, you simply have to increase your rate of speed and your efficiency. And when you should have outside help, get it. For it is sad but true that no one, least of all those for whom you think you are doing it, will thank you for making a drudge of yourself and losing the charm and beauty they have every right to expect and demand of you.

Think of housekeeping as a profession—as a career. 

And think of how very much depends upon your successful handling of your job. The physical, mental, cultural, and spiritual welfare of your family depends upon it. Can you consider yourself a good sport if you fail in it? It is up to you to study the needs of your family and give them what they require. You are the incentive and the guide. The entire success of your career as a housewife and mother de- 402

Modern Housewives pends upon you. It is up to you to put your very best into it.

Don't let circumstance cheapen you. The real aristocrat bends each circumstance, each difficulty or hardship, to her will, and never allows herself or her will to forget that she herself is the important thing, the pivot around which her world revolves and upon which it depends. Should the burden be heavier than anticipated, that must not crush you, for this means ruin of your self-respect, the worst thing in the world which could happen to you. 

Dressing to

Your Part 

Now there is another important phase to the business of being a housewife, and this you must never allow yourself to overlook. It is that of dressing to your part. You would not go to the office or to school in your oldest and shabbiest dress, would you? Then why do you think you should do your housework in it? In these hectic days the clever woman will look her best at all times for her husband and her children.

In the first place, your house dresses can be entirely practical, eminently suited to the purpose to which they will be put, and at the same time be attractive and becoming. There is absolutely no reason why they should be shapeless, ugly-looking affairs masquerading under the title of practicality. For if they are ugly they are decidedly impractical and extremely expensive in their psychological effects upon yourself as well as upon your family. Practicality is merely a matter of colors and materials that 403

Lovely Ladies you can launder often and well and lines that do not hamper your working. These colors and materials can be becoming to you, and these lines can be modish. 

There is no particular reason why you need wear your most expensive silk stockings in the house, but neither do you need to wear, in an attempt at economy, stockings that would make a lovely lady weep with mirth. 

You can have two or three pairs of stockings to be worn in the house only, and wear them in the house only-but they don't have to be all of your castoffs and torn odds and ends which you couldn't wear anywhere else. 

Getting breakfast isn't such a messy job, the way we do it!

Therefore you can wear a pretty négligé for it. Then whisk into your yesterday's and perhaps not so very fresh house dress for the dishwashing-but with always a fresh apron in the offing to slip on when the doorbell rings. After your truly dirtiest work is done put on a fresh and slightly more elaborate dress, perhaps, for your marketing and for luncheon. And this, with the proper kind of apron, should survive the rest of the day until it is time to dress for dinner, something a lovely lady always does.

A little thought and experience will show you how many and what kind of dresses you need, how many detachable aprons, how many shoes and stockings. The days in which one used to put on any old dress for the housework have gone. Nowadays a young housewife is sartorially equipped for her job as she is for her evenings, for her sports, for the street.

The most practical and simple types of frocks are neces- 404

Modern Housewives sary for the early morning work! Short sleeves, short and not too tight skirts, a careful avoidance of pleats or ruffles or ties that flop into the soup and the dishwater at the most annoying moments. All of these things are inefficient and waste energy and your nerves. 

Do you know the kind of flat-soled sandals that children wear? If you do much standing or walking in the course of the day's work, you will be grateful to me for suggesting them. Put on your high-heeled shoes when you shop, if you wish, but wear these others for your work.

The proper and becoming clothing has a great deal to do with your success in your career of housewife. If you doubt it, give my suggestions a month's chance, that is all. See the effect it has on your family, your friends, your tradespeople, and mostly upon your own personality. Don't let your neighbors say of you, "Yes, she is a pretty little thing, but you should see her while she is doing her housework!" 

And apart from that, since a lovely lady doesn't care what her neighbors say about her anyway, there is just the psychology and good sportsmanship of looking and playing your part-of modern, lovely-lady housewife. 405

Chapter IX COOKING CAN BE INTERESTING 

FEW things have changed more in the last fifty or seventy-five years than our gastronomic ideas. To-day thousands of people in large American cities go to a soda fountain for luncheon and see nothing strange or unusual in it. Our forebears, however, gave a little more attention to this business of eating, and to pique your sense of humor, and also to illustrate what I mean, I am going to give you a little menu which I have culled from the dusty archives of yesteryear.

It was during the days of horse cars and tippets, and a goodly number of America's potential traction magnates assembled to discuss such important questions as whether or not the horse cars should be heated, what kind of horse was best for this innovation, and how the service might be improved all around. 406

Cooking Can Be Interesting A Little Menu 

FACING such knotty problems, it was natural that the magnates wanted to fortify themselves beforehand with a little snack, so, before getting down to serious work, here's what they had: 

Oysters, green turtle soup, striped bass en coquille, fillet of sole à l'Italien, saddle of Southdown mutton, fillet of beef aux champignons, Philadelphia capon with cauliflower, terrapin à la Maryland, sweetbreads with purée of asparagus points, fillet of chicken with sauce suprême, pâté chaud à la financière, champagne ice, canvasback duck, larded quails, woodcock omelet soufflé, tutti frutti, charlotte russe, wine jellies, cream tortoni, pistachio meringues, Gorgonzola and cream cheese, olives, pears, oranges, Hamburg grapes, Malaga grapes, nuts, raisins, figs, French fruits, candied fruits, ice cream, sherbet, and coffee.

This modest little repast was washed down with Hockheimer, Mumm's Extra Dry, and Château Rose! There is no record of any casualties, and after the boys hurried through this formality of eating they presumably fell to serious business and made decisions the results of which we are feeling to this day. 

Of course you may never be called upon to prepare anything like the above, and I wouldn't advise you to cut out this menu and rush off to stock your ice box against the possible emergency, but there really is no reason why you shouldn't give a bit of serious thought to this matter of cooking. 407

Lovely Ladies Why Not Get a Modern Cook 

Book? 

EVERYTHING that you do you should do well, and never more so than in your profession of housekeeping. Cooking, for instance, is an art. Yet why is it that so many women cook two or three meals a day, year after year, and never learn to do it well? There really is no ex cuse for it at all.

There are many cook books that you can buy, cook books which not only give you recipes and tell you how to put ingredients together, but tell you as well the actual food value and caloric content of the recipe per helping. 

With such things at your disposal, how can your intelligence allow you not to be a good cook? Vegetable Values 

ONE thing especially I would like to tell you here, even though this isn't a cook book, and that is how to cook vegetables so that they are appetizing and retain their valuable minerals, which is not possible when they are cooked less appetizingly. They must be cooked usually in a minimum of water, just barely enough to prevent their burning. 

Greens, for example, can be cooked in no water at all, since sufficient water remains on them from washing. 

Cook them in a tightly covered pot and over a very low gas flame. My, but they are appetizing when they are cooked this way! The bottom ones will cook first, of course, so stir the whole mess.

Do you know how the restaurants and cafeterias produce such excellent green peas and string beans? Simply 408

Cooking Can Be Interesting by scalding them with boiling water first, and then popping them into cold salt water and letting it come to a boil -not too rapidly, and with the cover off. Use just enough water to cover them, and this you finally let boil back into them before serving. Carrots too will become beautifully pink if you treat them in this way, and you won't have to tell the children that they are good for the complexion in order to induce them to eat them. 

Spaghetti, macaroni, noodles, rice, etc., are put into furiously boiling salt water, and, unlike the recipe for vegetables, there must be a great deal of water. This is the secret of cooking them without their getting slimy and pasty. And no cover on them, either. 

Beans Take a 

Long Time

BEANS, either brown or white, get soaked overnight in cold water and baking soda, which removes from them their gasforming propensities. And then they are cooked on a very slow flame all day long. You must have an asbestos mat for this if you use a gas stove. Try putting a couple of whole peeled onions, a clove of garlic, salt, pepper, and four or five bay leaves into brown beans for the first four hours of their cooking. Then put a fairly large piece of salt pork and a fair amount of molasses into them for the next four hours. Just before serving them, but after they are finished cooking, a can of tomato sauce, not ketchup.

Try parboiling cut onions in a very little water for a few minutes, letting the water boil back into them before you 409

Lovely Ladies flop them into a hot greased frying-pan to fry them. This way they will always fry a golden brown and be thoroughly done without burning. 

Sprinkle buttered toast crumbs onto string beans as the French do, and watch the children ask for more! Try the same thing with chopped spinach. Or save bacon drippings and melt with a little lemon juice into spinach. 

Or sprinkle with nutmeg. Carrots with nutmeg, too.

Green peas with chopped onions, boiled together, is another French dish. Cauliflower sprinkled with the buttered toast crumbs is good, too. 

By the way, cauliflower, cabbage, and brussels sprouts are robbed of their indigestibility by not cooking them too long, but by putting them into hot boiling salt water. 

Anyone who tells you that he cannot eat these three vegetables means that he has never eaten them properly cooked. 

Never peel onions without wearing rubber gloves! It is true that if you peel onions under water (which is far more trouble than wearing gloves) the odor will not cling quite so badly to your hands. If you should ever be so careless as to forget your gloves, sour milk or buttermilk in rubber gloves, worn for two or three hours, will take the odor away.

The Art of Piecrust 

PIECRUST making is an art. If you have not mastered it, practise until you do, but practise intelligently, with the proper recipes. A piecrust that has stayed at least twelve hours 410

Cooking Can Be Interesting in the ice box after it is mixed and before it is cooked is bound to be more successful than one that has not. 

Just little hints, these: one of the duties of a clever housewife is intelligent cooking. You must know food values, and how to employ them in relation to the family's diet. 

Not only if you do your own cooking, but more especially if you have a cook. For good cooks are not necessarily intelligent cooks! "Occupation-housewife." Manage your home as you do everything else, with efficiency and the minimum of expense to your loveliness-and have time for this loveliness the while! 411

Chapter X PATIENTS AND PATIENCE 

EVERY Woman should know the few fundamental rules for care of the sick. Especially so if she is a wife or mother. Children will have children's diseases, and doctors are far too busy and preoccupied to give you nursing instructions. Ignorance on your part at this time may have permanently ill effects upon the child for life. 

The very first requisite to proper nursing is cleanliness. 

Therefore, you must immediately clear the room of all little cluttering things, which not only catch and keep dust, dirt, and germs, but when a person is very sick are extremely irritating. 

I cannot impress this upon you too strongly, and if you are calling upon a person who is very ill and you find him in a cluttered-up room, straighten it out for him! There is 412

Patients and Patience no worse mental agony than to be lying ill in an untidy room, and it is dreadful to know that the people around us do not realize this. Don't ask the patient whether he would like you to straighten up the room; do it! And above all things don't make a fuss about it. Don't even let him know what you are doing. But throw out untidy newspapers, straighten up books, put medicines and sickroom things out of sight, rearrange and freshen flowers, chatting the while and cheering him, as you came to do. 

I don't think there is anything more really distressing to a sick person than wilting flowers in glass vases that show old water and slimy stems! Even inartistically ar ranged fresh flowers are annoying. Don't cram a sickroom with flowers unless each group means something in its own way in it own vase. Oh, the crimes that are perpetrated upon us in the name of kindness! 

Another important thing is quiet. You cannot realize how little, average, daily noises actually hurt the person who is ill. Try to realize that this is so, and keep persons and things around the patient quiet. 

The personal cleanliness of the patient is very important. 

When you are taking care of a person you must realize that he is probably too ill to think of these things or do them of his own volition. It is you who must think to bring him a wash basin with soap and water, toothbrush and paste, and brush and comb, in the morning and after every meal. 

And why shouldn't you know how to give a sponge bath to one in bed? If a patient is going to be ill for longer 413

Lovely Ladies than two days and really cannot get up for his bathing, you must, with the doctor's permission, of course, bathe him.

Sponge Baths 

For this close all the doors and windows and see that the room is not too cold. 

Have two bath towels, a sponge or wash cloth, soap, eau de Cologne or alcohol, and talcum powder. Throw all of the covers off the bed and cover the patient with a single detached blanket. Now lay one folded bath towel under one arm and shoulder. Wash it quickly with the soapy wash cloth, and not so much water that it drips. Then wash the soap off again and at the same time, dry the part that you have washed with another bath towel, rub quickly with the eau de Cologne or alcohol, dust with talcum, and cover well with the blanket. That's all there is to it. Continue, one part of the body at a time, and not over too large an area so that the patient might be chilled. When you are washing the patient's back, have him turn over on his side and give him a very good, sharp rubbing with the alcohol, to stimulate the circulation and relieve the weariness that comes from lying in bed for any great length of time. Food

CLEANLINESS, quiet, sleep, and food. Food is perhaps the most important. If you never were delicate or dainty before in your life, be so with the food that you prepare for a sick person and in the way that you serve it. What may seem to be a lack of appetite on the part of the patient, and perhaps even a dan- 414

Patients and Patience gerous one, may be nothing more than an instinctive rebellion against the way the food is served, and the patient himself may not know it.

It is just as easy to serve milk in a shiny glass, resting on a saucer, as it is to serve it in a cracked cup. It is just as easy, and in fact easier, for you to prepare a dainty tray than not to do so. 

And those thick slices of toast dripping with melted butter that the patient may have complacently eaten every morning when he wasn't ill must be made thinner and more golden brown and less buttery, or they will not be accepted. Greasy soup is one thing for a hungry kiddie just returning from school and another thing when he is ill in bed.

If a patient's appetite is feeble, don't show him a lot of food at one time, but tempt him with one thing attractively presented every hour or so. 

I must stop and tell you here how to make custard, which is the slickest way ever invented to get eggs and milk into a person! You simply beat up two fresh eggs to a pint of milk, add two rounded tablespoonfuls of sugar and a tiny bit of vanilla flavoring. Pour the mixture into custard cups, which do not need to be buttered, sprinkle the top with cinnamon and a bit of nutmeg, and put the cups into a very slow oven to cook until they are solid, which will be in about an hour. You must set the custard cups in a pie tin half full of water and add water to this as it dries. And unless your oven is very slow, the custard will curdle and not be appetizing. 415

Lovely Ladies 

Every woman must know how to be an efficient nurse, and this she can be if she will consider the patient's point of view. Affection and kindness are all very well in their place, but a sick person will much more appreciate quiet nursing and efficiency. 

Let your sick folk be cross and cantankerous up to a certain point-and then step on them. Try to bear a lot of it for the time being-sometimes no answer and just a knowing grin will recall a patient to his nicer self! 416

Chapter XI DIVORCE A PHILOSOPHER has written: 

"When a man starts to talk to me of 'unalterable laws' I begin to suspect that he is an unalter able idiot!" 

The philosopher's words serve us well as a text for considering the very serious, the very much abused, and the often misunderstood matter of divorce. 

Divorce is an ideal of civilized life just as marriage is. The fact that many ideals, and these two in particular, frequently are abused by people without the capacity to understand them in no way affects the intrinsic merit of the ideal. 

I want you to understand, at the beginning of this chapter, that divorce is neither a sad thing, nor a thing to be regretted. Mankind is prone to error. Therefore in his wisdom he has set up such remedial institutions as he can to correct his mistakes. The institution of divorce is one 417

Lovely Ladies of the most important of these. There are neither unalterable laws nor decisions which, however wise they may appear when made, may not turn out to be extremely unwise.

Of course, it may be regarded as unfortunate that two people, having carefully considered the matter and then entered into marriage expecting lifelong happiness, should find, after all, that they are unsuited to each other and cannot find happiness together. This, I say, may be unfortunate and sad. But surely there is nothing sad about the procedure which delivers them from the predicament -offers them new freedom and another chance to achieve happiness, either alone or with others more temperamentally suited to them.

Some Divorce 

Abuses

UNDERSTANDING what divorce is, and never forgetting for a moment that it is a right and proper measure, based upon sound ethics and ideals, I want you to consider with me some of the abuses to which divorce has given rise, and also the several circumstances in which it is proper to invoke divorce, and in which, in fact, it would be highly improper not to do so.

I have counseled you to go slow about marriage-to give your fiancé a fair chance to know the real you, and to study him pretty thoroughly and be sure you will be happy with him before you go to the altar. You should proceed just twice as slowly in the matter of divorce. Before marriage you may be pardoned for considering your own 418

Divorce happiness of paramount importance and making it a first consideration. But after marriage you must not do that. After marriage you have a definite obligation to your husband, as he has to you. It is up to you to do everything you possibly can to insure his happiness, as you would your own, and his obligation to you is similar. You are, you see, a joint personality. Presumably, when you are happy he will be happy, and vice versa. 

It is necessary, therefore, upon approaching divorce, for you to summon all of your intelligence. You see you have no right to jeopardize your husband's happiness until you are sure that it is absolutely necessary to do this in order to save your own life and his from ultimate wreck on the ugly reef of an impossible union doggedly adhered to. 

You must ask yourself, first of all, if you have failed in your obligation to your husband or to the institution of marriage. If you have not failed, then you must ask yourself if he has failed. Perhaps your answer to this will be affirmative. What then? Is that sufficient reason for divorce? Not at all! If your husband has failed in his obligation to you, it still is your duty to try to show him where he has failed. Try to reason with him and point out his error to him. And if he is half the man you thought he was when you married him he will try to make up for his shortcoming. Then there may be no necessity for divorce. 

IN THE chapters immediately following we will consider several other angles of the divorce question. Here I am primarily concerned with disabusing your mind of any 419

Lovely Ladies false notions about the "rightness" or "wrongness" of divorce. Then I want to say a few words about what you Sometimes

Divorce Creeps up on You should do after you've decided that divorce is necessary.

You must be fair to yourself and fair to your husband in analyzing your marital situation if you fear for the safety of your union. It frequently happens that circumstances more or less beyond your control or his will cause you both to drift into a situ ation where divorce is the only logical remedy. 

To take a case in point. I know of a young couple who have been married about four years. They are on the brink of divorce. The reason is not an unusual one. The husband, because of business, finds it necessary to spend a good bit of time away from home. His contacts are pleasant and enlightening. During the last four years, because of the business friendships he has made, and because of the demands of his business, he has progressed intellectually at a very rapid gait. 

On the other hand, the wife, although a charming girl and a very willing person, has not had the advantage of these contacts. She has not kept pace with her husband, even knowing, as all intelligent women know, that it is necessary-ever so necessary-to do so. Suddenly, therefore, they have both awakened to the fact that they are almost strangers. They have different philosophies, different outlooks on life; they move in different circles, and they have different interests.

All this has come about since their marriage, and we 420

Divorce 

may, of course, say that they were unwise to let it happen. 

That is so, but frequently things like that happen almost imperceptibly, and it takes a very keen intellect indeed to detect this drifting apart. 

However, realizing their differences, they are facing divorce. It is the logical step. Perhaps he is to blame; perhaps she is to blame; or perhaps they are both to blame. 

What does it matter?

The Objective Point of View 

THE important thing about this particular case is that the husband, realizing the situation before his wife did, was man enough to act immediately. He told her that they must be divorced, and he explained why. At first she could not understand. She was shocked and very much hurt. The situation was unpleasant for both of them. But the husband was tactful, tender, and firm. He kept trying to make matters clear to her, and finally she realized the truth of the situation.

The result is that they will be divorced, but they will always be friends-really good friends. There is no rancor, no suggestion of deceit or infidelity. Theirs was an honest marriage, and theirs will be an honest and worthwhile divorce. 

Don't Be a

Moral Coward 

I WANT you to take this lesson to heart. I do not advocate divorce where it can possibly be avoided. Do everything you can to reconcile the situation and to restore your marriage to its original blissfulness. But if you see that a divorce is 421

Lovely Ladies necessary, do not be a moral coward. Look the matter squarely in the face. Realize that, when it is necessary, divorce is a boon and under no circumstance an admission of defeat. 

Then set about getting your divorce with as much consideration for your husband as it is possible for you to show. 

Be a good clean sport in the business and you will have nothing to regret. If your husband mentions divorce to you first, don't let yourself be hysterical. If he is a real man he won't mention divorce to you until he's pretty thoroughly convinced that it is necessary. And if he is that convinced, then the thing to do is to talk the matter over calmly and earnestly. He may be right, and he may convince you that he is. And if he is not right, then it's up to you to show him where he's wrong.

The Other Side of the Picture THEN there is the other angle to the same situation-that of a woman mentally and spiritually progressing beyond her husband. This happens less often, it is true, but nevertheless it does happen. Men sometimes let themselves be entangled in a petty round of business, poker or bridge, golf over the week-end, while the woman for any of a number of reasons may be strengthening herself spiritually, and intellectually progressing beyond his comprehension. 

If this happens to you, neither pity nor habit should permit you to deny the fact nor make you feel a compulsion to stay with such a husband. Children? What can they gain by contact with a father whom they cannot but sooner or later leave far behind on the road of intelligence? 422

Divorce 

Young things have not tolerance and understanding, and this cannot but result in tortures for the parent. Honoring thy father and thy mother merely because Nature designed that they be thy father and thy mother, regardless of whether or not they deserve your respect as persons, is an outworn injunction. Modern parents have long realized the fallacy of demanding respect and honor. 

Above all, use your head. Don't get panicky, don't try to blame your husband until you're quite sure that you are not the guilty party, and then, if you really need a divorce to straighten out the tangle, be independent enough and good sport enough to see the business through with the best grace possible. 423

Chapter XII THE OTHER WOMAN 

हु 

THE "other woman" is not a disease. She is a symptom-the warning, to be recognized and understood. 

Lack of understanding and perspective again contributes to the downfall of womankind. 

The average wife, suspecting that there is "another woman" in her husband's life, promptly gives way to an attack of hysteria, a fainting fit, moodiness, or some other sign of outraged sensibilities. Immediately there is a little lesson in cause and effect. It goes something like this: 

Cause: Other woman-Effect: Hysteria in the home. 

Result: Unpleasant scene. 

New cause: Unpleasant scene-Effect: Husband con vinced that, after all, other woman really is more desirable than wife. 424

The Other Woman 

Given the chance, the average wife proceeds immediately to scare her husband right into the protecting arms of his new flame. With a single flood of tears she wipes away her own charm, sets her husband's nerves on edge, makes the situation seem important when it really isn't, and sacrifices her own tactical advantage as a wife. This latter is an advantage, and a very important one, too. 

For men, by nature and despite contentions to the contrary, are very conventional animals-far more so than women. 

Here's a Hypo thetical Case 

IN THE first place, did you ever stop to think why your husband might become interested in another woman? There's no point in setting yourself up as the repository for all of the fourteen million-odd virtues which men have managed to invent for us women. Presumably you haven't got all of them, and maybe there's just one of them which your husband has found particularly fascinating in the little widow across the road. Let's say, for instance, that she makes delightful breakfast toast, and your husband,

Henry, having breakfasted there a few times, becomes enamored of the toast. 

Of course Henry would splutter and fuss a good bit if you told him he was in love with a plate of buttered toast, so you won't tell him. Instead, having accurately perceived the situation, you'll forego a few matinées and go into a huddle with your toaster in the interest of more and better toast. 425

Lovely Ladies 

Then, some miserable morning when it's snowing or raining, and Henry has decided that the office can limp along without him and that he'll just laze around the house all day, you'll be facing each other over the breakfast table. Suddenly you'll hear an exclamation from 

Henry, and you'll look up to hear something like this: "By Jove, Martha, but this is ex-cellent toast! Do you think you could scrape up a few more pieces right quick?” 'Certainly, dear," you reply. Then, while Henry is contentedly munching away, you inquire, very casually, "By the way, how is that widow across the road-Mrs. 

Perkins, isn't it?"

The chances are ten to one that Henry will look at you over his glasses and his newspaper, with a rather vacant stare, and ask, "What widow?" "Mrs. Perkins," you insist. 

He thinks a moment. Then: "Oh, yes, yes—Mrs. Perkins. M-m-m-m, why, I haven't seen her for a month or so. Saw her walking out the other evening with that architect who just moved in down at the end of the row. Why?" “Oh, nothing—I was just wondering. My goodness! 

Have you finished all that toast? Here-I'll fix you some more." 99 That's the end of the triangle and of the terrible "other woman." The fact of the matter is that men are prone to these little eccentricities. Part of your job is to rescue 

Henry when he gets emotionally wobbly. 

Now do not mistake my meaning. By treating the matter 426

The Other Woman lightly I do not mean to minimize its importance. It is important. Marriage after marriage goes on the rocks because of these little affairs. And sometimes Henry falls in love with something a little harder to cultivate than a mastery of buttered toast. But the fundamental usually is the same. In nine cases out of ten he is not in love with any other woman. He just thinks he is, and it is up to you to show him that he is not. But you mustn't be obvious about it, and above all you mustn't let him suspect that you are taking the matter seriously. That's the first mistake that most wives make, and usually it's fatal. Investing a thing with tragedy where there is no tragedy, or admitting tragedy when its denial might neutralize it, cannot have anything but tragic results.

Study the 

"Other

Woman" 

WHEN Henry starts to make eyes at another woman, sit right down and analyze the situation. Ask yourself if you are just as charming and beautiful and lovely as you ever were. Ask yourself if you are just as interesting as you were. If you find that you are just as beautiful and just as charming and just as interesting-then find out what the "other woman" has that you have not. 

When you have found that out acquire that extra virtue if you can, and if you cannot, then devise some adequate substitute. 

The clever woman will not fail in this. She will lay a careful plan, and she will stick to it. She will give Henry lots and lots of rope. But it will be soft, silky, scented rope, 427

Lovely Ladies and sooner or later Henry will manage-metaphoricallyto strangle the "other woman" with his rope and get so entangled himself that he'll come back to have his bruises healed.

We've been considering average cases-nine cases out of ten. There is a tenth case, and about it you can do little save be a good sport. It is inevitable that of the thousands and thousands of men and women who marry some of them should, after the ceremony, meet people who are temperamentally more suited to them than their lawful mates. Refusing to recognize a fact does not change it. 

So we must recognize this fact for what it is, even though not exaggerating it.

If this should happen to you-if your husband should really fall in love with another woman-there is only one thing for you to do. Give him up to her. Of course you must first be sure that their love is genuine, and if your husband is a reasonable and intelligent person you may easily ascertain that.

Remember that gallant ladies seldom lose a man's attention, and if they do lose his love they do not lose what is even more important, his admiration. 

The rule works both ways. If you should fall in love with another man and his love grew to mean more to you than your husband's and your home, it would be only fair for you to expect your husband to give you your freedom. It is a chance which you both take when you marry. It is a mental reservation which you should have when you say, "I do"; and although you should both fight 428

The Other Woman to avoid this circumstance, should it develop you can do nothing but bow to the inevitable.

Make a Plan to Win Back

Your Hus band's Love 

SO FAR I may have seemed to you to be treating the matter of the "other woman" with a little flippancy. Actually I have not, for the "other woman" is really no problem at all, and a triangle is no situation when handled with intelligence and tact. 

Suppose that your love for your husband is still very important to you, that you do love and want and need him, and that you are confronted with that nightmare of all feminine imagination-another woman. 

You are hurt, dreadfully hurt. But if you analyze that hurt and admit to yourself at least that it is your pride that hurts you, this knowledge will give you courage, because you know that in reattracting your husband's affection you will at the same time reinstate your faith in your own self.

Commence by analyzing the situation, making a plan, and then sticking to it. True, it is quite possible that your plan may not be successful, but it is sure to be unsuccessful if you change horses in midstream, as it were. 

First, you must not take the situation too seriously. That is largely the reason why such situations are so seldom handled successfully-they are taken too seriously. And then you must realize that you are attempting to do something that may not succeed, that you are taking a sporting chance to get something back that you want, and that if 429

Lovely Ladies you win it's all right-but if you lose it's all right, too. 

You are not going to take your husband's wandering attention too seriously. But you are going to see whether or not it has wandered through any fault of your own. 

The biggest mistake that women ever make is to allow the painful shock of the knowledge of such a situation to bring out all of their most unpleasant qualities, as well as a number of newly acquired ones that seem to fit the exigencies of the moment. Any intelligent reasoning about this cannot fail to show you the disadvantageous position in which you place yourself, and the fact that the inevitable result can only be that of throwing the man directly into the arms of the woman from whom you are trying to detach him, as it were, and causing him to make, subconsciously and perhaps even consciously, comparisons between you two that cannot possibly react in your favor. 

Be Sure the "Game Is 

Worth the

Candle" OF COURSE, it is hard to be your very best and sweetest self when you feel that your heart is being hourly torn right out of you, but what worth-while thing isn't difficult?

When it becomes too difficult in proportion to the results you expect to obtain, then stop it and become philosophical about your defeat. 

And don't, for heaven's sake, suddenly become very moral and tell yourself that you refuse to resort to "trickery" and "if he doesn't want you as you are, that you don't want him," et cetera. Because, if that is true, you won't be agonizing over it—and if it is not true it's silly 430

The Other Woman to try to fool yourself into thinking it is. Being your loveliest self is never trickery. 

If you refuse to take the situation seriously, never once let him see that you are hurt, or worried, or alarmed, or that you in any way consider your position in his life endangered, he himself can't get very serious about it or make any radical decisions. 

Treating the matter lightly for the time being, and being your most charming and lovely self, will create an "armor invincible"--something for which he will some day thank you, some day when his infatuation is past.

And if you adopt this attitude, as he loses interest in her, his interest and admiration for you will increase. In the long run you will be more permanently installed in his affections than ever, due to his admiration for your attitude throughout what he himself will by that time be considering-his mental aberration. 431

Chapter XIII LOSING YOUR HUSBAND 

SO FAR we have gone on the assumption that you are married to a man whom you love deeply, and are therefore willing and anxious to take all of the measures that intelligence suggests to you in order to recapture his love, which you feel that you are losing.

There is one thing that you must realize that the modern woman knows how to love more profoundly than women ever before knew how to love, because her love is a result of her intelligence. And with intelligence guiding and analyzing her emotions, a modern woman can make a 432

Losing Your Husband far better wife and mother than the average woman of the past generation, who was a rather primitive creature of impulse, at best. 

Don't Think 

You Must

Love a Man 

WHEN a modern woman loves, whether this be for a week or a lifetime, she indulges in all of the feminine subtleties that women have perfected since Eve first decked herself with flowers and the plumage of birds; since Cleopatra rolled out of a priceless rug at Cæsar's feet, since Sheba confounded Solomon with her wisdom and tarried to enamor him with her charms. 

Just as long as you are sure that your husband means more to you than anything in life, school your emotions and give him lots of understanding. But do not feel obliged to simulate. Your marriage is your own business. A divorce is likewise your business. Do not consult the butcher, the baker, the candlestick maker, or even your best friendretire behind the kindly doors of solitude and consult your own intelligence and judgment. It will point the way invariably.

But there is something else to consider, too. Before taking all of these steps, are you really losing ground in your husband's affections? Be very sure that you know what you are about; be very sure that hurt pride or jealousy or suspicion or any one of the other hundreds of sly little devils that are ever waiting to insinuate themselves between two happy people are not causing your imagination to run away with you 433

Lovely Ladies 

Though your husband may be temporarily infatuated with another woman, who holds an entirely different interest for him than you have, this does not necessarily mean that he has lost interest in you.

Still, if he has lost interest in you, don't go wildly searching for something or someone to which he has presumably pinned his wavering affection, for he may not yet have found that something or someone, the while he may have lost considerable interest in you.

Where There Is Love 

BUT look to yourself and question the reasons for his indifference. No really lovely and interesting woman has ever lost the love of a man who has once truly loved her. 

First, you must understand that a cooling of the romantic ardor that he may have shown you during the months or years before your marriage does not necessarily mean that he is losing interest in you or falling out of love. Lovers and love affairs can be kept at this ecstatic pitch, for these may be dropped at the first or tenth shadow that falls over the face of the moon. But husbands and marriage cannot. "You don't love me any more, boo-hoo!" is a fine, stupid way to infuriate a man. Nor will a proud woman stoop to ask this question of her husband, bringing up, with the very asking, a doubt of something that one should never doubt; and at the same time bringing into sound and hearing a question that can implant itself in his mind and trouble him with the necessity of silent denial-or affirmation. 434

Losing Your Husband 

Where there has ever been love there is always love. Its manifestations change, that is all. Where there has never been love, there is no love, and there should be no regret. 

Which is easier to say than to believe and practise, I know. 

But remember, one can regret that one has never had something one desired, but no one with a sense of humor can regret the loss or losing of something that one never had!

The Beauty of 

Quietness

MANIFESTATIONS of love must necessarily change. It is physically impossible to endure for too long the emotional pitch that is reached in the first ecstasy of love. Be content with the beauty and romance which you have had, which you should have made absolutely perfect of its kind, and yourself relax and appreciate the beauties of quieter and much fuller love based on growing understanding and companionship. 

Therefore, be quite sure that what you might think is indifference really is indifference before you try to remedy it! Familiarity 

THINK of this. Should a cherished and onetime exquisite possession change of itself, Jose the lovely contours that once delighted you, its jewels fall and never be replaced, its perfume evaporate and perhaps turn rancid in the going, then could that which you loved and cherished, that which you had every reason to believe was a thing of enduring worth and beauty, longer exist, save in memory? And memory craves solitude 435

Lovely Ladies for the exercise of its chimeras, a turning away from the unlovely actuality into the beautiful and gone. 

Are you changing in the eyes of your beloved? This is not the age of miracles. Men, and women too, see penetratively. Superficial beauty can capture but not hold for long. Real beauty and loveliness that depend upon a great deal more than outward prettiness can hold the imagination forever.

Do not try to use as an excuse the time-honored and hackneyed "familiarity breeds contempt." Familiarity does not breed contempt-with loveliness and intelligence, wisely used. It does breed accustomedness, as you yourself very well know. The most beautiful, long-desired, and seemingly unattainable art object in the world, the most exquisite surroundings in which you never dreamed you could live, become familiar to you once you secure them and live with them. You never cease to glory quietly in them, in their beauty and loveliness, which become dearer to you with every moment of possession. They become familiar, but never commonplace. 

Are You Losing Ground?

FAMILIARITY With you and your loveliness will never cause your husband to turn from you for any length of time. But if this loveliness is wearing away with the years, if Time's fingers are touching your soul not lightly, then you are no longer the lovely woman that he once loved. And that being so, how can you expect or hope to keep his affection? What right have you to demand it? 43€

Losing Your Husband 

Do not try to use age as an excuse to yourself. The passing of the years will never lose you any real love that you have ever had. A love based merely upon your youthful prettiness and immaturity was never love. But a love that is sincere, loves the real you that should be developing and becoming more beautiful with these passing years. 

If you are losing a real love, then you are not becoming more beautiful with the passing years. Outward loveliness, as you certainly know, is but the manifestation of inner qualities. And inner qualities blossom with maturity. 

In All of Us 

MARRIAGES have no right to be based on anything but love and understanding. If your marriage is based on less, you are not losing something, you are merely facing the inevitable drifting apart of two personalities that never had anything to forge them together in the first place. It is in no wise a defeat, so why consider it so? 

The only real defeat, if you are losing the man you love, provided his love was real in the first place, is the defeat of your own intelligence and loveliness. Look to yourself.

It may not be too late consciously to repair the damage within yourself.

The rest will follow of its own accord. 437

Chapter XIV 

CUPID'S CAPRICES 

Now just a word or two about an important situation that the best-intentioned married woman in the world might, and very often does, encounter. It has its parallel with the "other woman"-and it is indeed the "other man." 

Every married woman since the world began has found herself either in or perilously near to such a situation. 

And every modern woman has encountered the problem of a lover or lovers. If she is unmarried the problem is likely to be more amusing than serious, but if she is married-ah, that is a different story. 438

Cupid's Caprices 

You cannot, in fairness to yourself and your husband, accept a lover while you are married. Your instincts should tell you that, and even the most superficial reasoning will explain why. Let us consider the matter from the safest the material-angle, leaving questions of morals and ethics to the individual.

We modern women have learned that marriage is a profession-a very serious and sublime business. One does not enter into marriage lightly or undertake it with a heavy heart. One goes at it joyfully, with a heart brimming with hope, and the determination to mold for one's self a beautiful and bright future.

Perhaps you work several years for this ideal-you and your husband. Together you intrench yourself against life's disappointments and prepare to enjoy its greatest blessings. 

Then you meet an other man-perhaps casually—and your heart and your spirit leap. 

But think a minute. Is it fair to your husband for you to risk his happiness and your own for an uncertainty? Surely it is not. Is it good business? You have worked, perhaps sacrificed, to make your marriage a successful and beautiful thing. Isn't it rather silly to throw the whole thing over for something which you cannot possibly know anything about until it is too late? 

Be Careful of

Your Values 

IF YOU would choose the path of wisdom you must be careful of your values in life. 

The attentions of a comparatively unknown man, no matter how desirable he may appear, 439

Lovely Ladies hardly can balance in the scales against the constant affection of a husband who has learned to know you and who loves you. 

Do you think I've forgotten something? I haven't. I know I told you that if your husband really fell in love with another woman and loved her more than he did you it was your duty to give him up. You probably are wondering if I am going to counsel you to make this sacrifice without suggesting that you yourself might find a truly greater love than that which you have for your husband.

No, I am not going to do that. There have been women, and there always will be women, who, although comparatively happily married, meet a man with whom they can be more ideally happy. 

If that happens to you, go to the other man, and God bless you! Only be sure. Be careful. Be fair. Do everything that you possibly can to avoid it. A change of scenery or the adopting of a hobby will tide you over a possible bad place. You should instinctively realize that you owe it to your husband, your children, your marriage, to avoid other entanglements. 

But assuming that a sincere love has come into your life again, or perhaps even for the first time, despite the fact that you thought you had a sufficiency of love upon which to base your marriage, it is entirely possible that you do not actually owe as much to your husband and family as the denial of this love would entail. 440

Cupid's Caprices 

Be Honest with Yourself and Your Husband 

You cannot go on living with your husband, truly loving another man. Therefore, having done everything that you can do to avoid the situation, there is nothing left to do but to admit it and alter your life with as little pain as possible for everyone concerned. If you have fallen in love, go to your husband and tell him. Reason with him and try to arouse his sense of fairness, if it is lagging. If he is a modern man and a real man, he will offer to let you go.

But be sure that it is not you yourself this time who is merely infatuated. The desire for change, for thrills, the restlessness in all of us that ever urges us to seek and look and want, so often tricks us by disguising itself as any one of the worth-while emotions that have become a legitimate part of our lives.

The sophisticated woman knows the difference between love and love affairs. The latter may be quite harmlessstimulating and amusing.

But such affairs can be so harmful and dangerous that the wise woman will avoid them entirely. This is one of the little sacrifices that she makes on the altar of marriage, realizing what she is doing and appreciating the other things that she is getting in exchange. 

What if your unmarried sisters fall in and out of love with thrilling regularity and unhappiness? Remember that they do not have many, many of the other things that you do have. 44I

Chapter XV ALIMONY-A BADGE OF SLAVERY WE COME to a very important factor of divorce-alimony. Alimony, in nine cases out of ten, is one of the blackest marks against a supposedly emancipated womanhood. It is a badge of slavery, degradation, and moral debauch understandable only as one of the most deplorable blemishes of a woefully materialistic age. Occasionally, I admit, alimony is justified. When it is not justified it is a disgrace for which every decent modern woman must blush. 

What is the purpose of alimony? Is it some kind of extra bonus which you are supposed to receive for allowing a man to support you for a period of years, or months?

Is it a division of spoils, legalized for the benefit of thousands of female pirates sans decency and honor? Is it a public fund to which every married man must subscribe according to his means and from which every 442

Alimony-A Badge of Slavery woman may draw means and comforts according to her astuteness?

Alimony is none of these things, although from its abuses it would seem to be all of them and more too. In fine, it merely is an allowance to be made to a woman out of her husband's estate upon separation, for good and sufficient reason.

I am not going to discuss legal aspects of alimony. I want you to be interested only in ascertaining when alimony is justified and when it should be eschewed as if it were a plague. 

Why Alimony?

ALIMONY being an allowance made the wife from the estate of her husband, there must logically be some reason for it. What better reason can there be than that it is an attempt to balance the scales of justice? Presumably all penalties prescribed or allowable under civil law have basically the same purpose -to make up to an innocent party, at the expense of a guilty party, any losses which the first party has incurred through the guilt of the second.

If alimony were kept within these bounds, with allowance for exceptional cases, it would be a good and beneficent institution. But suppose, for a moment, that you and your husband, after five years of married life, have decided that you would be happier separated. Suppose, even, that you have fallen in love with someone else and seek your freedom, or that he has fallen in love with another woman and wants his. Why should you 443

Lovely Ladies seek alimony, unless through some definite service you assisted your husband in the creation of your joint estate, or unless there are other and mitigating circumstances? 

Do not misunderstand me. I don't mean that, upon separation, you should be turned out into the cold world without a penny, and perhaps without much opportunity of immediately gaining a livelihood. If your husband has any conception of decency he will and should make adequate provision for you. But if, for instance, he is a salaried man, without extensive estate, and he is guilty of no more heinous offense than falling in love with another woman, or, as is far more often the case, of merely falling out of love with you, or of having you fall out of love with him, why should he be saddled for the rest of his life with a weekly or monthly obligation which will eat up the greater portion of his earnings, kill his ambition and initiative, and force him to go without those things to which he is entitled? How could you justify yourself in being a party to such an arrangement, and how could you bear to sacrifice your self-respect and independence to a point where you would accept it?

THE situation is hardly altered if your husband happens to be wealthy. The courts of America seem to act on the principle that alimony should be allowed in proportion to the husband's estate and income rather than in accord with the actual needs of the wife. This practice, it seems to me, is one of the most important factors 444

Alimony-A Badge of Slavery contributing to the many abuses which have grown up about alimony.

Wealth Makes No Funda mental Differ ence 

As a modern, intelligent, and independent woman, and a lovely lady, don't you feel a bit ashamed for your weaker sisters when you see thousands of female parasites so lacking in sportsmanship that they accept, years after marriage, regular incomes from their husbands who were, for no better reason than that they once thought they loved the men?

A woman who accepts alimony which is not justified can have no more self-respect than a chorus girl who deliberately tricks a wealthy suitor into a compromising situation or statement and then, through clever and frequently crooked lawyers, proceeds to milk the victim of as many dollars as possible by alleging that he has "broken her heart" or some equally ridiculous thing.

Children and 

Alimony 

IF A married woman has children, and no estate of her own, it is right and proper that her husband should provide for the support and education of the children until they are of age, thereby fulfilling the silent promise that a man makes when he marries and consents to the bringing of new lives into the world. If the woman has an adequate estate of her own there is of course no reason why she should not share this expense and obligation. After all, they are her children as much as they are his, and her obligation to them in all directions is just as great as his or greater. 445

Lovely Ladies 

If, as I said, you leave marriage and face a period of uncertainty, your husband should, in fairness, provide for you. I have in mind those cases where marriage and perhaps even childbearing have robbed a woman of some measure of health, or otherwise affected her ability to take an independent place in the community. 

Alimony and

Your Cares ALIMONY may be justified in another direction. If, at the request of your future husband, you abandoned a really promising career-let us suppose in one of the arts—and then after a period of marriage you both agree to separate and it is impossible for you immediately to return to this career, your husband should duly consider that fact and, if possible, make adequate material allowance. But don't delude yourself and try to delude him into thinking that he did take you from a promising career if the actual truth is that he took you from an uninteresting, monotonous round of office work, from which you were very thankful indeed to be taken.

This reminds me of a very funny cartoon I once saw, in which a typically stupid and silly woman in tears was saying to her cigar-chewing and furious husband: "Boo-oo-0-0-0-0, and I gave you the best years of my life!" "Yeah-h-h, and who made them the best years of your life, I'd like to know!" bellows the infuriated male.

When alimony has aught of the stuff of fairness about 446

Alimony A Badge of Slavery it one may accept it without sacrificing one's dignity. 

But when it is unjustified and needless it then becomes a far different thing, and no woman can afford to stifle her conscience and sell her self-respect for a few of any man's dollars! 447

Chapter XVI OTHER DIVORCE PROBLEMS 

WE HAVE been considering so far the various factors that lead up to a decision to rectify an unhappy marriage by divorce. 

Actually, when human beings have reached a certain superior mental plane, there will be no divorce. Intelligence demands engagements, periods of time devoted to learning in every way to understand the man with whom you plan to build your life, have children, and build their lives. Sportsmanship and understanding, either real or assumed (assumed understanding can be kindly tolerance of things that you do not understand), and for the rest just the art of being a woman-these will insure you against losing any of your husband's admiration and affection. 448

Other Divorce Problems 

But to-day we still need divorce, since human nature struggles so blindly to rectify its mistakes. "Incompati- bility" BESIDES the situations that we have discussed there is another described by a word we so often use now in relation to divorce, and it is "incompatibility." Its meaning is rather obscure. It is, however, a clash between two temperaments -a possibility you should have taken into account before you married. But you didn't, and a year or a few years have elapsed, and you and your husband find that you simply cannot live together. Each of you has too decided a temperament, and those decided temperaments do not fit together. It is impossible for either of you to be subject to the other without losing so much of your personality that life would be unbearable. 

Be Very Slow to Fix Blame on Your Husband 

IF YOU have tried in every possible way to hold your marriage together, and if have exercised every you are sure that you ounce of your self-control and courage and understanding and you are at last convinced that it is no longer possible for you and your husband to live together, there is only one answer, and that is divorce. But in this event there is no blame on either side-and indeed there seldom is anyway in divorce. 

We are all too fond of trying to lay this miserable thing called blame on someone, when actually there is nothing 449

Lovely Ladies or no one to blame for the series of emotions, coincidences, and chemical reactions that cause things to happen. 

AND then there is one more justifiable 

Cruelty cause for this serious step of divorce. 

The courts call it "cruelty," and usually prefix it with "mental." If you should suffer in this way through the man you have married you must know just how long to be tolerant, and just when to refuse firmly to allow him to wreck your life further. 

Tolerance and understanding are one thing, and you can largely help a man to be the man that you believed him to be when you married him, but you must do so firmly. Tears, hysterics, threats that you will leave him are all petty artifices that have no point whatsoever. Tell him quietly and firmly, and make him realize that you mean it, that there are certain things he must change if he expects you to continue with your part of the bargain.

Then, after giving him every opportunity, if he continues unworthy, you must of course cease living with him.

Consider Your Children ESPECIALLY when there are children must you for their sake cease living with him. If there is absolutely no one concerned but yourself, and you love this man as we women have an odd, perverted way of doing, despite all of his cruelty, then you simply weigh your love against your happiness and decide whether your love compensates for the unhappiness. 450

Other Divorce Problems 

It is up to you to give your very best to marriage and everything that it entails. But have strength of character to bring it to a close if any of the circumstances I have suggested to you warrant it.

When a woman has looked into her heart and can truthfully say that she has done her very, very best to make her marriage a permanent success, and yet it has failed, then she should resort to divorce with a courageous heart and a tranquil mind. 451

BOOK IV BEING the consideration of other aspects of a lovely lady's life, and some of the conditions she may encounter, all to the end that she may grow in grace and loveliness always, wherever she may be, whatever may befall.

Chapter I SEWING AN ARISTOCRATIC PASTIME STRANGE and beautiful tapes tries have ever grown beneath the skillful fingers of princesses and ladies of rank, for sewing is an aristocratic pastime. 

Delicate and beautiful were the hands of Guinevere, and many were the poems and songs that they, expressing all of the queenly grace of a long and noble line, inspired in the knights of old. 

But these beautiful hands were not forever making gracious gestures of welcome and hospitality. For ever and anon, more frequently than not, indeed, the clarion note of war rang through the land, and Arthur with his noble knights rode forth to meet the foe. And while Arthur and 

Tristram, and Lancelot and Galahad smote the heathen amain until their shields rang for very joy in the battle, Guinevere remained at home. 

Did Guinevere remain idle awaiting the return of her 455

Lovely Ladies heroes? Never! Those delicate hands ever moved above the embroidery frame, now cleaving the air with the unerring skill of darting swallows, now fluttering listlessly like white butterflies over a field of clover. 

Sewing has always been an aristocratic pastime. Only wealthy women had time for it. Princesses who later became queens spent many hours over delicate laces and beautiful embroidery, some of which have been preserved and can be seen to-day in museums. 

It is only the unthinking modernist who scoffs at knowing how to sew. So afraid is she that the world might think of her as an artisan! The truly well-bred woman has nothing to fear-the world could not possibly think of her as other than what she is.

The Sewer's 

"Tool Box"

NO WORKMAN can be expected to turn out successful work without the proper tools. A well-equipped "tool box" is the first requisite to first-class results in home sewing. It should contain tailor's chalk and tracing wheel for marking notches, small rules, two or three pairs of sharp shears in assorted sizes, razor blades for ripping machine stitching, steel pins and pincushion, needles of assorted sizes, a couple of thimbles (to save tempers when you lose one!) tape measure, spool cotton, and silk.

THE mode is more difficult at one time for the home dressmaker than it is at others. Yet the subtle flares, tiers, drapes, and trains that have a way of springing up on us are not 456

Sewing-An Aristocratic Pastime really as intricate as they seem. When you stop to analyze them they lose all of their formidability, because after all there are only a certain number of things that can be done with the squares, trian- 

Simpler Than

It Looks materials. gles, and circles which can be cut out of The home dressmaker must learn that the difference between a "home-made" model and one that is purchased from an expensive couturier is in the finishing rather than the cut or fit.

Picoting is the simplest form of finishing, and should be used on all raw edges of lighter weight material. Other materials that do not ravel can be simply finished by cutting a tiny notched edge. What is called a "rolled hem" is very graceful also, and is certainly easy to handle on biased edges. And when you work with lace or sheer materials you will find it much simpler, and the finished product very much more satisfactory, if you have all of the raw edges picoted, whether they are going to show or not.

Neat bindings are essential to any good-looking garment, and if these happen to be the bane of your life, as they are of mine, or if you cannot with a little practice turn a neat hem or put in a faultless sleeve, either practise on cheaper material until you can do so, or take this much of your garment to a good dressmaker or pleating establishment and have it done for you. It is really a shame to spoil nice material by trying to save the few dollars that would be charged for this. 

But at that there is really no reason why you cannot 457

Lovely Ladies learn to make a binding. Always be very careful that the strip of material that you cut for this binding is a piece of bias material, then baste one edge of it to the edge of the piece you are binding, sew it down on the machine, and iron it before you finish sewing the other side by hand. 

Never forget to iron as you go. This will make your work ever so much easier to handle and the finished product ever so much nicer.

Thread is very important. Never try to economize here, because it is such a petty economy in the first place, and in the long run no economy at all. You may use cheap cotton thread of sufficient heaviness for your bastings, but for all other work use the best possible quality thread. 

Pile fabrics, which means any fabric with a nap, should always be sewed with spool silk, because it makes the smooth, flat seams that are important in heavy materials. 

And right here I want to stress the importance of basting. There is no surer way to spoil materials as well as tempers than to be hasty about this! Good

Trimming 

ONE of the cardinal sins of the home dressmaker is that of indulging in cheap trimming. To buy, for instance, a really good and expensive buckle is wisdom, because it will not only enhance the garment for which it is intended, but you can use it forevermore on other garments and on hats.

The same thing is true of the materials one buys by the yard. Buy something that you know is sturdy as well as artistic, and you may be sure it will trim many a gown. 458

Sewing An Aristocratic Pastime 

Fringing 

FRINGE sways its graceful way in and out of the mode. Any woman can make it with just a little patience and some friend to help wind the thread. One winds thread on a piece of cardboard the size the fringe is to be, then irons this entire piece of cardboard with the thread wound on it under a damp cloth. You cut off pieces of fringe as you go, threading two strands, at a time into a large-eyed needle. A second person can thread needles. I would suggest that you look carefully at some hand fringing somewhere, then experiment a bit, and you will find that, with just a little practice, you can fringe many a beautiful shawl, scarf, and dress.

Materials 

OFTEN you find that you can use the width

The Way of of the material around you-and if so it will save you a great deal. But in using materials like velvet, or those with a print or design in them, you must be very careful to see that the nap goes the proper way, which means that one has to be more careful than clever in cutting it. Flat crêpes and some woollen materials, which look the same no matter which way the light strikes them, lend themselves to very clever cutting.

So when you are selecting materials be very careful to look for this.

THE little lady with a mathematical twist is the most successful home dressmaker. One need not stand for hours before a mirror trying to make a garment on oneself, which is not only tiresome but usually disastrous. Simply 459

Lovely Ladies take your measurements, then measure your material accordingly and cut it. 

A Little 

Mathematics 

Helps 

For instance, if you measure fifteen inches from shoulder to shoulder across your back, the middle of your back will be seven and one-half inches in, and you will fold your piece of material from which you are cutting the back into a double piece seven and one-half inches wide. 

About Patterns 

ABOUT patterns, I cannot say. Either you are accustomed to using a pattern or you are not. Personally I prefer not to. Of course, each home dressmaker should have two or three basic patterns of her own that she cuts out of newspaper, and every new garment she makes can be built on one or the other of these. 

Everything should first be made of unbleached muslin, which is ever so cheap, and which you can fit on yourself easily before you begin to cut the more expensive material. 

What Are 

"Made- overs"? 

THE word is a bugaboo. Nothing is really "made over" from anything else. One should never look at a frock and see how that particular frock could be made overone should just rip it all to pieces, send the pieces to the laundry or wash and iron them yourself, and then design and make a new dress from the pieces. There may be new materials needed to combine with them. A made-over dress can be either just that, or a new dress-depending upon how you handle it. 460

Sewing-An Aristocratic Pastime 

There are infinite possibilities in this method of making over garments—the adding of a clever tuck here, of a chic touch there, the cunning frocks that can be made for children. Every clever woman uses good materials until they actually wear out. 

Making Hats at Home 

THE home milliner has a complicated problem, for it takes more skill to make a smart hat than anything else. The secret is to iron as you go, and to select very simple models and not try to depend upon a lot of trimming to cover up poor workmanship. The small soft fabric hats are quite easy to make if you remember to build up from a simple foundation.

Résumé 

SEWING is a delightful feminine occupation. 

In all countries and in all ages women's lovely hands have fluttered over tapestries, laces fragile as cobwebs pearled with dew, cloths of gold that stood of their own stiffness, altar cloths writhing with gilded serpents and jeweled passion flowers-and homespun garments that served many a rugged pioneer. 

All the "secrets" of sewing are simply explained away by intelligence. Know yourself, the colors and lines that are becoming to you. Select as expensive materials as possible, particularly if you are going to put a lot of hand work on something, and see that your garment is properly fitted and properly finished.

This last sentence reminds me of those little shops that are nothing short of a boon to the home dressmaker, where 461

Lovely Ladies you can have more buttonholes made for ten cents than you could make in an hour; lovely pleating and shirrings and embroidery and beading that you never could do for yourself. All you do is ask them how much material you will need for the thing that you want; they will tell you, and you will get it, and the garment is almost made for you. 

Sounds simple, doesn't it? It is, once you understand the necessary rules: careful finishing, wise planning and execution, and particular attention to details. 462

Chapter II ART IN CLOTHING 

FIND me a woman who is not interested in clothing, particularly her clothing. I don't think you can, even in the backwaters of civilization. But if you do, hold on to her, for she is worth money-as a freak. Of course there are many badly dressed women, but can you think of one who prefers to be dressed badly? 

I hardly think so.

In this chapter I'm going to talk very generally about the theory of dress, about art in clothing, how to express yourself (for every art is merely self-expression) in the clothing you wear, and in other parts of the book you will find specific suggestions as to clothes that will fit your particular type, the lines that you should wear, and the wise and economic purchasing of your wardrobe.

In civilized life clothing is and must be of primary 463

Lovely Ladies importance in every woman's life. Beautiful and proper clothing, proper, that is, as considered by the age in which it is worn, has a tremendous effect not only upon those who see it but upon those who wear it. 

The days of mad scrambling from one style to another, when women tolerated freakish clothing that had no regard to beauty and harmony, have passed. Those grotesque styles were a product of the restlessness of women and their need of activity which was suppressed in every other line.

With the increasing freedom allowed women for selfexpression and mental development, clothing is assuming an entirely different aspect, and this in spite of strong efforts on the part of those who are making their living in the so-called style industry. Paris alone supports more than three hundred dressmaking establishments that are worthy to be called the haute couture! The fact that the style industry is the third largest in the world shows every thinking woman to what extent the activities of the textile makers, garment designers and makers, fashion magazines, and fashion writers trade upon the feminine imagination.

A Necessary 

Art 

NOR is it advisable to seek to deny this carefully cultivated art. On the contrary, the time has certainly come when we must refine this instinct so that it actually does help to express the beauty and loveliness which are the prerogatives of every modern woman, not just of the favored few. Modern women are learning to enhance their own personal beauty, 464

Art in Clothing realizing that beauty is no longer considered a matter of regular features and doll-like stares; learning to express whatever distinguishing characteristics they have that are not exactly like those of every other woman. And by the very effort of this expression women are developing personality and individuality on a scale never before dreamed of. 

I HEAR you ask, "What about the cropped 

The Magazine and marcelled, cigarette-smoking and

Type cocktail-drinking, expensively perfumed and artificially pearled individual who is heralded in the fashion magazines as the typical modern élégante?” 

I admit it. This type is heralded as the typical modern élégante. It is the prevailing type at the moment. But in this rapidly moving age this does not mean that it is the type of the next moment. And one must remember that the fashion writers and artists who are responsible for these depictions are supported by the fashion magazines—and they all cater to the majority.

Everywhere one sees the rise of personal individuality in clothing. And "a little child shall lead them”—at internationally famous places of elegance the world over the outstanding figures and individuals are the débutantes!

Their mothers may continue to abbreviate skirts and tresses, but not so the young élégante. She realizes that the day of fashion and gesture molds for women is gone. 

DRESSING to your individuality and thereby making yourself a personality is not as complicated as it sounds. But it 465

Lovely Ladies does take intelligence.

And intelligence is the forte of the modern woman. 

A Necessary 

Knowledge Nor does one commence out of nothing. 

One has a definite basis upon which to work, and that is personal coloring, cast of features, and general temperament. The three are usually related.

The colors of one's clothing are selected to harmonize with and enhance personal coloring; colors are carefully avoided that are detrimental to or neutralize that coloring.

Types of garments, or "silhouettes" as the fashion magazines so often say, are chosen to conform to one's general temperament as indicated by characteristic attitude. One's figure is taken into consideration, and materials are draped in a way to enhance and balance its lines. Thus figure defects are minimized and even eliminated altogether.

Of course, it all sounds very vague and technical to the average unreflective mind, and that is exactly the reason why the attractively-clothed and truly lovely modern woman is so obviously a product of intelligence. Nowadays a woman does not dismiss her ignorance in clothing on the grounds that she has no so-called "good taste." This supposedly mysterious talent readily develops in the light of intelligence.

A LIMITED income has long been an excuse for failure to express beauty in one's clothing. But, as is so often the case, 466

Art in Clothing this is merely a convenient reason for not indulging in the necessary mental effort. It is admittedly true that it is 

For the

Limited Income easier to express elegance in our clothing when we have the means to do so, but it is equally certain that it is not necessary to spend a great deal of money in order to be suitably dressed.
