Nail file and any other manicure preparations you ordinarily use

Deodorant 

Rouge, lipstick, and any other aids to beauty that you use 

Little first-aid kit 

Your own face soap, in a soap box 

Small scissors 

Hygienic necessities.

That is all that is absolutely necessary, except a little box holding the gauze sacs containing oatmeal, almond meal, and orris root about which I told you in the chapter on Beauty; you will remember I stressed the importance of your never being without them.

But there are other little things that a lovely lady must take if her voyage is to be of any duration, things which she can also take on her short voyage if she has the room in her dressing case for them.

One is a small box containing enough dry shampoo for one or two shampoos. This dry shampoo can be orris root, or cornmeal, or a mixture of both, or any dry shamthat you prefer. With this can go a tiny bottle of hair poo 513

Lovely Ladies tonic, and a wee bottle of olive oil. Now your razor with new blades, or depilatory if you use that, and a bottle of some sort of alcoholic rub that will be very refreshing on a warm, messy day. And a tiny bottle of mild cathartic pills. Also a tiny box of aspirin or other headache tablets. 

And cosmetic or rubber gloves.

Your cleansing tissues are especially important. The towels that you can procure on trains and boats are very nice towels-for polishing your shoes-but not for a lovely lady's delicate skin! And your cosmetic gloves are important, because on trains the soot and cinder will grind into your hands while you sleep unless they are covered, and while they are covered they will be automatically removing the grime of the day. This applies to boat travel as well as train travel, only in the case of the latter it will be soot and dirt and in the other it will be dryness from salt air, or chapping or sunburn. 

So, there is your dressing case, complete! Can't you see how you really have to devote a separate receptacle to all of these things? Not only so that they are convenient to get to, but in order to keep them from possibly spilling on your clothes. If you have more room in your dressing case you might put into it your dressing robe, night robe, and dressing slippers, but this only if you are taking a short voyage.

I like to carry one whole suitcase in which this dressing case is contained, but in which I can also put such odd, bulky things as books, camera, shoes, rubbers, raincoat, short umbrella, and anything else that I am likely to want 514

The Lovely Lady Travels to get at during the day, and to find which I should not have to scramble through all of those intimate little things that are packed in the larger suitcase with my clothes. 

You see, the case doesn't make an extra piece of luggage, and you can lift the dressing case out to carry it with you to the dressing room; yet at the same time it keeps all the odd, bulky things together in one place. Personally, I hate to have even the smartest raincoat in the world come in contact with my dainty frocks and lingerie. And, oh, yes, in this large suitcase, too, you carry your laundry bag. In it you can put everything that might soil your nice things. 

Other General Things 

FOR a voyage that requires traveling for any length of time you must be prepared to encounter more varieties of weather than you ever encounter in the year around at home. Say that your trip is from New York in June for 

Europe on a tour that includes France, Italy, and Switzerland. You are going to find both very cold weather and tropical weather. Therefore, every lovely lady will have an extra sweater, an extra-warm woollen scarf or two, and a couple of suits of quite heavy underwear. Don't laugh. It is one thing when it is below zero at home to be all wrapped up in your furs and probably living in a steam-heated house, and another to encounter belowzero weather on a European train. And you can't well take your fur coat with you, you see. 515

Lovely Ladies 

Travel books and so-called "guides" for the inexperienced traveler always assure one that films and most everything else necessary are findable almost anywhere in the world. Which is a bit exaggerated, like Mark 

Twain's death, because he wasn't dead and they aren't findable. 

Naturally, you should not clutter up your baggage with too many things if you can possibly avoid it, but packing, as I have just told you, in this suitcase which contains your dressing case, you can take all of those things. And I'll promise you that there isn't one of them that you won't be able to use. There is one thing, writing paper, which I cannot imagine one being unable to get in any part of the world, however. 

Note that "soap in a soap box" on the list. I can hear you say that surely soap can be bought in any part of the world. Perhaps so-but what soap and what prices! Which reminds me of your wash cloth, which you had better take, despite the fact that it is a cold, damp thing to handle. 

The only country in the world with sufficient sense of the subtleties to furnish its hotel guests with wash cloths is 

America! And do not forget sun glasses, very important things for boat traveling in the tropics or sunny snowcovered slopes.

That about covers all of the general travel suggestions. 

You must remember that it is uncomfortable and irritating to yourself and to everyone around you to be unprepared in any way while traveling. With just a little intelligence and careful planning, you won't be. 516

The Lovely Lady Travels 

Train 

Journeys 

OF COURSE, all these things do not apply alike to boat, train, and airplane travel, although the general suggestions do. And much depends upon the duration of your trip. 

On a train you will have to take all necessary things in your hand luggage. One can manage very nicely with two pieces of hand baggage for the longest train journey in the world, because if such a journey extends over a week there are usually stop-overs where it is possible to have laundry done. If you fill one suitcase as I have suggested with your dressing case and all the other bulky things, and then fill your second suitcase with your clothing, stockings, lingerie, etc., what more do you want?

For train travel you must remember the necessity of going back and forth from your berth compartment to the dressing room. Therefore, your dressing robe is of importance, and cannot, to be in good taste, be of the flimsy kimono or négligé variety. A dressing robe you must have, and that's all there is to it. Some very experienced travelers will tell you that you can use your raincoat, which you must take, as a dressing robe. Of course you could, but what lovely lady wants to?

Dining Elegance 

I NEVER could understand why it is that etiquette demands that one dress for dinner on a boat but does not demand dressing for dinner on a train. By "dressing for dinner” I do not mean wearing a dinner dress or a party frock, of course, but I do mean wearing a slightly nicer dress, and 517

Lovely Ladies certainly not the same dress in which you have been traveling all day. Just see what a change of shoes and frock will do to you and how much you and everyone who sees you will appreciate it! After one or two meals on a train in the usual cafeteria-like atmosphere you will be glad to insist upon dining in a more leisurely fashion. 

NIGHT train travel is particularly hard on 

Extra Care a lovely lady's hair, complexion, and nerves. Therefore, instead of neglecting your beauty routine for that length of time, which unfortunately usually happens, double it. At least on a train you cannot have the excuse of no time for beauty care. Don't, above all things, neglect your eye wash three times a day, the lavish use of your hand lotion, and the wearing of your cosmetic gloves at night. Also the use of your hair tonic to counteract as much as possible the unaccustomed exposure to dust and climatic changes.

Boat Trips 

BOAT trips differ from train trips in that you can either take a small wardrobe trunk into your stateroom or you may have access to your larger trunk in the baggage room or hold, especially if the trip is of some duration. Also one dresses for dinner and dancing almost every night, which necessitates some planning for your dinner frocks, wraps, and accessories. 

But the suggestions for your traveling case do not change.

If you are taking a boat voyage and you are certain that you can have your small wardrobe trunk in your state- 518

The Lovely Lady Travels room, this one suitcase, packed as suggested, and your trunk may be enough. If not, it stays packed just as it is anyway, and then you can have one more suitcase for clothing that requires flat packing and your trunk as well. 

On almost any boat trip you will encounter a variety of weather, and you must therefore have extra sweaters, jacquettes, and scarfs for warmth. 

Of course, on the boats that pass through the tropics do not forget to plan for warm weather in your selection of clothing, and particularly shoes. Remember your bathing suit and cap and dressing robe suitable for passing from your stateroom to the swimming pool, a promenade which is usually lined with people who have nothing better to do than to watch their fellow travelers. 

ON ANY boat trip you can always coax

Laundry or bribe the stewardess to do some laundry for you, if the boat has no other facilities. But be extremely wary of having your laundry done by outside laundries under the so-called auspices of the boat, when the boat is either stopping over or passing from port to port, unless the company guarantees against loss and damage, which it usually does not very emphatically, and issues a printed statement to that effect. 

Mardi Gras 

Ан, THE time-honored fancy-dress ball that used to attend every sea voyage and occasionally still does! If you are uncertain of its taking place, have the makings of a Spanish costume in the offing, 519

Lovely Ladies with which you can use your Spanish shawl which, Mardi Gras or no Mardi Gras, will always serve as an evening wrap. And don't ever permit anyone to coerce you into togging yourself out in a comedy costume, if you are not the comedy type! You won't look, act, or feel comfortable, and your whole evening will be spoiled. 

Checking Excess 

It is not customary to dress for dinner the first night or the last night on a boat. 

Other nights, yes. Therefore, if you are going on any kind of a tour where skeleton baggage (the very minimum) is an absolute necessity, after the longer trip on a boat, you may pack your evening clothes and other things only necessary for the boat in one trunk or suitcase that can be either shipped when you land at the first port or checked until your return. 

One usually forgets or does not think of many things which later one finds would have added greatly to the comfort and the interest of the voyage. On your second trip over the same route you know these things very well, but the voyage itself has lost that never-to-be-recaptured novelty of "one's first trip."

Therefore, it is the wise and lovely lady who after reading all of the suggestions in this chapter remembers to act upon them when planning a journey-and let me assure you, you will never regret doing so. I remember my first voyage and the mistakes I made. If only there had been a lovely lady's book to tell me things!

And now that you have been voyaging for so long a time, 520

The Lovely Lady Travels in bon fashion, I hope, through this chapter, I shall conclude with an amusing but very practical letter that a friend of mine wrote to a little friend of hers who had never crossed the ocean, telling in no uncertain and picturesque language just what she might do to make her trip a practical and pleasant one: 

DIONNE DEAREST: 

Paris-on-the Seine, France. 

For heaven's sake, apply for your passport at once, even if you are not sailing for two months! I suppose that the good old government that flourishes 'neath the Stars and Stripes has its reasons for doing all of the incomprehensible things that it does do about passports, but I've never been able to figure out what they were-and some of them are darned aggravating and take a lot of time. The chances are that your advent into this world, among other things, was never properly registered, and there will be all the fun of looking that up, and if you can't find it, of tracking down some veracious personage whom you can coerce into swearing with pen and ink that you really were born, where and what for. And get all the "vee-sahs" that you will need unless you are going to have plenty of time in which to get them at your first port.

Carrying luggage on the Continent is rather expensive. And for the same reason don't get any more clothes than just what need for your voyage as, at the present writing, they are so much less expensive here. you

Bring, however, a very good supply of stockings, if you are going to stay any length of time, and better wear them all once unless you want to pay duty on them.

Now listen, ducky, I know how much you appreciate your morning coffee, and the kind of cigarettes that you are used to, so you had better bring a can of one and a carton of the 521

Lovely Ladies other-because you are certainly going to have to change your tastes in them-and the wrench is awful. You can taper off with what you do bring, and it won't be so bad. Open both of these things, of course, for the same reasons that you wore all your stockings. 

As soon as you get on the boat and have found your stateroom and seen that all of your hand luggage is in it, make a bee line for the dining room and arrange for your table. And get the second sitting, for which you may have to sacrifice a little food, but will gain in interesting companionship. 

Then go and arrange for your deck chair, or, if you prefer, arrange this first, or else those awful persons known in the guide books under the title of "experienced travelers" will probably beat you to it and grab up all of the desirable spots. Don't get a chair near a fog horn, or near a smoke funnel.

Or near the dining room. Or on the sunny side of the boat when you are going through the tropics. Or on the windy side of the boat when you are crossing the Atlantic. Or in an unsheltered place under any circumstances. And rent yourself a steamer rug. 

And then, after these things are done, arrange with the bathroom steward for your bath. And the best time, and for some reason the least popular time, is just before dinner. You will always want to bathe before you dress for dinner. (Incidentally you will probably be pretty sleepy in the mornings at the usual bath hour!) Which reminds me, too, to tell you not to bother 522

The Lovely Lady Travels with breakfast at all, but arrange with the stewardess to bring you coffee and fruit in the morning at whatever time you think you are likely to awaken. 

Don't forget your bathing suit and bathing slippers, or your fancy-dress costume. And when I say "don't forget them," I mean don't forget to put them in your hand luggage, not in your trunk, unless you're sure you can have the trunk in your cabin.

Under no circumstances tip anybody until you are about to land, except on the very long voyages. Some people do, but you are not supposed to, especially being a good-looking little lady traveling alone. Then tip your bath steward, your waiter, your bedroom stewardess, and your deck steward. Don't tip your dining-room steward unless he has given you some special attention, but if all the fuss and bother has fallen upon the shoulders of your table steward (waiter), then increase his tip a little. Also, if you have for any reason (what are the wild waves saying?) had most of your meals in your room, don't tip your dining-room steward or your waiter very much, but tip your stewardess, who has waited on you in your room, rather generously. There will be various other attendants, who will make you think that they deserve a tip, but they don't, unless they have given you some very special service not in their regular line of work. But the deck steward, for instance, who may or may not be extra nice to you, gets a tip for good conduct, if he displays any-because his so-called "regular services,' for which the steamship company pays him, end at about the time he has rented you your deck chair and steamer rug. Please try to realize that it is just as vulgar to overtip as it is unpleasant not to tip enough, but you are the person to figure the size of the tip according to the service you get, not the person who receives it. 

Now do make out your declaration slip for the customs officers at every port very carefully, and don't try to get away with anything on your first voyage. Maybe you can and maybe 523

Lovely Ladies you can't, but why bother when they allow you such a lot of things duty free anyway? As for clothes and things like that, just wear 'em a little (even if you have to sleep in a few dresses the night before you get in). In particular, wear all of your shoes and stockings once.

If on any boat for any good reason you are not pleased with your stateroom, or your stateroom companions, or your stewardess, arrange with the purser to change your stateroom the second or third day out, not the first. This can usually be arranged if you insist. 

And don't forget the mails. I don't mean the sex, because they will probably be cluttering up your entire voyage in a way that will preclude the possibility of your forgetting them. I mean the good old mail that you can dismiss with a postage stamp and know that it won't come back. I used to leave a wide trail of souvenirs behind me all over the world, until I found that it was easier to mail them home, to someone whom I had already instructed to take care of them for me, than to have to leave them en route because of lack of space. 

So there you are. I can't meet you at Le Havre, but it will be no trick at all for you to come into Paris on the boat train, as the station will be teeming with American Expressers who will show you everything, everywhere, and every place. And oh, dear, dear, by that time you will have a sufficient number of escorts who will do all of these things for you anyway. I will meet you at the Gare St. Lazare, and we will jointly struggle with the customs officers. 

Bon voyage, and much love!

ZABETTA. 

The young lady in question, of course, was making two boat trips, one from San Francisco to New York, via the 

Panama Canal, and the other from New York to Paris, hence the suggestions that apply to the tropics. 524

LOVELY LADIES 

AND now you and I come to the end of the path that a lovely lady must tread toward freedom and happiness, beauty and loveliness.

We have discussed the abstract, spiritual qualities that every lovely lady should possess.

We have gained the simple knowledge it is necessary to have, and the rules to follow in order to have and to hold beauty in all of her physical expressions. 

We have discussed the sociological aspects of the modern woman's life, and I have tried to point out to you ways to acquire a safe, sane point of view based entirely upon intelligence and reasonable thinking. 

This is a textbook of loveliness. Use it as such, and as such permit your daughters to use it. Times and customs may change, but the need for feminine beauty and loveliness, never. Using this textbook as a basis, you and your daughters can build upon it your own ideas of living, changing them with the fluid times.

Study, practise, and understand the contents of this book, and you will be a truly lovely lady!

THE END
